As IPMs only improve an initial (non-optimal) solution, we also have to discuss how to construct such an initial solution. This is done in Appendix A.1. Further, the IPMs only yield a fractional almost optimal and almost feasible solution, which is why in Appendix A.3 we prove how to convert the solution to a truly feasible solution. Rounding this fractional almost optimal feasible solution to a truly optimal integral solution is done in Appendix A.4.

In Section 8 we combine all these tools and data structures to obtain algorithms for minimum weight perfect bipartite matching, where Section 8.4 obtains an  $\widetilde{O}(n\sqrt{m})$ -time algorithm via the  $\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{m})$  iteration IPM and Section 8.5 obtains an  $\widetilde{O}(m + n^{1.5})$ -time algorithm via the faster  $\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{n})$  iteration IPM. At last, we show in Section 8.6 how this result can be extended to problems such as uncapacitated minimum cost flows (i.e. transshipment) and shortest paths.

# 4 IPM

Throughout this section we let  $\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$  denote a non-degenerate matrix, let  $b \in \mathbb{R}^n$  and  $c \in \mathbb{R}^m$ , and consider the problem of solving the following linear program  $(P)$  and its dual  $(D)$

$$
(P) \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \min  _ {x \in \mathbb {R} _ {\geq 0} ^ {m}: \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x = b} c ^ {\top} x \quad \text {a n d} \quad (D) \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \max  _ {y \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n}: \mathbf {A} y \leq c} b ^ {\top} y. \tag {15}
$$

First, in Section 4.1 we provide our general IPM framework for solving (15). Then, in Section 4.2 we show how to instantiate this framework with the logarithmic barrier function and thereby enable  $\widetilde{O}(n\sqrt{m})$  time minimum-cost perfect matching algorithms. Next, in Section 4.3 we show how to instantiate this framework with the Lee-Sidford barrier [LS14] as used in [BLSS20] and obtain an improved  $\widetilde{O}(m + n^{1.5})$ -time minimum-cost perfect matching algorithm. The analysis in both Section 4.2 and Section 4.3 hinge on technical properties of a potential function deferred to Section 4.4. Finally, additional properties of our IPM needed to obtain our final runtimes are given in Section 4.5.

# 4.1 Linear Programming Meta-algorithm

Here we present our core routine for solving (15). This procedure, Algorithm 1, is a general meta algorithm for progressing along the central path in IPMs. This general routine maintains primal-dual, approximately feasible points induced by a weight function  $\tau(x,s)$  defined as follows.

Definition 4.1 ( $\epsilon$ -Centered Point). For fixed weight function  $\tau: \mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m} \times \mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m} \to \mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}$ , and  $\gamma \in [0,1)$  we call  $(x,s,\mu) \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m} \times \mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m} \times \mathbb{R}_{>0}$ ,  $\epsilon$ -centered if  $\epsilon \in [0,1/80)$  and the following hold

- (Approximate Centrality)  $w \approx_{\epsilon} \tau(x, s)$  where  $w \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} w(x, s, \mu) \in \mathbb{R}^m$  and  $[w(x, s, \mu)]_i \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \frac{x_i s_i}{\mu}$ .  
(Dual Feasibility) There exists  $y \in \mathbb{R}^n$  such that  $\mathbf{A}y + s = c$ .  
- (Approximate Primal Feasibility)  $\| \mathbf{A}^{\top}x - b\|_{(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{S}^{-1}\mathbf{A})^{-1}}\leq \epsilon \gamma \sqrt{\mu}$ .

Under mild assumptions on the weight function  $\tau$ , the set of 0-centered  $(x,s,\mu)$  form a continuous curve, known as the central path, from a center of the polytope (at  $\mu = \infty$ ) to the solution to the linear program (at  $\mu = 0$ ). This path changes depending on the weight function and in Section 4.2 and Section 4.3 we analyze two different paths, the one induced by the standard logarithmic barrier and the one induced by the LS-barrier [LS19] respectively. The former is simpler and yields  $\widetilde{O}(n\sqrt{m})$  time matching algorithms whereas the second is slightly more complex and yields a  $\widetilde{O}(m + n^{1.5})$  time matching algorithm.

Here, we provide a unifying meta algorithm for leveraging both of these paths. In particular, we provide Algorithm 1 which given centered points for one value of  $\mu$ , computes centered points for a target value of  $\mu$ . Since we provide different algorithms depending on the choice of weight function, we present our algorithm in a general form depending on how a certain SHORTSTEP procedure is implemented. Algorithm 1 specifies how to use SHORTSTEP and then we implement this procedure in Section 4.2 for the logarithmic barrier and Section 4.3 for the LS-barrier.

To analyze and understand this method we measure the quality of  $(x,s,\mu)\in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}\times \mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}\times \mathbb{R}_{>0}$  by a potential function we call the centrality potential.

Definition 4.2 (Centrality Potential). For all  $(x,s,\mu) \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m} \times \mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m} \times \mathbb{R}_{>0}$  we define the centrality potential as follows

$$
\Phi (x, s, \mu) \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \Phi (w (x, s, \mu)) \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \sum_ {i \in [ n ]} \phi \left(\frac {[ w (x , s , \mu) ] _ {i}}{[ \tau (x , s) ] _ {i}}\right)
$$

where for all  $v \in \mathbb{R}^m$  we let  $\phi(v)_i \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \phi(v_i) \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \exp(\lambda(v_i - 1)) + \exp(-\lambda(v_i - 1))$  for all  $i \in [n]$  and  $\lambda \geq 1$  is a parameter we chose later. Further, we let  $\phi'(w), \phi''(w) \in \mathbb{R}^n$  be the vectors with  $[\phi'(w)]_i \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \phi'(w_i)$  and  $[\phi''(w)]_i \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \phi''(w_i)$  for all  $i \in [n]$ .

This centrality potential is the same as the one used in several recent IPM advances, it was key to the IPM advances in [LS19], was used extensively in [CLS19, LSZ19, Bra20, BLSS20], and most recently was used in the IPM of [JSWZ20]. Correspondingly, the analysis of this meta-algorithm is primarily a clean abstraction for these papers and properties of the potential function are deferred to Section 4.4. The novelty in our IPM is the attainment of efficient implementations of SHORTSTEP in Section 4.2 and Section 4.3.

In the remainder of this section we define a valid SHORTSTEP procedure (Definition 4.4), provide PATHFOLLOWING (Algorithm 1) which uses it, and analyze this algorithm (Lemma 4.4).

Algorithm 1: Path Following Meta Algorithm  
1 procedure PATHFOLLOWING  $(x^{(\mathrm{init})}\in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m},s^{(\mathrm{init})}\in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m},\mu^{(\mathrm{init})} > 0,\mu^{(\mathrm{end})} > 0)$   
2 /* Assume SHORTSTEP is a  $(\epsilon, r, \lambda, \gamma, \tau)$ -short step procedure  
3 /* Assume  $x^{(\mathrm{init})} s^{(\mathrm{init})} \approx_{\epsilon/2} \mu^{(\mathrm{init})} \cdot \tau(x^{(\mathrm{init})}, s^{(\mathrm{init})})$   
4  $x \gets x^{(\mathrm{init})}, s \gets s^{(\mathrm{init})}, \mu \gets \mu^{(\mathrm{init})}$   
5 while  $\mu \neq \mu^{(\mathrm{end})}$  do  
6  $x^{(0)} \gets x, s^{(0)} \gets s, \mu^{(0)} \gets \mu$   
7 for  $i = 1, 2, \dots, \frac{\epsilon}{r}$  do  
8  $\mu^{(\mathrm{new})} \gets \mathrm{median}(\mu^{(\mathrm{end})}, (1 - r)\mu, (1 + r)\mu)$   
9  $(x, s) \gets \mathrm{SHORTSTEP}(x, s, \mu, \mu^{(\mathrm{new})})$   
10  $\mu \gets \mu^{(\mathrm{new})}$   
11 Compute  $p_1 \approx_{1/3} \Phi(x, s, \mu), p_2 \approx_{1/3} \|A^\top x - b\|_{(A^\top X S^{-1} A)^{-1}}$ .  
12 if  $p_1 > e^{-1/3} \exp\left(\frac{3\lambda\epsilon}{4}\right)$  or  $p_2 > \epsilon \gamma e^{-1/3} \sqrt{\mu}$  then break;  
13 if  $p_1 > e^{-1/3} \exp\left(\frac{\lambda\epsilon}{4}\right)$  or  $p_2 > \epsilon \gamma e^{-1/3} \sqrt{\mu}$  then  
14  $x \gets x^{(0)}, s \gets s^{(0)}, \mu \gets \mu^{(0)}$   
15 return  $(x, s)$

Definition 4.3 (Short Step Procedure). We call a SHORTSTEP  $(x,s,\mu ,\mu^{\mathrm{(new)}})$  a  $(\epsilon ,r,\lambda ,\gamma ,\tau)$ -short step procedure if  $\epsilon \in (0,1 / 80)$ ,  $r\in (0,1 / 2)$ ,  $\lambda \geq 12\epsilon^{-1}\log (16m / r)$ ,  $\tau :\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}\times \mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}\to \mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}$ , and  $\gamma \in [0,1)$  and given input  $x,s\in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}$  and  $\mu ,\mu^{(\mathrm{new})}\in \mathbb{R}_{>0}$  such that  $(x,s,\mu)$  is  $\epsilon$ -centered and  $|\mu^{(\mathrm{new})} - \mu |\leq r\cdot \mu$  the procedure outputs random  $x^{(\mathrm{new})}$ ,  $s^{(\mathrm{new})}\in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}$  such that

1.  $\mathbf{A}y^{(\mathrm{new})} + s^{(\mathrm{new})} = c$  for some  $y^{(\mathrm{new})}\in \mathbb{R}^n$  
2.  $\mathbb{E}[\Phi (x^{(\mathrm{new})},s^{(\mathrm{new})},\mu^{(\mathrm{new})})]\leq (1 - \lambda r)\Phi (x,s,\mu) + \exp (\lambda \epsilon /6)$  (for  $\Phi$  defined by  $\tau$  and  $\lambda$ )  
3.  $\mathbb{P}\left[\| \mathbf{A}^\top x^{(\mathrm{new})} - b\|_{(\mathbf{A}^\top \mathbf{X}^{(\mathrm{new})}(\mathbf{S}^{(\mathrm{new})})^{-1}\mathbf{A})^{-1}}\leq (\epsilon /2)\gamma \sqrt{\mu^{(\mathrm{new})}}\right]\geq 1 - r.$

Lemma 4.4. Let  $x^{(\mathrm{init})}, s^{(\mathrm{init})} \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^m$  and  $\mu^{(\mathrm{init})}, \mu^{(\mathrm{end})} > 0$  satisfy  $\mathbf{A}y^{(\mathrm{init})} + s^{(\mathrm{init})} = c$  for some  $y^{(\mathrm{init})} \in \mathbb{R}^n$  and satisfy

$$
x ^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})} s ^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})} \approx_ {\epsilon / 2} \mu^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})} \cdot \tau (x ^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})}, s ^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})}) a n d \| \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x ^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})} - b \| _ {(\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X} ^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})} \mathbf {S} ^ {(\mathrm {i n i t}) - 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1}} \leq \epsilon \gamma \sqrt {\mu^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})}}.
$$

PATHFOLLOWING  $(x^{(\mathrm{init})}, s^{(\mathrm{init})}, \mu^{(\mathrm{init})}, \mu^{(\mathrm{end})})$  (Algorithm 1) outputs  $(x^{(\mathrm{end})}, s^{(\mathrm{end})})$  such that

$$
x ^ {(\mathrm {e n d})} s ^ {(\mathrm {e n d})} \approx_ {\epsilon / 3} \mu^ {(\mathrm {e n d})} \cdot \tau (x ^ {(\mathrm {e n d})}, s ^ {(\mathrm {e n d})}) a n d \| \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x ^ {(\mathrm {e n d})} - b \| _ {(\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X} ^ {(\mathrm {e n d})} \mathbf {S} ^ {(\mathrm {(e n d)} - 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1}} \leq \epsilon \gamma \sqrt {\mu^ {(\mathrm {(e n d)})}}
$$

in expected time  $O\left(\frac{1}{r}\mathcal{T}\right) \cdot |\log (\mu^{\mathrm{(end)}} / \mu^{\mathrm{(init)}})|$  where  $O(\mathcal{T})$  expected time to compute  $p_1, p_2$  such that  $p_1 \approx_{1/3} \Phi(x, s, \mu)$  and  $p_2 \approx_{1/3} \| \mathbf{A}^\top x - b \|_{(\mathbf{A}^\top \mathbf{X}\mathbf{S}^{-1}\mathbf{A})^{-1}}$  and to invoke SHORTSTEP once on average. Further, the  $(x, s, \mu)$  at each Line 7 and the termination of the algorithm are  $\epsilon$ -centered.

Proof. Note that the algorithm divides into phases of size  $\frac{\epsilon}{r}$  (Line 7 to Line 15). In each phase, if  $\Phi$  or  $\| \mathbf{A}^\top x - b\|_{(\mathbf{A}^\top \mathbf{X}\mathbf{S}^{-1}\mathbf{A})^{-1}}$  is too large, then Line 14 occurs and the phase is repeated. To bound the runtime, we need to show that Line 14 does not happen too often. Notationwise, we use  $x^{(i)}, s^{(i)}, \mu^{(i)}$  to denote the state of  $x, s, \mu$  at the end of the  $i$ -th iteration in a phase, and  $x^{(0)}, s^{(0)}, \mu^{(0)}$  denote the vectors at the beginning of a phase which are stored in case we need to revert to those vectors.

First, we claim that throughout the algorithm,  $\Phi$  and  $\| \mathbf{A}^{\top}x - b\|_{(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{S}^{-1}\mathbf{A})^{-1}}$  are small at the beginning of each phase, i.e. Line 7.

$$
\Phi (x ^ {(0)}, s ^ {(0)}, \mu^ {(0)}) \leq \frac {r}{8} \exp (\frac {3}{4} \lambda \epsilon) \mathrm {a n d} \| \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x ^ {(0)} - b \| _ {(\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X} ^ {(0)} \mathbf {S} ^ {(0) - 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1}} \leq \epsilon \gamma \sqrt {\mu^ {(0)}}.
$$

At the beginning of the algorithm, we have by assumption that

$$
x s \approx_ {\epsilon / 2} \mu \tau (x, s) \text {a n d} \| \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x - b \| _ {(\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1}} \leq \epsilon \gamma \sqrt {\mu}.
$$

Since  $\epsilon \in [0,1 / 8]$ , we have  $\left|\frac{x_i s_i}{\mu \cdot \tau(x,s)_i} - 1\right| \leq \frac{2}{3}\epsilon$ . Using  $\lambda \geq 12\epsilon^{-1} \log(16m / r)$  and Lemma 4.35 yields that

$$
\Phi (x, s, \mu) \leq 2 m \cdot \exp \left(\frac {2}{3} \lambda \epsilon\right) \leq 2 \left(\frac {r}{1 6} \exp \left(\frac {1}{1 2} \lambda \epsilon\right)\right) \cdot \exp \left(\frac {2}{3} \lambda \epsilon\right) \leq \frac {r}{8} \exp \left(\frac {3}{4} \lambda \epsilon\right).
$$

Further, when we replace  $(x^{(0)}, s^{(0)}, \mu^{(0)})$  (i.e., when the last phase is successful), we have  $p_1 \leq e^{-1/3} \exp \left(\frac{\lambda \epsilon}{4}\right)$  and  $p_2 \leq \epsilon \gamma e^{-1/3} \sqrt{\mu}$ . By the definition of  $p_1$  and  $p_2$ , and that  $(1/2) \leq \exp(-1/3)$  this implies the same desired bound on  $\Phi$  and  $\| \mathbf{A}^\top x - b \|_{(\mathbf{A}^\top \mathbf{X} \mathbf{S}^{-1} \mathbf{A})^{-1}}$  and the claim holds. Similarly, as long as the phase continues (i.e. the conditions at the end of each iteration hold), we have that for all iterations  $i$

$$
\Phi (x ^ {(i)}, s ^ {(i)}, \mu^ {(i)}) \leq \exp \left(\frac {3}{4} \lambda \epsilon\right) \text {a n d} \| \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x ^ {(i)} - b \| _ {(\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X} ^ {(i)} \mathbf {S} ^ {(i) - 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1}} \leq \epsilon \gamma \sqrt {\mu}.
$$

Note that  $\Phi(x, s, \mu) \leq \exp\left(\frac{3}{4} \lambda \epsilon\right)$  implies  $\left|\frac{x_j s_j}{\mu \cdot \tau(x, s)_j} - 1\right| \leq \frac{3}{4} \epsilon$  for all coordinates by Lemma 4.35. Using  $\epsilon \in [0, 1/8]$ , this implies that  $x^{(i)} s^{(i)} \approx_{\epsilon} \mu^{(i)} \tau(x^{(i)}, s^{(i)})$ . Combined with property 1 of a short step procedure, this implies that  $(x^{(i)}, s^{(i)}, \mu^{(i)})$  is  $\epsilon$ -centered for each iteration  $i$ .

Now, we show that Line 14 does not happen too often. Note that there are three reasons. Line 14 happens, one is that it exceeds the threshold for  $\| \mathbf{A}^\top x^{(i)} - b\|_{(\mathbf{A}^\top \mathbf{X}^{(i)}\mathbf{S}^{(i) - 1}\mathbf{A})^{-1}}$  at some iteration, one is that it exceeds the threshold for  $\Phi$  at some iteration, another is that it exceeds the threshold for  $\Phi$  at the end of the phase.

For  $\| \mathbf{A}^\top x^{(i)} - b\|_{(\mathbf{A}^\top \mathbf{X}^{(i)}\mathbf{S}^{(i) - 1}\mathbf{A})^{-1}}$  conditioned on the phase reaching iteration  $i$ , we can use the guarantee of SHORTSTEP to get

$$
\left\| \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x ^ {(i)} - b \right\| _ {\left(\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X} ^ {(i)} \mathbf {S} ^ {(i) - 1} \mathbf {A}\right) ^ {- 1}} \leq \frac {\epsilon \gamma}{2} \sqrt {\mu^ {(i)}}
$$

with probability  $1 - r$ , in which case  $p_2 \leq (\epsilon / 2)e^{1/3}\gamma \sqrt{\mu^{(i)}} \leq \epsilon e^{-1/3}\gamma \sqrt{\mu^{(i)}}$ . Thus, the probability that the phase fails at iteration  $i$  due to the check on  $p_2$  is at most  $r$ , and union bound over  $\epsilon / r$  iterations gives that with probability at least  $1 - \epsilon$ , the algorithm passes per iteration threshold on  $\| \mathbf{A}^\top x^{(i)} - b \|_{(\mathbf{A}^\top \mathbf{X}^{(i)} \mathbf{S}^{(i) - 1} \mathbf{A})^{-1}}$  for all iterations in a phase.

For  $\Phi$ : again conditioned on the phase reaching iteration  $i$ , the guarantee of SHORTSTEP yields

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} \left[ \Phi \left(x ^ {(i)}, s ^ {(i)}, \mu^ {(i)}\right) \right] \leq (1 - \lambda r) \Phi \left(x ^ {(i - 1)}, s ^ {(i - 1)}, \mu^ {(i - 1)}\right) + \exp \left(\frac {1}{6} \lambda \epsilon\right) \\ \leq (1 - \lambda r) \Phi (x ^ {(i - 1)}, s ^ {(i - 1)}, \mu^ {(i - 1)}) + \frac {r ^ {2}}{8} \exp (\frac {3}{4} \lambda \epsilon). \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last inequality is because of the  $\log (240m / \epsilon^2)$  term in the definition of  $\lambda$ . We know at the beginning of the phase,  $\Phi(x^{(0)}, s^{(0)}, \mu^{(0)}) \leq \frac{r}{8} \exp \left(\frac{3}{4} \lambda \epsilon\right)$  by the first claim, and knowing the phase reaches iteration  $i$  won't make  $\mathbb{E}[\Phi(x^{(k)}, s^{(k)}, \mu^{(k)})]$  increase for any  $k \in [0, i]$ . Together with the bound above, we know  $\mathbb{E}[\Phi(x^{(i)}, s^{(i)}, \mu^{(i)})] \leq \frac{r}{8} \exp \left(\frac{3}{4} \lambda \epsilon\right)$ . By Markov's inequality we have that  $\Phi(x^{(i)}, s^{(i)}, \mu^{(i)}) \leq \frac{1}{8} \exp \left(\frac{3}{4} \lambda \epsilon\right)$  with probability at least  $1 - r$ , and in this case  $p_1 \leq \frac{e^{1/3}}{8} \exp \left(\frac{3}{4} \lambda \epsilon\right) \leq e^{-1/3} \exp \left(\frac{3}{4} \lambda \epsilon\right)$ . Thus, the (a priori) probability that the algorithm fails the check at the end of iteration  $i$  is at most  $r$ , and union bound over the  $\epsilon/r$  iterations yields that with probability at least  $1 - \epsilon$  the check for  $\Phi$  is passed in each iteration of a given phase.

Furthermore, by expanding the guarantee of SHORTSTEP over the  $\epsilon /r$  iterations, we know at the end of the phase

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} \left[ \Phi \left(x ^ {\left(\epsilon / r\right)}, s ^ {\left(\epsilon / r\right)}, \mu^ {\left(\epsilon / r\right)}\right) \right] \leq (1 - \lambda r) ^ {\frac {\epsilon}{r}} \Phi \left(x ^ {(0)}, s ^ {(0)}, \mu^ {(0)}\right) + \frac {1 - \left(1 - \lambda r\right) ^ {\frac {\epsilon}{r}}}{\lambda r} \exp \left(\frac {1}{6} \lambda \epsilon\right) \\ \leq e ^ {- \lambda \epsilon} \frac {r}{8} \exp \left(\frac {3}{4} \lambda \epsilon\right) + \frac {1}{\lambda r} \exp \left(\frac {1}{6} \lambda \epsilon\right) \\ \leq \frac {1}{8} \exp \left(\frac {1}{4} \lambda \epsilon\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

where we used that  $\lambda \geq 12\epsilon^{-1}\log (16m / r)$ . With probability at least  $3 / 4$ ,  $\Phi (x,s,\mu)\leq \frac{1}{2}\exp (\frac{1}{4}\lambda \epsilon)$  at the end of the phase, and hence  $p_1\leq \frac{e^{1 / 3}}{2}\exp (\frac{1}{4}\lambda \epsilon)\leq e^{-1 / 3}\exp (\frac{1}{4}\lambda \epsilon)$ . Namely, it passes end of phase check for  $\Phi$ .

Union bounds over all three triggers, each phases success without reset with probability at least  $\frac{1}{4}$ . Hence, in expected  $O(1 / r)$  iterations,  $\mu$  increases or decreases by  $\Theta (1)$ . This yields the runtime  $O(\frac{1}{r}\mathcal{T})\cdot |\log (\mu^{(\mathrm{end})} / \mu^{\mathrm{(init)}})|$ .

Finally, we note that when the algorithm ends, we have  $p_1 \leq e^{-1/3} \exp \left( \frac{1}{4} \lambda \epsilon \right)$  which implies  $\Phi \leq \exp \left( \frac{1}{4} \lambda \epsilon \right)$  and  $x^{(\mathrm{end})} s^{(\mathrm{end})} \approx_{\epsilon/3} \mu^{(\mathrm{end})} \cdot \tau(x^{(\mathrm{end})}, s^{(\mathrm{end})})$ .

# 4.2 Short-step Method for Log Barrier Weights

In this section we provide and analyze a short step when the weight function is constant, i.e.  $\tau(x,s) \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \vec{1} \in \mathbb{R}^m$  for all  $x,s \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^m$ . The central path induced by this weight function is also the one induced by the logarithmic barrier function, i.e.  $(x_{\mu}, s_{\mu}, \mu)$  is 0-centered if and only if

$$
x _ {\mu} = \underset {x \in \mathbb {R} _ {\geq 0} ^ {m}: \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x = b} {\operatorname {a r g m i n}} c ^ {\top} x - \mu \sum_ {i \in [ m ]} \log (x _ {i}) \text {a n d} s _ {\mu} = \underset {s \in \mathbb {R} _ {\geq 0} ^ {m}, y \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n}: \mathbf {A} y + s = c} {\operatorname {a r g m a x}} b ^ {\top} y + \mu \sum_ {i \in [ m ]} \log (s _ {i}). \tag {16}
$$

Our algorithm using this weight function is given as Algorithm 2. The algorithm simply computes an approximate gradient of  $\Phi(x, s, \mu)$  for the input  $(x, s, \mu)$  and then takes a natural Newton step to decrease the value of this potential as  $\mu$  changes and make  $x$  feasible. The key novelty in our step over previous IPMs is that we sparsify the projection onto  $x$  by random sampling. We show that steps on  $x$  can be made  $\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{m} + n)$ -sparse, ignoring the effect of a known additive term, by carefully picking a sampling distribution. Further, we show that the amount of infeasibility this sampling induces can be controlled and  $\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{m})$  iterations of the method suffice. Ultimately, this leads to a  $\widetilde{O}(n\sqrt{m})$  time algorithm for matching.

Our main result of this section is the following lemma which shows that Algorithm 2 is a valid SHORTSTEP procedure for the meta-algorithm of Section 4.1. In this section we simply prove this lemma. Later, we show how to efficiently implement Algorithm 2 to obtain efficient linear programming and matching algorithms and improve using the Lee-Sidford barrier.

Lemma 4.5 (Log Barrier Short Step). Algorithm 2 is an  $(\epsilon, r, \lambda, \gamma, \tau)$  SHORTSTEP procedure (see Definition 4.3) for all  $\epsilon \in (0, 1/80)$  with weight function  $\tau(x, s) \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \vec{1} \in \mathbb{R}^m$  for all  $x, s \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^m$  and parameters  $\lambda \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} 36\epsilon^{-1}\log(240m/\epsilon^2)$ , let  $\gamma \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \epsilon/(100\lambda)$ , and  $r \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \epsilon\gamma/\sqrt{m}$ , i.e.

Algorithm 2: Short Step (Log Barrier Weight Function)  
1 procedure SHORTSTEP  $(x\in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m},s\in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m},\mu \in \mathbb{R}_{>0},\mu^{\mathrm{(new)}}\in \mathbb{R}_{>0})$    
2 Fix  $\tau (x,s)\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\vec{1}\in \mathbb{R}^m$  for all  $x\in \mathbb{R}_0^m,s\in \mathbb{R}_0^m$  in Definition 4.2 \*/   
Let  $\lambda \stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}36\epsilon^{-1}\log (240m / \epsilon^2)$ $\gamma \stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=} \epsilon /(100\lambda)$ $r\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=} \epsilon \gamma /\sqrt{m}$ $\epsilon \leq 1 / 80$  .   
Assume  $(x,s,\mu)$  is  $\epsilon$  -centered   
Assume  $\delta_{\mu}\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\mu^{\mathrm{(new)}} - \mu$  satisfies  $|\delta_{\mu}|\leq r\mu$    
Approximate the iterate and compute step direction   
Find  $\overline{x}$  and s such that  $\overline{x}\approx_{\epsilon}x$  and  $\overline{s}\approx_{\epsilon}s$    
Find  $\overline{v}\in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}$  such that  $\| \overline{v} -w\|_{\infty}\leq \gamma$  for  $w\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}w(x,s,\mu)$ $g\gets -\gamma \nabla \Phi (\overline{v}) / \| \nabla \Phi (\overline{v})\| _2$    
/\* Sample rows   
A def X-1/2S-1/2A, w def w(x,s,μ), X def Diag(x), S def Diag(s), W def Diag(w)   
Find H such that H ≈ A   
Let  $\delta_p\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\overline{\mathbf{W}}^{-1 / 2}\overline{\mathbf{A}}\overline{\mathbf{H}}^{-1}\overline{\mathbf{A}}^\top \overline{\mathbf{W}}^{1 / 2}g$  ,  $\delta_c\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=} \mu^{-1 / 2}\cdot \overline{\mathbf{W}}^{-1 / 2}\overline{\mathbf{A}}\overline{\mathbf{H}}^{-1}(\mathbf{A}^\top x - b)$  , and   
 $\delta_r\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=} \delta_p + \delta_c$    
Find  $q\in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n}$  such that  $q_{i}\geq \sqrt{m}\cdot ((\delta_{r})_{i}^{2} / \| \delta_{r}\|_{2}^{2} + 1 / m) + C\cdot \sigma_{i}(\overline{\mathbf{A}})\log (m / (\epsilon r))\gamma^{-2}$    
for some large enough constant  $C$    
Sample a diagonal matrix R E Rn where each Rii = min{qi,1}-1 independently with probability min{qi,1} and Rii = 0 otherwise   
/\* Compute projected step   
 $\delta_x\gets \overline{\mathbf{X}} (g - \mathbf{R}\delta_r)$ $\delta_s\gets \overline{\mathbf{S}}\delta_p$ $x^{(\mathrm{new})}\gets x + \delta_x$  and  $s^{(\mathrm{new})}\gets s + \delta_s$    
return (x(new),s(new))

1.  $\lambda \geq 12\epsilon^{-1}\log (16m / r)$  
2.  $\mathbf{A}y^{(\mathrm{new})} + s^{(\mathrm{new})} = c$  for some  $y^{(\mathrm{new})}\in \mathbb{R}^n$  
3.  $\mathbb{E}[\Phi (x^{(\mathrm{new})},s^{(\mathrm{new})},\mu^{(\mathrm{new})})]\leq (1 - \lambda r)\Phi (x,s,\mu) + \exp (\lambda \epsilon /6)$  
4.  $\mathbb{P}\left[\| \mathbf{A}^\top x^{(\mathrm{new})} - b\|_{(\mathbf{A}^\top \mathbf{X}^{(\mathrm{new})}(\mathbf{S}^{(\mathrm{new})})^{-1}\mathbf{A})^{-1}}\leq \frac{\epsilon}{2}\sqrt{\mu^{(\mathrm{new})}}\right]\geq 1 - r.$

We prove Lemma 4.5 at the end of this section in Section 4.2.3. There we prove claims 1 and 2, which follow fairly immediately by straightforward calculation. The bulk of this section is dedicated to proving claim 3, i.e. Lemma 4.11 in Section 4.2.1, which essentially amounts to proving that the given method is an  $\tilde{O} (\sqrt{m})$ -iteration IPM. This proof leverages several properties of the centrality potential,  $\Phi$  given in Section 4.4. Finally, claim item 4 of the lemma follows by careful calculation and choice of the sampling distribution  $q$  and is given towards the end of this section as Lemma 4.12 in Section 4.2.2.

# 4.2.1 Centrality

Here we analyze the effect of Algorithm 2 on the centrality potential  $\Phi$  to prove claim 3 of Lemma 4.5. Our proof leverages Lemma 4.34 and Lemma 4.36 which together reduces bound-

ing the potential  $\Phi (x^{(\mathrm{new})},s^{(\mathrm{new})},\mu^{(\mathrm{new})})$  to carefully analyzing bounding the first and second moments of  $\delta_s,\delta_x$ , and  $\delta_{\mu}$  (as well as there multiplicative stability).

More precisely, Lemma 4.34 shows that provided  $x^{(\mathrm{new})}$ ,  $s^{(\mathrm{new})}$ , and  $\mu^{(\mathrm{new})}$  do not change too much multiplicatively, i.e.  $\| \mathbf{X}^{-1}\delta_x\|_{\infty}$ ,  $\| \mathbf{S}^{-1}\delta_s\|_{\infty}$ , and  $|\delta_{\mu} / \mu |$  are sufficiently small, then

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} [ \Phi (x ^ {(\mathrm {n e w})}, s ^ {(\mathrm {n e w})}, \mu^ {(\mathrm {n e w})}) ] \leq \Phi (x, s, \mu) + \phi^ {\prime} (w) ^ {\top} \mathbf {W} \left[ (\mathbf {X} ^ {- 1} \mathbb {E} [ \delta_ {x} ] + \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {s} - \mu^ {- 1} \delta_ {\mu} \vec {1}) \right] \\ + O \left(\mathbb {E} \| \mathbf {X} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {x} \| _ {\phi^ {\prime \prime} (w)} ^ {2} + \| \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {x} \| _ {\phi^ {\prime \prime} (w)} ^ {2} + \| \mu^ {- 1} \delta_ {\mu} \vec {1} \| _ {\phi^ {\prime \prime} (w)} ^ {2}\right). \tag {17} \\ \end{array}
$$

(To see this, pick  $u^{(1)} = x$ ,  $\delta^{(1)} = \delta_x$ ,  $c^{(1)} = 1$ ,  $u^{(2)} = s$ ,  $\delta^{(2)} = \delta_s$ ,  $c^{(2)} = 1$ ,  $u^{(3)} = \mu \vec{1}$ ,  $\delta^{(3)} = \delta_{\mu} \vec{1}$ , and  $c^{(3)} = -1$  in Lemma 4.34 as we do in the proof of Lemma 4.10 later in this section). Lemma 4.34 follows by careful analysis of the second order Taylor expansion of  $\Phi$  and multiplicative stability of  $\phi$ .

Now, (17) implies that to bound that the centrality potential  $\Phi$  decreases in expectation, it suffices to show the following:

- First-order Expected Progress Bound: The first-order contribution of  $\delta_x$  and  $\delta_s$  in expectation, i.e.  $\overline{\mathbf{W}}[\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\delta_x] + \overline{\mathbf{S}}^{-1}\delta_s]$  is sufficiently well-aligned with  $-\phi'(w)$ , i.e.  $g$  (up to scaling). (We show this in Lemma 4.7.)  
- Multiplicative Stability and Second Order  $s$  Bound:  $s$  does not change too much multiplicatively, i.e.  $\| \overline{\mathbf{S}}^{-1}\delta_s\|_{\infty}$  is small, and its second order contribution to (17) is small, i.e.  $\| \mathbf{S}^{-1}\delta_x\|_{\phi''(w)}^2$  is small. (We show this in Lemma 4.8.)  
- Multiplicative Stability and Second Order  $x$  Bound:  $x$  does not change too much multiplicatively, i.e.  $\| \overline{\mathbf{X}}^{-1}\delta_x\|_{\infty}$  is small with probability 1, and its expected second order contribution to (17) is small, i.e.  $\mathbb{E}\| \mathbf{X}^{-1}\delta_x\|_{\phi''(w)}^2$  is small. (We show this in Lemma 4.9.)

We provide these results one at a time, after first providing some basic bounds on the components of a step, i.e.  $\delta_p$  and  $\delta_c$ , in Lemma 4.6. With these results in hand and some basic analysis of  $\delta_{\mu}$ , in Lemma 4.10, we can show that

$$
\mathbb {E} [ \Phi (x ^ {(\mathrm {n e w})}, s ^ {(\mathrm {n e w})}, \mu^ {(\mathrm {n e w})}) ] \leq \Phi (x, s, \mu) + \phi^ {\prime} (w) ^ {\top} g + O (\| \phi^ {\prime} (w) \| _ {2}) + O (\| \phi^ {\prime \prime} (w) \| _ {2})
$$

for some sufficiently small constants hidden in the  $O(\cdot)$  notation above. We obtain the desired Lemma 4.11 by applying Lemma 4.36 which leverages properties of  $\phi$  to show that progress of this forms yields to a sufficiently large decrease to  $\Phi$  whenever it is sufficiently large.

Lemma 4.6 (Basic Step Size Bounds). In Algorithm 2 we have that

$$
\left\| \delta_ {p} \right\| _ {2} \leq \exp (4 \epsilon) \gamma a n d \left\| \delta_ {c} \right\| _ {2} \leq \epsilon \exp (4 \epsilon) \gamma \tag {18}
$$

and consequently, as  $\epsilon < 1 / 80$

$$
\| \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {s} \| _ {2} \leq \exp (5 \epsilon) \gamma a n d \| \mathbf {X} ^ {- 1} \mathbb {E} [ \delta_ {x} ] \| _ {2} \leq 3 \gamma .
$$

Proof. Recall that  $\delta_p = \overline{\mathbf{W}}^{-1/2} \overline{\mathbf{A}} \mathbf{H}^{-1} \overline{\mathbf{A}}^\top \overline{\mathbf{W}}^{1/2} g$  and  $\overline{\mathbf{H}} \approx_{\gamma} \overline{\mathbf{A}}^\top \overline{\mathbf{A}}$  for  $\gamma \leq \epsilon$ . Since  $(x, s, \mu)$  is  $\epsilon$ -centered we have  $\overline{w} \approx_{3\epsilon} \vec{1}$  ( $\overline{w} \approx_{2\epsilon} w$  and  $w \approx_{\epsilon} 1$ ). Consequently,  $\overline{\mathbf{W}}^{-1} \preceq \exp(-3\epsilon) \mathbf{I}$  and

$$
\| \delta_ {p} \| _ {2} = \| \overline {{\mathbf {A H}}} ^ {- 1} \overline {{\mathbf {A}}} ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {W}}} ^ {1 / 2} g \| _ {\overline {{\mathbf {W}}} ^ {- 1}} \leq \exp (3 \epsilon / 2) \| \overline {{\mathbf {H}}} ^ {- 1} \overline {{\mathbf {A}}} ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {W}}} ^ {1 / 2} g \| _ {\overline {{\mathbf {A}}} ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {A}}}}. \tag {19}
$$

Now, applying  $\overline{\mathbf{H}}\approx_{\gamma}\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{A}}$  and  $\gamma \leq \epsilon$  twice we have

$$
\left\| \overline {{\mathbf {H}}} ^ {- 1} \overline {{\mathbf {A}}} ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {W}}} ^ {1 / 2} g \right\| _ {\overline {{\mathbf {A}}} ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {A}}}} \leq \exp (\epsilon / 2) \left\| \overline {{\mathbf {A}}} ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {W}}} ^ {1 / 2} g \right\| _ {\overline {{\mathbf {H}}} ^ {- 1}} \leq \exp (\epsilon) \left\| \overline {{\mathbf {W}}} ^ {1 / 2} g \right\| _ {\overline {{\mathbf {A}}} (\overline {{\mathbf {A}}} ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {A}}}) ^ {- 1} \overline {{\mathbf {A}}} ^ {\top}}. \tag {20}
$$

Further, since  $\overline{\mathbf{A}} (\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{A}})^{-1}\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}$  is a projection matrix we have  $\mathbf{0} \preceq \overline{\mathbf{A}} (\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{A}})^{-1}\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{\top} \preceq \mathbf{I}$  and

$$
\left\| \overline {{\mathbf {W}}} ^ {1 / 2} g \right\| _ {\overline {{\mathbf {A}}} \left(\overline {{\mathbf {A}}} ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {A}}}\right) ^ {- 1} \overline {{\mathbf {A}}} ^ {\top}} \leq \| g \| _ {\bar {w}} \leq \exp (3 \epsilon / 2) \| g \| _ {2} = \exp (3 \epsilon / 2) \gamma , \tag {21}
$$

where in the second to last step we again used  $\overline{w} \approx_{3\epsilon} \vec{1}$  and in the last step we used  $\|g\|_2 = \gamma$ . Combining (19), (20), and (21) yields that  $\|\delta_p\|_2 \leq \exp(4\epsilon)\gamma$  as desired.

Next, as  $\overline{x} \approx_{\epsilon} x$  and  $\overline{s} \approx_{\epsilon} s$  we have  $\overline{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{A}} \approx_{2\epsilon} \mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{S}^{-1}\mathbf{A}$ . Since  $\overline{\mathbf{H}} \approx_{\gamma} \overline{\mathbf{A}}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{A}}$  for  $\gamma \leq \epsilon$  this implies  $\overline{\mathbf{H}} \approx_{3\epsilon} \mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{S}^{-1}\mathbf{A}$ . Since,  $\delta_c = \mu^{-1/2}\overline{\mathbf{W}}^{-1/2}\overline{\mathbf{A}}\overline{\mathbf{H}}^{-1}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}x - b)$  and again  $\overline{w} \approx_{3\epsilon} \overrightarrow{1}$  we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \delta_ {c} \| _ {2} = \frac {1}{\sqrt {\mu}} \| \overline {{\mathbf {A H}}} ^ {- 1} [ \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x - b ] \| _ {\overline {{\mathbf {W}}} ^ {- 1}} \leq \frac {\exp (3 \epsilon / 2)}{\sqrt {\mu}} \| \overline {{\mathbf {H}}} ^ {- 1} [ \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x - b ] \| _ {\overline {{\mathbf {A}}} ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {A}}}} \\ \leq \frac {\exp (2 \epsilon)}{\sqrt {\mu}} \| \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x - b \| _ {\overline {{\mathbf {H}}} ^ {- 1}} \leq \frac {\exp (7 \epsilon / 2)}{\sqrt {\mu}} \| \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x - b \| _ {(\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1}} \leq \epsilon \exp (4 \epsilon) \gamma . \\ \end{array}
$$

where in the last step we used that  $\| \mathbf{A}^\top x - b\|_{(\mathbf{A}^\top \mathbf{X}\mathbf{S}^{-1}\mathbf{A})^{-1}}\leq \epsilon \gamma \sqrt{\mu}$  since  $(x,s,\mu)$  is  $\epsilon$ -centered.

Since,  $\| \mathbf{S}^{-1}\delta_s\| _2\leq \exp (\epsilon)\| \overline{\mathbf{S}}^{-1}\delta_s\| _2 = \exp (\epsilon)\| \delta_p\| _2$  this yields the desired bound on  $\| \overline{\mathbf{S}}^{-1}\delta_s\| _2$  as well. The remainder of the result follows as by definition and triangle inequality we have

$$
\| \overline {{\mathbf {X}}} ^ {- 1} \mathbb {E} [ \delta_ {x} ] \| _ {2} = \| g - \delta_ {p} - \delta_ {c} \| _ {2} \leq \| g \| + \| \delta_ {p} \| _ {2} + \| \delta_ {c} \| _ {2}
$$

and we have  $(1 + (1 + \epsilon)\exp (3\epsilon))\leq 3$  for  $\epsilon \in [0,1 / 80]$

We now want to prove in Lemma 4.7 that  $\overline{\mathbf{W}}[\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\delta_x] + \overline{\mathbf{S}}^{-1}\delta_s]$  is sufficiently well-aligned with  $g$ . For that we will use the following Lemma 4.32 which will be proven in Section 4.4 and bounds additive  $\ell_2$ -error with respect to multiplicative spectral-error.

Lemma 4.32. If  $\mathbf{M} \approx_{\epsilon} \mathbf{N}$  for symmetric  $PD$ $\mathbf{M}, \mathbf{N} \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$  and  $\epsilon \in [0,1/2)$  then

$$
\| \mathbf {N} ^ {- 1 / 2} (\mathbf {M} - \mathbf {N}) \mathbf {N} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {2} \leq \epsilon + \epsilon^ {2}.
$$

Consequently, if  $x \approx_{\epsilon} y$  for  $x, y \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}$  and  $\epsilon \in [0,1/2)$  then  $\|\mathbf{X}^{-1}y - \vec{1}\|_{\infty} \leq \epsilon + \epsilon^2$ .

Lemma 4.7 (First-order Expected Progress Bound). In Algorithm 2 we have

$$
\left\| \mathbf {W} \left(\mathbf {X} ^ {- 1} \mathbb {E} [ \delta_ {x} ] + \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbb {E} [ \delta_ {s} ]\right) - g \right\| _ {2} \leq 7 \epsilon \gamma .
$$

Proof. Note that

$$
\mathbf {X} ^ {- 1} \mathbb {E} [ \delta_ {x} ] + \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbb {E} [ \delta_ {s} ] = \overline {{\mathbf {X}}} ^ {- 1} \mathbb {E} [ \delta_ {x} ] + \overline {{\mathbf {S}}} ^ {- 1} \mathbb {E} [ \delta_ {s} ] + (\overline {{\mathbf {X}}} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {X} - \mathbf {I}) \mathbf {X} ^ {- 1} \mathbb {E} [ \delta_ {x} ] + (\overline {{\mathbf {S}}} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {S} - \mathbf {I}) \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbb {E} [ \delta_ {s} ].
$$

Further, by the design of  $\mathbb{E}[\delta_x]$  and  $\mathbb{E}[\delta_s]$  we have  $\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\delta_x] + \overline{\mathbf{S}}^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\delta_s] = g - \delta_c$ . Also, since  $\overline{\mathbf{X}}\approx_{\epsilon}\mathbf{X}$  and  $\overline{\mathbf{S}}\approx_{\epsilon}\mathbf{S}$ , Lemma 4.32 implies that  $\| \overline{\mathbf{X}}^{-1}\mathbf{X} - \mathbf{I}\| _2\leq \epsilon +\epsilon^2\leq \epsilon \exp (\epsilon)$  and similarly,  $\| \overline{\mathbf{S}}^{-1}\mathbf{S} - \mathbf{I}\| _2\leq \epsilon \exp (\epsilon)$ . Therefore Lemma 4.6 implies

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \mathbf {W} (\mathbf {X} ^ {- 1} \mathbb {E} [ \delta_ {x} ] + \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbb {E} [ \delta_ {s} ]) - \mathbf {W} g \| _ {2} \\ \leq \| \delta_ {c} \| _ {\mathbf {W} ^ {2}} + \| (\overline {{\mathbf {X}}} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {X} - \mathbf {I}) \overline {{\mathbf {X}}} ^ {- 1} \mathbb {E} [ \delta_ {x} ] \| _ {\mathbf {W} ^ {2}} + \| (\overline {{\mathbf {S}}} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {S} - \mathbf {I}) \overline {{\mathbf {S}}} ^ {- 1} \mathbb {E} [ \delta_ {s} ] \| _ {\mathbf {W} ^ {2}} \\ \leq \exp (\epsilon) \left(\| \delta_ {c} \| _ {2} + \epsilon \exp (\epsilon) \left(\| \overline {{\mathbf {X}}} ^ {- 1} \mathbb {E} [ \delta_ {x} ] \| _ {2} + \| \overline {{\mathbf {S}}} ^ {- 1} \mathbb {E} [ \delta_ {s} ] \| _ {2}\right)\right) \\ \leq \exp (\epsilon) (\epsilon \exp (4 \epsilon) + \epsilon \exp (\epsilon) (3 + \exp (5 \epsilon)) \gamma \\ = \epsilon \gamma \cdot \Big (\exp (\epsilon) \cdot (\exp (4 \epsilon) + \exp (\epsilon)) \cdot (3 + \exp (5 \epsilon)) \Big). \\ \end{array}
$$

Further, as  $w\approx_{\epsilon}\mathbf{I}$ , Lemma 4.32 implies

$$
\| \mathbf {W} g - g \| _ {2} \leq \| \mathbf {W} - \mathbf {I} \| _ {2} \| g \| _ {2} \leq (\epsilon + \epsilon^ {2}) \gamma = \epsilon \gamma \cdot (1 + \epsilon).
$$

The result follows as  $\exp (\epsilon)(\exp (4\epsilon) + (\exp (\epsilon))(3 + \exp (5\epsilon))) + (1 + \epsilon)\leq 7$  for  $\epsilon \in [0,1 / 80]$ .

Lemma 4.8 (Multiplicative Stability and Second Order  $s$  Bound). In Algorithm 2 we have

$$
\| \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {s} \| _ {\infty} \leq \exp (5 \epsilon) \gamma a n d \| \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {s} \| _ {\phi^ {\prime \prime} (v)} ^ {2} \leq \exp (1 0 \epsilon) \gamma^ {2} \| \phi^ {\prime \prime} (v) \| _ {2}.
$$

Proof. The result follows from Lemma 4.6,  $\| \mathbf{S}^{-1}\delta_s\|_{\infty}\leq \| \mathbf{S}^{-1}\delta_s\| _2$ , and

$$
\| \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {s} \| _ {\phi^ {\prime \prime} (v)} ^ {2} \leq \| \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {s} \| _ {\infty} \cdot \| \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {s} \| _ {2} \cdot \| \phi^ {\prime \prime} (v) \| _ {2}.
$$

![](images/e5ed5d77fdf7fc322d12ccf0235be7fdc601d4cec44ba858b7a7c827400dbea8.jpg)

Lemma 4.9 (Multiplicative Stability and Second Order  $x$  Bound). In Algorithm 2 we have

$$
\mathbb {P} \Big [ \| \mathbf {X} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {x} \| _ {\infty} \leq \exp (6 \epsilon) \gamma \Big ] = 1 a n d \mathbb {E} \Big [ \| \mathbf {X} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {x} \| _ {\phi^ {\prime \prime} (v)} ^ {2} \Big ] \leq 7 \gamma^ {2} \| \phi^ {\prime \prime} (v) \| _ {2}.
$$

Proof. Recall that  $\delta_x = \overline{\mathbf{X}} g - \overline{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{R}\delta_r$  and  $x \approx_{\epsilon} \overline{x} \in \mathbb{R}^m$ . Consequently,

$$
\| \mathbf {X} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {x} \| _ {\infty} \leq \exp (\epsilon) \cdot \left(\| g \| _ {\infty} + \| \mathbf {R} \delta_ {r} \| _ {\infty}\right).
$$

Now,  $\mathbf{R}_{ii} \leq [\min\{1, q_i\}]^{-1} = \max\{1, q_i^{-1}\}$  by definition and therefore

$$
\| \mathbf {R} \delta_ {r} \| _ {\infty} \leq \max _ {i \in [ m ]} \max \left\{| [ \delta_ {r} ] _ {i} |, \frac {| [ \delta_ {r} ] _ {i} |}{\sqrt {m} ([ \delta_ {r} ] _ {i} ^ {2} / \| \delta_ {r} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + 1 / m)} \right\} \leq \max \left\{\| \delta_ {r} \| _ {\infty}, \frac {1}{2} \| \delta_ {r} \| _ {2} \right\} \leq \| \delta_ {r} \| _ {2}.
$$

where we used that  $q_{i} \geq \sqrt{m}\left(\left[\delta_{r}\right]_{i}^{2} / \left\| \delta_{r}\right\|_{2}^{2} + 1 / m\right)$  by assumption and

$$
\left| \left[ \delta_ {r} \right] _ {i} \right| \leq \frac {\sqrt {m}}{2 \| \delta_ {r} \| _ {2}} \left[ \delta_ {r} \right] _ {i} ^ {2} + \frac {\| \delta_ {r} \| _ {2}}{2 \sqrt {m}} = \frac {\sqrt {m}}{2} \| \delta_ {r} \| _ {2} \cdot \left(\left[ \delta_ {r} \right] _ {i} ^ {2} / \| \delta_ {r} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + 1 / m\right). \tag {22}
$$

Since by definition and triangle inequality  $\| \delta_r\| _2\leq \| \delta_p\| _2 + \| \delta_c\| _2$  the desired bound on  $\| \mathbf{X}^{-1}\delta_x\|_{\infty}$  follows from Lemma 4.6 and  $\exp (5\epsilon)(1 + \epsilon)\leq 2$  for  $\epsilon \in [0,1 / 80]$ .

Now, since  $\overline{x} \approx_{\epsilon} x$  the definition of  $\delta_x$  and  $\|a + b\|_2^2 \leq 2\|a\|_2^2 + 2\|b\|_2^2$  imply

$$
\| \mathbf {X} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {x} \| _ {\phi^ {\prime \prime} (v)} ^ {2} \leq \exp (2 \epsilon) \| g - \mathbf {R} \delta_ {r} \| _ {\phi^ {\prime \prime} (v)} ^ {2} \leq 2 \exp (2 \epsilon) \| g \| _ {\phi^ {\prime \prime} (v)} ^ {2} + 2 \exp (2 \epsilon) \| \mathbf {R} \delta_ {r} \| _ {\phi^ {\prime \prime} (v)} ^ {2}.
$$

Now,

$$
\| g \| _ {\phi^ {\prime \prime} (v)} ^ {2} \leq \| \phi^ {\prime \prime} (v) \| _ {2} \| g \| _ {\infty} \| g \| _ {2} \leq \| \phi^ {\prime \prime} (v) \| _ {2} \| g \| _ {2} ^ {2} = \| \phi^ {\prime \prime} (v) \| _ {2} \gamma^ {2}.
$$

Further, the definition of  $\mathbf{R}$  and that

$$
[ \min \{1, q _ {i} \} ] ^ {- 1} \leq 1 + \frac {1}{q _ {i}} \leq 1 + \frac {\| \delta_ {r} \| _ {2} ^ {2}}{\sqrt {m} [ \delta_ {r} ] _ {i} ^ {2}}
$$

imply that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} _ {\mathbf {R}} \left[ \| \mathbf {R} \delta_ {r} \| _ {\phi^ {\prime \prime} (v)} ^ {2} \right] = \sum_ {i \in [ m ]} [ \phi^ {\prime \prime} (v) ] _ {i} \cdot \frac {[ \delta_ {r} ] _ {i} ^ {2}}{\min \{q _ {i} , 1 \}} \\ \leq \sum_ {i \in [ m ]} \left[ \phi^ {\prime \prime} (v) \right] _ {i} \cdot \left[ \left[ \delta_ {r} \right] _ {i} ^ {2} + \frac {1}{\sqrt {m}} \| \delta_ {r} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \right] \\ \leq \| \phi^ {\prime \prime} (v) \| _ {2} \| \delta_ {r} \| _ {\infty} \| \delta_ {r} \| _ {2} + \| \phi^ {\prime \prime} (v) \| _ {2} \| \delta_ {r} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \\ = 2 \| \phi^ {\prime \prime} (v) \| _ {2} \| \delta_ {r} \| _ {2} ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

The result follows again from  $\| \delta_r\| _2\leq \| \delta_p\| _2 + \| \delta_c\| _2$ , Lemma 4.6, and  $2\exp (2\epsilon) + 4\exp (2\epsilon)((1+\epsilon)\exp (4\epsilon))^2\leq 7$  for  $\epsilon \in [0,1 / 80]$ .

With the previous bounds on first and second order error, we now want to bound in Lemma 4.10 how much our potential  $\Phi$  changes. For that we first require the following expansion of  $\Phi$  obtained via Taylor-approximation. This expansion is proven in Section 4.4.

Lemma 4.34. For all  $j \in [k]$  let vector  $u^{(j)} \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^n$ , exponent  $c_{j} \in \mathbb{R}$  and change  $\delta^{(j)} \in \mathbb{R}^n$  induce vectors  $v, v^{(\mathrm{new})} \in \mathbb{R}^n$  defined by

$$
v _ {i} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \prod_ {j \in [ k ]} (u _ {i} ^ {(j)}) ^ {c _ {j}} a n d v _ {i} ^ {(\mathrm {n e w})} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \prod_ {j \in [ k ]} (u _ {i} ^ {(j)} + \delta_ {i} ^ {(j)}) ^ {c _ {j}} f o r a l l i \in [ n ].
$$

Further, let  $\mathbf{V} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \mathbf{Diag}(v)$  and  $\mathbf{U}_j \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \mathbf{Diag}(u^{(j)})$  and suppose that  $\| \mathbf{U}_j^{-1} \delta^{(j)} \|_{\infty} \leq \frac{1}{12(1 + \lambda \| c \|_1)}$  for all  $j \in [k]$  and  $v \leq \frac{13}{12} \vec{1}$ . Then  $v^{(\mathrm{new})} \approx_{1/(11\lambda)} v$  and

$$
\Phi (v ^ {(\mathrm {n e w})}) \leq \Phi (v) + \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} c _ {j} \phi^ {\prime} (v) ^ {\top} \mathbf {V} \mathbf {U} _ {j} ^ {- 1} \delta^ {(j)} + 2 (1 + \| c \| _ {1}) \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} | c _ {j} | \cdot \| \mathbf {U} ^ {- 1} \delta^ {(j)} \| _ {\phi^ {\prime \prime} (v)} ^ {2}.
$$

We now have everything to bound the effect of a step in terms of  $\| \phi'(w) \|_2$  and  $\| \phi''(w) \|_2$ .

Lemma 4.10 (Expected Potential Decrease). In Algorithm 2 we have

$$
\mathbb {E} [ \Phi (x ^ {\mathrm {(n e w)}}, s ^ {\mathrm {(n e w)}}, \mu^ {\mathrm {(n e w)}}) ] \leq \Phi (x, s, \mu) + \phi^ {\prime} (w) ^ {\top} g + 9 \epsilon \gamma \| \phi^ {\prime} (w) \| _ {2} + 8 0 \gamma^ {2} \| \phi^ {\prime \prime} (w) \| _ {2}.
$$

Proof. We apply Lemma 4.34 with  $u^{(1)} = x$ ,  $\delta^{(1)} = \delta_x$ ,  $c^{(1)} = 1$ ,  $u^{(2)} = s$ ,  $\delta^{(2)} = \delta_s$ ,  $c^{(2)} = 1$ ,  $u^{(3)} = \mu \vec{1}$ ,  $\delta^{(3)} = \delta_{\mu} \vec{1}$ , and  $c^{(3)} = -1$ . Note that,  $v$  in the context of Lemma 4.34 is precisely  $w(x,s,\mu)$  and  $v^{(\mathrm{new})}$  in the context of Lemma 4.34 is precisely  $w(x + \delta_x,s + \delta_s,\mu +\delta_\mu)$ .

Now, by Lemma 4.8, the definition of  $\gamma$ , and  $\epsilon \in [0,1/80]$  we have

$$
\| \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {s} \| _ {\infty} \leq 2 \gamma \leq \frac {1}{5 0 \lambda} \leq \frac {1}{1 2 (1 + \| c \| _ {1} \lambda)}.
$$

Similarly, Lemma 4.9 implies

$$
\| \mathbf {X} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {x} \| _ {\infty} \leq 2 \gamma \leq \frac {1}{5 0 \lambda} \leq \frac {1}{1 2 (1 + \| c \| _ {1} \lambda)}.
$$

Further, by definition of  $r$  we have  $\mu^{-1}|\delta_{\mu}| \leq \gamma \leq (12(1 + \|c\|_1\lambda))^{-1}\mathrm{l}$ . Thus, Lemma 4.34 implies

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} [ \Phi (x ^ {(\mathrm {n e w})}, s ^ {(\mathrm {n e w})}, \mu^ {(\mathrm {n e w})}) ] \leq \Phi (x, s, \mu) + \phi^ {\prime} (w) ^ {\top} \mathbf {W} \left[ (\mathbf {X} ^ {- 1} \mathbb {E} [ \delta_ {x} ] + \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {s} - \mu^ {- 1} \delta_ {\mu} \vec {1}) \right] \\ + 8 \left(\| \mathbb {E} \mathbf {X} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {x} \| _ {\phi^ {\prime \prime} (w)} ^ {2} + \| \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {x} \| _ {\phi^ {\prime \prime} (w)} ^ {2} + \| \mu^ {- 1} \delta_ {\mu} \vec {1} \| _ {\phi^ {\prime \prime} (w)} ^ {2}\right). \tag {23} \\ \end{array}
$$

Now, since  $w\approx_{\epsilon}\vec{1}$ , the assumption on  $\delta_{\mu}$  implies

$$
| (\delta_ {\mu} / \mu) \phi^ {\prime} (w) ^ {\top} \mathbf {W} \vec {1} | \leq | (\delta_ {\mu} / \mu) | \exp (\epsilon) \| \phi^ {\prime} (w) \| _ {2} \sqrt {m} \leq 2 \epsilon \gamma \| \phi^ {\prime} (w) \| _ {2}
$$

and

$$
\| (\delta_ {\mu} / \mu) \vec {1} \| _ {\phi^ {\prime \prime} (w)} ^ {2} = | (\delta_ {\mu} / \mu) | ^ {2} \| \phi^ {\prime \prime} (w) \| _ {1} \leq (\epsilon \gamma / \sqrt {m}) ^ {2} \sqrt {m} \| \phi^ {\prime \prime} (w) \| _ {2} \leq \gamma^ {2} \| \phi^ {\prime \prime} (w) \| _ {2}.
$$

The result follows by applying Lemmas 4.7, 4.8, and 4.9 to the expectation of (23).

To obtain our desired bound on  $\mathbb{E}[\Phi (x^{(\mathrm{new})},s^{(\mathrm{new})},\mu^{(\mathrm{new})})]$  we will use the following general Lemma 4.36 to obtain bounds on  $\| \phi^{\prime}(w)\|_{2}$  and  $\| \phi ''(w)\| _2$  . Lemma 4.36 is proven in Section 4.4.

Lemma 4.36. Let  $U \subset \mathbb{R}^m$  be an axis-symmetric convex set which is contained in the  $\ell_{\infty}$  ball of radius 1 and contains the  $\ell_{\infty}$  ball of radius  $u \leq 1$ , i.e.  $x \in U$  implies  $\|x\|_{\infty} \leq 1$  and for some  $\|x\|_{\infty} \leq u$  implies  $x \in U$ . Let  $w, v \in \mathbb{R}^m$  such that  $\|w - v\|_{\infty} \leq \delta \leq \frac{1}{5\lambda}$ . Let  $g = -\gamma \operatorname{argmax}_{z \in U} \langle \nabla \Phi(v), z \rangle$  and  $\|h\|_U \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \max_{z \in U} \langle h, z \rangle$  with  $\gamma \geq 0$  and

$$
\delta_ {\Phi} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \phi^ {\prime} (w) ^ {\top} g + c _ {1} \gamma \| \phi^ {\prime} (w) \| _ {U} + c _ {2} \gamma^ {2} \| \phi^ {\prime \prime} (w) \| _ {U}
$$

for some  $c_{1}, c_{2} \geq 0$  satisfying  $\lambda \gamma \leq \frac{1}{8}$ ,  $2\lambda \delta + c_{2}\lambda \gamma \leq 0.5(1 - c_{1})$ . Then,

$$
\delta_ {\Phi} \leq - 0. 5 (1 - c _ {1}) \lambda \gamma u \Phi (w) + m
$$

Finally, we can prove the desired bound on  $\mathbb{E}[\Phi (x^{(\mathrm{new})},s^{(\mathrm{new})},\mu^{(\mathrm{new})})]$  by combining the preceding lemma with known bounds on  $\| \phi^{\prime}(w)\|_{2}$  and  $\| \phi ''(w)\| _2$  . We first provide a very general lemma for this which we can use in both the log barrier and LS barrier analysis and then we use it to prove our desired bound.

Lemma 4.11 (Centrality Improvement of Log Barrier Short-Step). In Algorithm 2 we have

$$
\mathbb {E} \bigl [ \Phi (x ^ {\mathrm {(n e w)}}, s ^ {\mathrm {(n e w)}}, \mu^ {\mathrm {(n e w)}}) \bigr ] \leq (1 - \lambda r) \Phi (x, s, \mu) + \exp (\lambda \epsilon / 6).
$$

Proof. Consider an invocation of Lemma 4.36 with  $\delta = \gamma$ ,  $c_{1} = 9\epsilon$ ,  $c_{2} = 80$ ,  $U$  set to be the unit  $\ell_{2}$  ball. Note that the assumptions of Lemma 4.36 hold as  $\gamma = \epsilon/(100\lambda)$ . Further, as  $U$  contains the  $\ell_{\infty}$  ball of radius  $\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}$ ,  $\| \overline{v} - w \|_{\infty} \leq \gamma$ , and  $g = -\gamma \nabla \Phi(\overline{v}) / \| \nabla \Phi(\overline{v}) \|_{2}$  we see that Lemma 4.36 applied to the claim of Lemma 4.10 yields that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} \left[ \Phi \left(x ^ {\text {(n e w)}}, s ^ {\text {(n e w)}}, \mu^ {\text {(n e w)}}\right) \right] \leq \Phi (x, s, \mu) - \left(\frac {1 - c _ {1}}{2}\right) \frac {\lambda \gamma}{\sqrt {m}} \Phi (x, s, \mu) + m \\ \leq \left(1 - \frac {\lambda \gamma}{4 \sqrt {m}}\right) \Phi (x, s, \mu) + m. \\ \end{array}
$$

The result follows from  $\exp (\lambda \epsilon /6)\geq m$  and  $r = \frac{\epsilon\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}$
