\begin{array}{l} \left| \phi^ {\prime} (w) \right| = \lambda \cdot \left(\exp (\lambda | w - 1 |) - \exp (- \lambda | w - 1 |)\right) \\ = \frac {1}{\lambda} \phi^ {\prime \prime} (w) - 2 \lambda \exp (- \lambda | w - 1 |)) \geq \frac {1}{\lambda} \phi^ {\prime \prime} (w) - 2 \lambda . \\ \end{array}
$$

The bound on  $\phi''(z_1)$  is then immediate from  $\phi''(z_1) = \lambda^2 \cdot (\exp(\lambda(z_1 - )) + \exp(\lambda(1 - z_1)))$ .

Lemma 4.34. For all  $j \in [k]$  let vector  $u^{(j)} \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^n$ , exponent  $c_{j} \in \mathbb{R}$  and change  $\delta^{(j)} \in \mathbb{R}^n$  induce vectors  $v, v^{(\mathrm{new})} \in \mathbb{R}^n$  defined by

$$
v _ {i} \stackrel {{\mathrm {d e f}}} {{=}} \prod_ {j \in [ k ]} (u _ {i} ^ {(j)}) ^ {c _ {j}} a n d v _ {i} ^ {(\mathrm {n e w})} \stackrel {{\mathrm {d e f}}} {{=}} \prod_ {j \in [ k ]} (u _ {i} ^ {(j)} + \delta_ {i} ^ {(j)}) ^ {c _ {j}} f o r a l l i \in [ n ].
$$

Further, let  $\mathbf{V} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \mathbf{Diag}(v)$  and  $\mathbf{U}_j \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \mathbf{Diag}(u^{(j)})$  and suppose that  $\| \mathbf{U}_j^{-1} \delta^{(j)} \|_{\infty} \leq \frac{1}{12(1 + \lambda \| c \|_1)}$  for all  $j \in [k]$  and  $v \leq \frac{13}{12} \vec{1}$ . Then  $v^{(\mathrm{new})} \approx_{1/(11\lambda)} v$  and

$$
\Phi (v ^ {(\mathrm {n e w})}) \leq \Phi (v) + \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} c _ {j} \phi^ {\prime} (v) ^ {\top} \mathbf {V} \mathbf {U} _ {j} ^ {- 1} \delta^ {(j)} + 2 (1 + \| c \| _ {1}) \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} | c _ {j} | \cdot \| \mathbf {U} ^ {- 1} \delta^ {(j)} \| _ {\phi^ {\prime \prime} (v)} ^ {2}.
$$

Proof. For all  $t \in [0,1]$  let  $z_{t} \in \mathbb{R}^{n}$  be defined for all  $i \in [n]$  by  $[z_t]_i \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \prod_{j \in [k]} (u_i^{(j)} + t \cdot \delta_i^{(j)})^{c_j}$  and  $f(t) \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \Phi(z_t)$ . Note that  $z_0 = v$  and  $z_1 = v^{(\mathrm{new})}$  and consequently  $f(1) = \Phi(v^{(\mathrm{new})})$  and  $f(0) \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \Phi(v)$ . Further, by Taylor's theorem,  $f(1) = f(0) + f'(0) + \frac{1}{2} f''(\zeta)$  for some  $\zeta \in [0,1]$ . Consequently, it suffices to show that  $z_t \approx_{1/(11\lambda)} z_0$ , compute  $f'(0)$  and upper bound  $f''(\zeta)$ .

First, by direct calculation, we know that for all  $i \in [n]$  and  $t \in [0,1]$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {d}{d t} \left[ z _ {t} \right] _ {i} = \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} \left[ \left(\prod_ {j ^ {\prime} \in [ k ] \backslash j} \left(u _ {i} ^ {(j ^ {\prime})} + t \cdot \delta_ {i} ^ {(j ^ {\prime})}\right) ^ {c _ {j ^ {\prime}}}\right) \cdot \left(\frac {d}{d t} \left(u _ {i} ^ {(j)} + t \cdot \delta_ {i} ^ {(j)}\right) ^ {c _ {j}}\right) \right] \\ = \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} \left[ \left([ z _ {t} ] _ {i} \cdot \left(u _ {i} ^ {(j)} + t \cdot \delta_ {i} ^ {(j)}\right) ^ {- c _ {j}}\right) \cdot c _ {j} \left(u _ {i} ^ {(j)} + t \cdot \delta_ {i} ^ {(j)}\right) ^ {c _ {j} - 1} \cdot \delta_ {i} ^ {(j)} \right] \\ = [ z _ {t} ] _ {i} \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} \frac {c _ {j} \cdot \delta_ {i} ^ {(j)}}{u _ {i} ^ {(j)} + t \cdot \delta_ {i} ^ {(j)}} \\ \end{array}
$$

and

$$
\frac {d ^ {2}}{d t ^ {2}} [ z _ {t} ] _ {i} = \left[ \frac {d}{d t} [ z _ {t} ] _ {i} \right] \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} \frac {c _ {j} \cdot \delta_ {i} ^ {(j)}}{u _ {i} ^ {(j)} + t \cdot \delta_ {i} ^ {(j)}} - [ z _ {t} ] _ {i} \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} \frac {c _ {j} (\delta_ {i} ^ {(j)}) ^ {2}}{(u _ {i} ^ {(j)} + t \cdot \delta_ {i} ^ {(j)}) ^ {2}}.
$$

Thus,  $\mathbf{Z}_t\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathbf{Diag}(z_t)$ ,  $u_{t}^{(j)}\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}u^{(j)} + t\delta^{(j)}$ ,  $\mathbf{U}_t^{(j)}\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathbf{Diag}(u_t^{(j)})$ , and  $\widetilde{\delta}_t^{(j)}\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}[\mathbf{U}_t^{(j)}]^{-1}\delta^{(j)}$  satisfy

$$
\frac {d}{d t} z _ {t} = \mathbf {Z} _ {t} \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} c _ {j} \widetilde {\delta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} \text {a n d} \frac {d ^ {2}}{d t ^ {2}} z _ {t} = \mathbf {Z} _ {t} \left(\left[ \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} c _ {j} \widetilde {\delta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} \right] ^ {2} - \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} c _ {j} [ \widetilde {\delta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} ] ^ {2}\right) \tag {44}
$$

where for  $a \in \mathbb{R}^n$  we define  $a^2 \in \mathbb{R}^n$  with  $[a^2]_i \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} [a_i]^2$ .

Applying chain rule and (44) yields that

$$
f ^ {\prime} (t) = \sum_ {i \in [ n ]} \phi^ {\prime} ([ z _ {t} ] _ {i}) \frac {d}{d t} [ z _ {t} ] _ {i} = \phi^ {\prime} (z _ {t}) ^ {\top} \mathbf {Z} _ {t} \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} c _ {j} \widetilde {\delta} _ {t} ^ {(j)}.
$$

Since  $z_0 = v$ ,  $\mathbf{U}_0^{(j)} = \mathbf{U}_j$ , and  $\widetilde{\delta}_0^{(j)} = \mathbf{U}_j^{-1}\delta^{(j)}$  we have  $f'(0) = \sum_{j \in [k]} c_j \phi'(v)^\top \mathbf{V} \mathbf{U}^{-1}\delta^{(j)}$  as desired.

Further application of chain rule and (44) yields that

$$
\begin{array}{l} f ^ {\prime \prime} (t) = \sum_ {i \in [ n ]} \phi^ {\prime \prime} ([ z _ {t} ] _ {i}) \left(\frac {d}{d t} [ z _ {t} ] _ {i}\right) ^ {2} + \sum_ {i \in [ n ]} \phi^ {\prime} ([ z _ {t} ] _ {i}) \frac {d ^ {2}}{d t ^ {2}} z _ {t} \\ = \sum_ {i \in [ n ]} \phi^ {\prime \prime} ([ z _ {t} ] _ {i}) \cdot \left[ \mathbf {Z} _ {t} \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} c _ {j} \widetilde {\delta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} \right] _ {i} ^ {2} + \sum_ {i \in [ n ]} \phi^ {\prime} ([ z _ {t} ] _ {i}) \cdot \left([ z _ {t} ] _ {i} \left[ \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} c _ {j} \widetilde {\delta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} \right] _ {i} ^ {2} - [ z _ {t} ] _ {i} \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} [ \widetilde {\delta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} ] _ {i} ^ {2}\right) \\ = \left\| \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} c _ {j} \widetilde {\delta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} \right\| _ {{\bf Z} _ {t} \phi^ {\prime \prime} (z _ {t}) {\bf Z} _ {t} + {\bf Z} _ {t} \phi^ {\prime} (z _ {t})} ^ {2} - \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} c _ {j} \left\| \widetilde {\delta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} \right\| _ {{\bf Z} _ {t} \phi^ {\prime} (z _ {t})} ^ {2}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Now, since by Cauchy Schwarz for all  $i \in [n]$  we have

$$
\sum_ {j \in [ k ]} \left[ c _ {j} \widetilde {\delta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} \right] _ {i} ^ {2} \leq \left(\sum_ {j \in [ k ]} | c _ {j} |\right) \cdot \left(\sum_ {j \in [ k ]} | c _ {j} | \cdot \left[ \widetilde {\delta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} \right] _ {j} ^ {2}\right)
$$

we have that

$$
\begin{array}{l} f ^ {\prime \prime} (t) \leq \| c \| _ {1} \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} | c _ {j} | \left\| \widetilde {\delta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} \right\| _ {\mathbf {Z} _ {t} \phi^ {\prime \prime} (z _ {t}) \mathbf {Z} _ {t} + \mathbf {Z} _ {t} | \phi^ {\prime} (z _ {t}) |} ^ {2} - \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} c _ {j} \left\| \widetilde {\delta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} \right\| _ {\mathbf {Z} _ {t} \phi^ {\prime} (z _ {t})} ^ {2} \\ \leq \| c \| _ {1} \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} | c _ {j} | \left\| \widetilde {\delta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} \right\| _ {\mathbf {Z} _ {t} \phi^ {\prime \prime} (z _ {t}) \mathbf {Z} _ {t}} ^ {2} + (\| c \| _ {1} + 1) \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} | c _ {j} | \cdot \left\| \widetilde {\delta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} \right\| _ {\mathbf {Z} _ {t} | \phi^ {\prime} (z _ {t}) |} ^ {2} \\ \leq \left(\| c \| _ {1} + 1\right) \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} | c _ {j} | \cdot \left\| \widetilde {\delta} _ {t} ^ {(j)} \right\| _ {\left(\mathbf {Z} _ {t} ^ {2} + \mathbf {Z} _ {t}\right) \phi^ {\prime \prime} \left(z _ {t}\right)} ^ {2} \tag {45} \\ \end{array}
$$

where in the last line we used that  $\phi''(z_t) \geq \lambda |\phi'(z_{t,})| \geq |\phi'(z_t)|$  by Lemma 4.33 and  $\lambda \geq 1$ .

Now, by construction and assumption we have that  $v$ ,  $v^{(\mathrm{new})}, u^{(j)}$ , and  $u_{t}^{(j)}$  are all entrywise positive. Since  $\| \mathbf{U}_j^{-1}\delta^{(j)}\|_\infty \leq \frac{1}{12(1 + \lambda\|c\|_1)}$  for all  $j \in [k]$  we have that for all  $t \in [0,1]$  and  $i \in [n]$

$$
[ u _ {t} ^ {(j)} ] _ {i} \leq \left(1 - \frac {1}{1 2 (1 + \lambda \| c \| _ {1})}\right) ^ {- | c _ {j} |} [ u _ {t} ^ {(j)} ] _ {0} \leq \exp \left(\frac {| c _ {j} |}{1 1 (1 + \lambda \| c \| _ {1})}\right) [ u _ {t} ^ {(j)} ] _ {0}
$$

where we used that  $1 - x \leq \exp(-x)$  for all  $x$  yields and for all  $x \in (0, 1)$  and  $c \geq 0$  it is the case that  $(1 - x)^{-c} \geq \max \left\{(1 + x)^{c}, (1 + x)^{-c}, (1 + x)^{-c}\right\}$ . Consequently,  $\mathbf{U}_t^{(j)} \preceq \exp\left(\frac{1}{11\lambda}\right)\mathbf{U}$  and for all  $t \in [0, 1]$  and  $i \in [n]$  we have

$$
[ z _ {t} ] _ {i} \leq \prod_ {j \in [ k ]} [ u _ {t} ^ {(j)} ] _ {i} \leq \prod_ {j \in [ k ]} \exp \left(\frac {| c _ {j} |}{1 1 (1 + \lambda \| c \| _ {1})}\right) [ u ^ {(j)} ] _ {i} \leq \exp (\frac {1}{1 1 \lambda}) [ z _ {0} ] _ {i}.
$$

Thus,  $\mathbf{Z}_t \preceq \exp\left(\frac{1}{11\lambda}\right)\mathbf{V}$  and by analogous calculation  $\mathbf{U}_t^{(j)} \succeq \exp\left(-\frac{1}{11\lambda}\right)\mathbf{U}^{(j)}$  and  $\mathbf{Z}_t \succeq \exp\left(-\frac{1}{11\lambda}\right)\mathbf{V}$ .

By the preceding paragraph,  $u_{t}^{(j)} \approx_{1/(11\lambda)} u^{(j)}$  and  $z_{t} \approx_{1/(11\lambda)} v$ . Further,  $v \leq \frac{13}{12}$  and Lemma 4.32 implies

$$
\| z _ {t} - v \| _ {\infty} = \left\| \left(\mathbf {V} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {Z} _ {t} - \vec {1}\right) \mathbf {V} \right\| _ {2} \leq \left\| \mathbf {V} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {Z} _ {t} - \vec {1} \right\| _ {2} \left\| \mathbf {V} \right\| _ {2} \leq \left(\frac {1}{1 1 \lambda} + \left(\frac {1}{1 1 \lambda}\right) ^ {2}\right) \frac {1 3}{1 2} \leq \frac {0 . 1 1}{\lambda}.
$$

Consequently, by Lemma 4.33 we have that  $\phi''(z_t) \leq \exp(0.11)\phi''(v)$ . Combining these facts with (45) then yields that

$$
\begin{array}{l} f ^ {\prime \prime} (t) \leq \left(\| c \| _ {1} + 1\right) \sum_ {j \in [ k ]} \left| c _ {j} \right| \cdot \left\| \mathbf {U} ^ {- 1} \delta^ {(j)} \right\| _ {\phi^ {\prime \prime} (v)} \\ \cdot \left(\left[ \exp \left(\frac {1}{1 1 \lambda}\right) \frac {1 3}{1 2} \right] ^ {2} + \left[ \exp \left(\frac {1}{1 1 \lambda}\right) \frac {1 3}{1 2} \right]\right) \exp \left(\frac {1}{1 1 \lambda}\right) \exp (0. 1 1) \\ \end{array}
$$

and the result follows from  $\lambda \geq 1$  and  $f(1) = f(0) + f'(0) + \frac{1}{2} f''(\zeta)$  for some  $\zeta \in [0,1]$ .

![](images/3c99bc3875740ac2117daf4b1001e8a9af5ca20c63c849865fb741cf74ded3da.jpg)

Lemma 4.35 (Lemma 54 of [LS19]). For all  $x \in \mathbb{R}^m$ , we have

$$
e ^ {\lambda \| x \| _ {\infty}} \leq \Phi (x) \leq 2 m e ^ {\lambda \| x \| _ {\infty}} \quad a n d \quad \lambda \Phi (x) - 2 \lambda m \leq \| \nabla \Phi (x) \| _ {1} \tag {46}
$$

Furthermore, for any symmetric convex set  $U \subseteq \mathbb{R}^m$  and any  $x \in \mathbb{R}^m$ , let  $x^\flat \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \operatorname{argmax}_{y \in U} \langle x, y \rangle$  and  $\|x\|_U \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \max_{y \in U} \langle x, y \rangle$ . Then for all  $x, y \in \mathbb{R}^m$  with  $\|x - y\|_\infty \leq \delta \leq \frac{1}{5\lambda}$  we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} e ^ {- \lambda \delta} \| \nabla \Phi (y) \| _ {U} - \lambda \| \nabla \Phi (y) ^ {\flat} \| _ {1} \leq \left\langle \nabla \Phi (x), \nabla \Phi (y) ^ {\flat} \right\rangle \\ \leq e ^ {\lambda \delta} \| \nabla \Phi (y) \| _ {U} + \lambda e ^ {\lambda \delta} \| \nabla \Phi (y) ^ {b} \| _ {1}. \tag {47} \\ \end{array}
$$

If additionally  $U$  is contained in a  $\ell_{\infty}$  ball of radius  $R$  then

$$
e ^ {- \lambda \delta} \| \nabla \Phi (y) \| _ {U} - \lambda m R \leq \| \nabla \Phi (x) \| _ {U} \leq e ^ {\lambda \delta} \| \nabla \Phi (y) \| _ {U} + \lambda e ^ {\lambda \delta} m R. \tag {48}
$$

Lemma 4.36. Let  $U \subset \mathbb{R}^m$  be an axis-symmetric convex set<sup>10</sup> which is contained in the  $\ell_{\infty}$  ball of radius 1 and contains the  $\ell_{\infty}$  ball of radius  $u \leq 1$ , i.e.  $x \in U$  implies  $\|x\|_{\infty} \leq 1$  and for some  $\|x\|_{\infty} \leq u$  implies  $x \in U$ . Let  $w, v \in \mathbb{R}^m$  such that  $\|w - v\|_{\infty} \leq \delta \leq \frac{1}{5\lambda}$ . Let  $g = -\gamma \operatorname{argmax}_{z \in U} \langle \nabla \Phi(v), z \rangle$  and  $\|h\|_U \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \max_{z \in U} \langle h, z \rangle$  with  $\gamma \geq 0$  and

$$
\delta_ {\Phi} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \phi^ {\prime} (w) ^ {\top} g + c _ {1} \gamma \| \phi^ {\prime} (w) \| _ {U} + c _ {2} \gamma^ {2} \| \phi^ {\prime \prime} (w) \| _ {U}
$$

for some  $c_{1}, c_{2} \geq 0$  satisfying  $\lambda \gamma \leq \frac{1}{8}$ ,  $2\lambda \delta + c_{2}\lambda \gamma \leq 0.5(1 - c_{1})$ . Then,

$$
\delta_ {\Phi} \leq - 0. 5 (1 - c _ {1}) \lambda \gamma u \Phi (w) + m
$$

Proof. Since entrywise  $|\phi''(w)| \leq \lambda \cdot (|\phi'(w)| + 2\lambda)$  by Lemma 4.33, we have that

$$
\| \phi^ {\prime \prime} (w) \| _ {U} \leq \lambda \| \phi^ {\prime} (w) \| _ {U} + 2 \lambda^ {2} \| 1 \| _ {U} \leq \lambda \| \phi^ {\prime} (w) \| _ {U} + 2 \lambda^ {2} m
$$

where in the first step we used triangle inequality and the axis-symmetry of  $U$  and in the second step we used that  $U$  is contained in a  $\ell_{\infty}$  ball of radius 1.

Now, we bound the term  $\phi'(w)^\top g$ . Applying (47) and (48) in Lemma 4.35, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {- \phi^ {\prime} (w) ^ {\top} g}{\gamma} \geq e ^ {- \lambda \delta} \| \phi^ {\prime} (v) \| _ {U} - \lambda \| g \| _ {1} (47) \\ \geq e ^ {- 2 \lambda \delta} \| \phi^ {\prime} (w) \| _ {U} - e ^ {- \lambda \delta} \lambda m - \lambda \| g \| _ {1} (48) \\ \geq e ^ {- 2 \lambda \delta} \| \phi^ {\prime} (w) \| _ {U} - 2 \lambda m. \quad \left(U \text {i s c o n t a i n e d i n a b a l l o f r a d i u s} 1\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

Hence, as  $e^{-2\lambda \delta} \geq 1 - 2\lambda$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \delta_ {\Phi} \leq - (1 - 2 \lambda \delta - c _ {1} - c _ {2} \lambda \gamma) \gamma \| \phi^ {\prime} (w) \| _ {U} + 2 \lambda m \gamma + 2 c _ {2} \gamma^ {2} \lambda^ {2} m \\ \leq - 0. 5 (1 - c _ {1}) \gamma \| \phi^ {\prime} (w) \| _ {U} + m / 2 \\ \end{array}
$$

where we used  $\lambda \gamma \leq 1/8$  and  $2\lambda \delta + c_{2}\lambda \gamma \leq \frac{1 - c_{1}}{2}$ . Since  $U$  contains the  $\ell_{\infty}$  ball of radius  $u$ , we have  $\| \phi'(w) \|_U \geq u \| \phi'(w) \|_1$ . Using this and (46) in Lemma 4.35 yields

$$
\begin{array}{l} \delta_ {\Phi} \leq - 0. 5 (1 - c _ {1}) \gamma u \| \phi^ {\prime} (w) \| _ {1} + 0. 5 m \\ \leq - 0. 5 \left(1 - c _ {1}\right) \lambda \gamma u \Phi (w) + \lambda \gamma m u + 0. 5 m \\ \end{array}
$$

and the result follows from the facts that  $\lambda \gamma \leq 1 / 8$  and  $u\leq 1$

10 A set  $U\subset \mathbb{R}^m$  is axis-symmetric if  $x\in U$  implies  $y\in U$  for all  $y\in \mathbb{R}^m$  with  $y_{i}\in \{-x_{i},x_{i}\}$  for all  $i\in [m]$ .

# 4.5 Additional Properties of our IPMs

Here we provide additional properties of our IPMs. First, we show that although the  $x$  iterates of Algorithm 3 may change more than in [BLSS20] in a single step due to sampling, they do not drift too far away from a more stable sequence of points. We use this lemma to efficiently maintain approximations to regularized leverage scores, which are critical for this method.

Lemma 4.37. Suppose that  $q_{i} \geq \frac{m}{n} \frac{T}{\beta^{2}} \log(mT) \cdot ((\delta_{r})_{i}^{2} / \| \delta_{r} \|_{2}^{2} + 1/m)$  for all  $i \in [m]$  where  $\beta \in (0,0.1)$  and  $T \geq \sqrt{n}$ . Let  $(x^{(k)}, s^{(k)})$  be the sequence of points found by Algorithm 3. With probability  $1 - m^{-10}$ , there is a sequence of points  $\hat{x}^{(k)}$  from  $1 \leq k \leq T$  such that

1.  $\widehat{x}^{(k)}\approx_{\beta}x^{(k)}$  
2.  $\| (\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{(k)})^{-1}(\widehat{x}^{(k + 1)} - \widehat{x}^{(k)})\|_{\tau (\widehat{x}^{(k)},s^{(k)})}\leq 5\gamma ,$  
3.  $\| \mathbf{T}(\widehat{x}^{(k)},s^{(k)})^{-1}(\tau (\widehat{x}^{(k + 1)},s^{(k + 1)}) - \tau (\widehat{x}^{(k)},s^{(k)}))\|_{\tau (\widehat{x}^{(k)},s^{(k)})}\leq 80\gamma .$

Proof. We define the sequence  $\widehat{x}^{(k)}$  recursively by  $\widehat{x}^{(1)} = x^{(1)}$  and

$$
\ln \hat {x} ^ {(k)} \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \ln \hat {x} ^ {(k - 1)} + \mathbb {E} _ {k} [ \ln x ^ {(k)} ] - \ln x ^ {(k - 1)}
$$

where  $\mathbb{E}_k[\ln x^{(k)}]\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{R}_k}[\ln x^{(k)}|x^{(k - 1)}]$  where  $\mathbf{R}_k$  is the random choice of  $\mathbf{R}$  at the  $k$ -th iteration. Let  $\Delta^{(k)}\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\ln \hat{x}^{(k)} - \ln x^{(k)}$  and note that this implies that

$$
\Delta^ {(k)} = \Delta^ {(k - 1)} + \mathbb {E} _ {k} [ \ln x ^ {(k)} ] - \ln x ^ {(k)}. \tag {49}
$$

Consequently, is a martingale, i.e.  $\mathbb{E}_k[\Delta^{(k)}] = \Delta^{(k - 1)}$ . Since  $\hat{x}^{(1)} = x^{(1)}$ , we have  $\Delta^{(1)} = 0$  and consequently to bound  $\Delta^{(k)}$ , it suffices to show that  $\Delta^{(k)} - \Delta^{(k - 1)}$  is small in the worst case and that the variance of  $\Delta^{(k)} - \Delta^{(k - 1)}$  is small.

For the worst case bound, note that by (49) and Jensen's inequality

$$
\left| \Delta_ {i} ^ {(k)} - \Delta_ {i} ^ {(k - 1)} \right| = \left| \ln x _ {i} ^ {(k)} - \mathbb {E} _ {k} \ln x _ {i} ^ {(k)} \right| \leq \sup  _ {x ^ {(k)}, y ^ {(k)}} \left| \ln x _ {i} ^ {(k)} - \ln y _ {i} ^ {(k)} \right| \tag {50}
$$

where  $x_{i}^{(k)}$  and  $y_{i}^{(k)}$  are two independent samples using two different independent  $\mathbf{R}_k$  to take a step from  $x^{(k - 1)}$ . Lemma 4.19 shows that  $x^{(k)}\approx_{0.1}x^{(k - 1)}$  and  $y^{(k)}\approx_{0.1}x^{(k - 1)}$ . Consequently,  $x^{(k)}\approx_{0.2}y^{(k)}$ . Therefore, we have  $\left|\Delta_i^{(k)} - \Delta_i^{(k - 1)}\right|\leq \frac{3}{2}\frac{|x_i^{(k)} - y_i^{(k)}|}{x_i^{(k - 1)}}$ . Using that  $x^{(k)} - y^{(k)} = \overline{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{R}_{x,k}\delta_r - \overline{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{R}_{y,k}\delta_r$  (where  $\mathbf{R}_{x,k}$  and  $\mathbf{R}_{y,k}$  are two different independent draws of  $\mathbf{R}$  in iteration  $k$ ) and  $\overline{x}\approx_{0.1}x$ , we can simplify (50) to

$$
\left| \Delta_ {i} ^ {(k)} - \Delta_ {i} ^ {(k - 1)} \right| \leq 2 | (\mathbf {R} \delta_ {r}) _ {i} - (\mathbf {R} ^ {\prime} \delta_ {r}) _ {i} |.
$$

Recall that the  $i$ -th row of  $\delta_r$  is sampled with probability  $\widetilde{q}_i \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \min(q_i, 1)$ . If the  $i$ -th row is sampled with probability 1, then the difference is 0. Hence, we can only consider the case  $\widetilde{q}_i = q_i \geq \frac{m}{n} \frac{T}{\beta^2} \log(mT) \cdot ((\delta_r)_i^2 / \| \delta_r \|_2^2 + 1/m)$ . In this case, we have

$$
\left| \Delta_ {i} ^ {(k)} - \Delta_ {i} ^ {(k - 1)} \right| \leq \frac {2}{q _ {i}} | \delta_ {r} | _ {i} \leq \frac {2}{q _ {i}} | \delta_ {r} | _ {i} = \frac {2 | \delta_ {r} | _ {i}}{\frac {m}{n} \frac {T}{\beta^ {2}} \log (m T) \cdot ((\delta_ {r}) _ {i} ^ {2} / \| \delta_ {r} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + 1 / m)}.
$$

One can check the worst case is given by  $|\delta_r|_i = \|\delta_r\|_2 / \sqrt{m}$  and we get

$$
\left| \Delta_ {i} ^ {(k)} - \Delta_ {i} ^ {(k - 1)} \right| \leq \frac {2}{q _ {i}} | \delta_ {r} | _ {i} \leq \frac {2}{q _ {i}} | \delta_ {r} | _ {i} = \frac {\beta^ {2} n}{\sqrt {m} T \log (m T)} \| \delta_ {r} \| _ {2}
$$

$$
\leq M \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \frac {\sqrt {n} \gamma \beta^ {2}}{4 0 T \log (m T)}.
$$

where we used  $\tau(x^{(k)}, s^{(k)}) \geq \frac{n}{m}$  and  $\| \delta_r \|_{\tau(x^{(k)}, s^{(k)})} \leq \frac{\gamma}{40}$  (31) at the last sentence.

For the variance bound, note that variance of a random variable  $X$  can be written as  $\mathbb{E}(X - \mathbb{E}X)^2 = \frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}(X - Y)^2$  where  $Y$  is an independent sample from  $\mathbf{X}$ . Hence, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {E} _ {k} \left[ [ \Delta^ {(k)} - \Delta^ {(k - 1)} ] _ {i} ^ {2} \right] = \mathbb {E} _ {k} \left[ [ \ln x ^ {(k)} - \mathbb {E} _ {k} \ln x ^ {(k)} ] _ {i} ^ {2} \right] = \frac {1}{2} \mathbb {E} _ {k} \left[ [ \ln x ^ {(k)} - \ln y ^ {(k)} ] _ {i} ^ {2} \right] \\ \leq \mathbb {E} _ {k} \left[ \frac {(x _ {i} ^ {(k)} - y _ {i} ^ {(k)}) ^ {2}}{(x _ {i} ^ {(k - 1)}) ^ {2}} \right] \leq 2 \mathbb {E} _ {k} \left[ (\mathbf {R} _ {x, k} \delta_ {r} - \mathbf {R} _ {y, k} \delta_ {r}) _ {i} ^ {2} \right] \\ \end{array}
$$

where we used that  $x^{(k)}\approx_{0.2}y^{(k)}$ ,  $x^{(k)} - y^{(k)} = \overline{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{R}_{x,k}\delta_r - \overline{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{R}_{y,k}\delta_r$  and  $\overline{x}\approx_{0.1}x$  as before at the last line. Again, if the  $i$ -th row sampled with probability 1, then the difference is 0. Hence, the last line is bounded by

$$
\mathbb {E} _ {k} \left[ [ \Delta^ {(k)} - \Delta^ {(k - 1)} ] _ {i} ^ {2} \right] \leq \frac {2}{q _ {i}} [ \delta_ {r} ] _ {i} ^ {2} \leq \frac {2 n \| \delta_ {r} \| _ {2} ^ {2}}{m T \log (m T)} \leq \sigma^ {2} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \frac {\gamma^ {2} \beta^ {2}}{1 0 0 T \log (m T)}
$$

where we used  $q_{i} \geq \frac{m}{n} \frac{T}{\beta^{2}} \log(mT) \cdot (\delta_{r})_{i}^{2} / \| \delta_{r} \|_{2}^{2}, \tau(x^{(k)}, s^{(k)}) \geq \frac{n}{m}$  and  $\| \delta_{r} \|_{\tau(x^{(k)}, s^{(k)})} \leq \frac{\gamma}{40}$  (31) at the end.

Now, we apply Bernstein inequality and get that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {P} (| \Delta_ {i} ^ {(k)} | \leq \beta \mathrm {f o r a n y} k \in [ T ]) \leq 2 T \exp (- \frac {\beta^ {2} / 2}{\sigma^ {2} T + \frac {1}{3} M \beta}) \\ = 2 T \exp \left(- \frac {\frac {1}{2} \beta^ {2}}{\frac {\gamma^ {2} \beta^ {2}}{1 0 0 T \log (m T)} T + \frac {1}{3} \cdot \frac {\sqrt {n} \gamma \beta^ {2}}{4 0 T \log (m T)} \cdot \beta}\right) \\ \leq 2 T \exp (- 2 5 \log (m T)) \leq \frac {1}{m ^ {1 0}}. \\ \end{array}
$$

This completes the proof of  $\widehat{x}^{(k)}\approx_{\beta}x^{(k)}$  for all  $k\leq T$

For the Property 2, we note that  $\widehat{x}^{(k + 1)}\approx_{0.1}\widehat{x}^{(k)}\approx_{0.1}x^{(k)}$  and hence

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| (\widehat {\mathbf {X}} ^ {(k)}) ^ {- 1} (\widehat {x} ^ {(k + 1)} - \widehat {x} ^ {(k)}) \| _ {\tau (\widehat {x} ^ {(k)}, s ^ {(k)})} \leq 2 \| \ln \widehat {x} ^ {(k + 1)} - \ln \widehat {x} ^ {(k)} \| _ {\tau (x ^ {(k)}, s ^ {(k)})} \\ = 2 \| \mathbb {E} \ln x ^ {(k + 1)} - \ln x ^ {(k)} \| _ {\tau (x ^ {(k)}, s ^ {(k)})}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Using that  $x^{(k + 1)}\approx_{0.1}x^{(k)}$  , we have

$$
\ln x _ {i} ^ {(k + 1)} = \ln x _ {i} ^ {(k)} + ((\mathbf {X} ^ {(k)}) ^ {- 1} (x ^ {(k + 1)} - x ^ {(k)})) _ {i} \pm ((\mathbf {X} ^ {(k)}) ^ {- 1} (x ^ {(k + 1)} - x ^ {(k)})) _ {i} ^ {2}
$$

for all  $i$  and hence

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \left(\widehat {\mathbf {X}} ^ {(k)}\right) ^ {- 1} \left(\widehat {x} ^ {(k + 1)} - \widehat {x} ^ {(k)}\right) \right\| _ {\tau \left(\widehat {x} ^ {(k)}, s ^ {(k)}\right)} \\ \leq 2 \| (\mathbf {X} ^ {(k)}) ^ {- 1} (\mathbb {E} x ^ {(k + 1)} - x ^ {(k)}) \| _ {\tau (x ^ {(k)}, s ^ {(k)})} + 2 \| \mathbb {E} ((\mathbf {X} ^ {(k)}) ^ {- 1} (x ^ {(k + 1)} - x ^ {(k)})) ^ {2} \| _ {\tau (x ^ {(k)}, s ^ {(k)})}. \\ \end{array}
$$

Lemma 4.18 shows that the first term is bounded by  $2\gamma$  and Lemma 4.19 shows that the second term bounded by  $12\gamma^2$ . Hence, the whole term is bounded by  $5\gamma$ .

For the Property 3, we let  $\delta_{\tau} = \tau (\widehat{x}^{(k + 1)},s^{(k + 1)}) - \tau (\widehat{x}^{(k)},s^{(k)})$ . By Lemma 4.21, we have

$$
\delta_ {\tau} = \boldsymbol {\Lambda} (1 - 2 \alpha) \widehat {\mathbf {X}} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {\widehat {x}} + 6 \eta
$$

where  $\Lambda = \Lambda (\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{1 / 2 - \alpha}\mathbf{S}^{-1 / 2 - \alpha}\mathbf{A})$  and

$$
\left| \eta_ {i} \right| \leq \sigma_ {i} \left(e _ {i} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {P} ^ {(2)} \widehat {\mathbf {X}} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {\widehat {x}}\right) ^ {2} + e _ {i} ^ {\top} \left(\boldsymbol {\Sigma} + \mathbf {P} ^ {(2)}\right) \left(\widehat {\mathbf {X}} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {\widehat {x}}\right) ^ {2}.
$$

Using  $\| \mathbf{T}^{-1}\mathbf{P}^{(2)}\|_{\tau}\leq 1$ $\| \mathbf{T}^{-1}\pmb {\Lambda}\|_{\tau}\leq 2$  and  $\| \mathbf{T}^{-1}\mathbf{P}^{(2)}\|_{\infty}\leq 1$  (Lemma 4.25), we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \mathbf {T} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {\tau} \| _ {\tau} \leq \| \mathbf {T} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\Lambda} \widehat {\mathbf {X}} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {\widehat {x}} \| _ {\tau} + 6 \| \mathbf {T} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {- 1} \left(\mathbf {P} ^ {(2)} \widehat {\mathbf {X}} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {\widehat {x}}\right) ^ {2} \| _ {\tau} + 6 \| \mathbf {T} ^ {- 1} (\boldsymbol {\Sigma} + \mathbf {P} ^ {(2)}) \left(\widehat {\mathbf {X}} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {\widehat {x}}\right) ^ {2} \| _ {\tau} \\ \leq 2 \| \widehat {\mathbf {X}} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {\widehat {x}} \| _ {\tau} + 6 \| (\mathbf {T} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {P} ^ {(2)} \widehat {\mathbf {X}} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {\widehat {x}}) ^ {2} \| _ {\tau} + 1 2 \| (\widehat {\mathbf {X}} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {\widehat {x}}) ^ {2} \| _ {\tau} \\ \leq 1 4 \left\| \widehat {\mathbf {X}} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {\widehat {x}} \right\| _ {\tau} + 6 \left\| \mathbf {T} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {P} ^ {(2)} \widehat {\mathbf {X}} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {\widehat {x}} \right\| _ {\infty} \cdot \left\| \mathbf {T} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {P} ^ {(2)} \widehat {\mathbf {X}} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {\widehat {x}} \right\| _ {\tau} \\ \leq 1 4 \left\| \widehat {\mathbf {X}} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {\widehat {x}} \right\| _ {\tau} + 6 \left\| \widehat {\mathbf {X}} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {\widehat {x}} \right\| _ {\infty} \cdot \left\| \widehat {\mathbf {X}} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {\widehat {x}} \right\| _ {\tau} \\ \leq 1 4 \cdot 5 \gamma + 6 \cdot \frac {1}{3} \cdot 5 \gamma \\ \end{array}
$$

where we used  $\| \widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{-1}\delta_{\widehat{x}}\|_{\tau}\leq 5\gamma$  (property 2) and  $\| \widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{-1}\delta_{\widehat{x}}\|_{\infty}\leq \frac{1}{3}$  (since  $x^{(k)}\approx_{0.1}x^{(k - 1)}$ ).

![](images/9039e13c86dd6d65a94f8b6c80bec39eeef0af9f09a414cb819052bd59f6a130.jpg)

Our IPM maintains a point  $x$  that is nearly feasible, that is a point with small norm  $\| \mathbf{A}^\top x - b \|_{(\mathbf{A}^\top \mathbf{X}\mathbf{S}^{-1}\mathbf{A})^{-1}}^2$ . We now show that there is a truly feasible  $x'$  with  $x_i' \approx x_i$  for all  $i$ .

Lemma 4.38. Let  $(x,s)$  be a primal dual pair with  $xs\approx_1\tau (x,s)\cdot \mu$  for  $\mu >0$ , where  $x$  is not necessarily feasible. Let  $\mathbf{H}^{-1}\approx_{\delta}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{S}^{-1}\mathbf{A})^{-1}$  for some  $\delta \in [0,1 / 2]$ . Then the point

$$
x ^ {\prime} := x + \mathbf {X S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A H} ^ {- 1} (b - \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x)
$$

satisfies both

$$
\left\| \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x ^ {\prime} - b \right\| _ {(\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1}} ^ {2} \leq 5 \delta \cdot \left\| \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x - b \right\| _ {(\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1}} ^ {2}, a n d \tag {51}
$$

$$
\left\| \mathbf {X} ^ {- 1} \left(x - x ^ {\prime}\right) \right\| _ {\infty} ^ {2} \leq \frac {5}{\mu} \| \mathbf {A} x - b \| _ {\left(\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}\right) ^ {- 1}} ^ {2}. \tag {52}
$$

Proof. Let  $\delta_x = \mathbf{X}\mathbf{S}^{-1}\mathbf{A}(b - \mathbf{A}^\top x)$ . We start by proving (51).

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x ^ {\prime} - b \right\| _ {(\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1}} ^ {2} = \left\| \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} (x + \delta_ {x}) - b \right\| _ {(\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1}} ^ {2} \\ = \| (\mathbf {I} - \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X} \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A} \mathbf {H} ^ {- 1}) (\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x - b) \| _ {(\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X} \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1}} ^ {2} \\ \end{array}
$$

By using  $\mathbf{H}^{-1}\approx_{\delta}(\mathbf{AXS}^{-1}\mathbf{A})^{-1}$  we can bound

$$
\begin{array}{l} (\mathbf {I} - \mathbf {H} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X} \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) (\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X} \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1} (\mathbf {I} - \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X} \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A} \mathbf {H} ^ {- 1}) \\ = \left(\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X} \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}\right) ^ {- 1} - 2 \mathbf {H} ^ {- 1} + \mathbf {H} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X} \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A} \mathbf {H} ^ {- 1} \\ \prec (1 - 2 \exp (- \delta) + \exp (2 \delta)) \cdot (\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X} \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1} \\ \prec 5 \delta (\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X} \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1}. \\ \end{array}
$$

This then implies

$$
\left\| \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x ^ {\prime} - b \right\| _ {(\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1}} ^ {2} \leq 5 \delta \left\| \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x - b \right\| _ {(\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1}},
$$

so we have proven (51). Next, we prove (52).

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| \mathbf {X} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {x} \| _ {\infty} = \max  _ {i \in [ m ]} \| e _ {i} ^ {\top} \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A} \mathbf {H} ^ {- 1} (b - \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x) \| _ {\infty} \\ \leq \max _ {i \in [ m ]} \| e _ {i} ^ {\top} \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A} \mathbf {H} ^ {- 1} (b - \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x) \| _ {2} \\ \leq \max  _ {i \in [ m ]} \| e _ {i} ^ {\top} \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A} \mathbf {H} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {2} \| \mathbf {H} ^ {- 1 / 2} (b - \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x) \| _ {2} \\ \leq \exp (2 \delta) \cdot \max _ {i \in [ m ]} \| e _ {i} ^ {\top} \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A} (\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X} \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {2} \| b - \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x \| _ {(\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X} \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1}}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the third step uses Cauchy-Schwarz and the last step the approximation guarantee of  $\mathbf{H}^{-1}$ . The first norm of above term can be bounded by

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| e _ {i} ^ {\top} \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A} (\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X} \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1 / 2} \| _ {2} ^ {2} = e _ {i} ^ {\top} \frac {1}{(\mathbf {X S}) ^ {1 / 2}} \frac {\mathbf {X} ^ {1 / 2}}{\mathbf {S} ^ {1 / 2}} \mathbf {A} (\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X} \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \frac {\mathbf {X} ^ {1 / 2}}{\mathbf {S} ^ {1 / 2}} \frac {1}{(\mathbf {X S}) ^ {1 / 2}} e _ {i} \\ = \frac {\sigma (\frac {\mathbf {X} ^ {1 / 2}}{\mathbf {S} ^ {1 / 2}}) _ {i}}{x _ {i} s _ {i}} \leq \frac {\sigma (\frac {\mathbf {X} ^ {1 / 2 - \alpha}}{\mathbf {S} ^ {1 / 2 + \alpha}}) _ {i}}{x _ {i} s _ {i}} \leq \frac {\tau (x , s)}{x _ {i} s _ {i}} \leq \frac {3}{\mu}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where the last step uses  $xs \approx_1 \mu \cdot \tau(x, s)$  and  $\sigma(x, s) \leq \tau(x, s)$  for both possible choices of  $\tau$  (i.e. the IPM of Section 4.2 uses  $\tau(x, s) = 1$  and Section 4.3 uses  $\tau(x, s) = \sigma(x, s) + n / m$ ). By using  $\delta \leq 1/2$  we then obtain

$$
\left\| \mathbf {X} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {x} \right\| _ {\infty} ^ {2} \leq \frac {5}{\mu} \cdot \left\| \mathbf {A} x - b \right\| _ {\left(\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X} \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}\right) ^ {- 1}} ^ {2}.
$$

![](images/2ed23c3a011553f1ab3cb497aed44548d6518409efd4c0b6829710d3c952dde7.jpg)

In the remaining of this section, we bound how much the iterates of our IPMs move from the initial point. Our proof relies on bounds on how fast the center of a weighted self-concordant barrier moves. In particular we use the following lemma from [LS19] specialized to the weighted log barrier. $^{11}$

Lemma 4.39 (Special Case of [LS19] Lemma 67). For arbitrary  $\mathbf{A}_1 \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times m}$ ,  $b_1 \in \mathbb{R}^m$ ,  $\mathbf{A}_2 \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times k}$ ,  $b_2 \in \mathbb{R}^k$ ,  $b_3 \in \mathbb{R}^n$  and all  $w \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^k$ , let  $\Omega = \{x \in \mathbb{R}^n : \mathbf{A}_1^\top x = b_1, \mathbf{A}_2^\top x > b_2\}$  let

$$
x _ {w} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \arg \min  _ {x \in \Omega} b _ {3} ^ {\top} x - \sum_ {i \in [ k ]} w _ {i} \log ([ \mathbf {A} _ {2} ^ {\top} x - b _ {2} ] _ {i}).
$$

For all  $w^{(0)}, w^{(1)} \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^k$ , it holds that  $x_{w^{(0)}} + t(x_{w^{(1)}} - x_{w^{(0)}}) \in \Omega$  for all  $t \in (-\gamma, 1 + \gamma)$  where

$$
\gamma = \frac {\theta^ {2}}{1 + 2 \theta} a n d \theta = \frac {\min _ {i \in [ k ]} \min \{w _ {i} ^ {(0)} , w _ {i} ^ {(1)} \}}{\sum_ {i \in [ k ]} | w _ {i} ^ {(0)} - w _ {i} ^ {(1)} |}.
$$

Using the lemma above, we can bound how the fast primal-dual central path moves by applying the lemma on both primal and dual separately.

Lemma 4.40. Fix arbitrary non-degenerate  $\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ ,  $b \in \mathbb{R}^n$ , and  $c \in \mathbb{R}^m$ . For all  $w \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^m$ , we define  $(x_w, s_w) \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2m}$  be the unique vector such that  $\mathbf{X}_w s_w = w$ ,  $\mathbf{A}^\top x_w = b$ ,  $\mathbf{A} y + s_w = c$  for some  $y$ . For any  $w^{(0)}, w^{(1)} \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^m$ , we have that entrywise

$$
\frac {1}{\kappa} x _ {w ^ {(1)}} \leq x _ {w ^ {(0)}} \leq \kappa x _ {w ^ {(1)}} a n d \frac {1}{\kappa} s _ {w ^ {(1)}} \leq s _ {w ^ {(0)}}, \leq \kappa s _ {w ^ {(1)}}
$$

where  $\kappa = 4(W_{1} / W_{\mathrm{min}})^{2}$  for  $W_{1} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \| w^{(0)} \|_{1} + \| w^{(1)} \|_{1}$  and  $W_{\mathrm{min}} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \min_{i \in [k]} \min \{w_{i}^{(0)}, w_{i}^{(1)}\}$ .

Proof. Apply Lemma 4.39 with  $\mathbf{A}_1 = \mathbf{A}$ ,  $b_1 = b$ ,  $\mathbf{A}_2 = \mathbf{I}$ ,  $b_2 = 0$  and  $b_3 = c$  so that  $x_w$  is as defined in Lemma 4.39. Lemma 4.39 implies that  $x_{w^{(0)}} + t(x_{w^{(1)}} - x_{w^{(0)}}) > 0$  for all  $t \in (-\gamma, 1 + \gamma)$  with  $\gamma$  and  $\theta$  defined as in Lemma 4.39. Consequently,  $\frac{1 + \gamma}{\gamma} x_{w^{(1)}} \geq x_{w^{(0)}} \geq \frac{\gamma}{1 + \gamma} x_{w^{(1)}}$ . Now,  $\theta \geq \frac{W_{\min}}{W_1}$  as  $|w_i^{(0)} - w_i^{(1)}| \leq |w_i^{(0)}| + |w_i^{(1)}|$ . Since  $\theta^2 / (1 + 2\theta)$  increases monotonically for positive  $\theta$  and  $\frac{W_{\min}}{W_1} \leq 1$  we have  $\gamma \geq \frac{1}{3} (\frac{W_{\min}}{W_1})^2$ . Similarly, since  $\gamma / (1 + \gamma)$  increases monotonically for positive  $\gamma$  and  $\frac{1}{3} (\frac{W_{\min}}{W_1})^2 \leq \frac{1}{3}$  we have  $\frac{\gamma}{1 + \gamma} \geq \frac{1}{4} (\frac{W_{\min}}{W_1}) \geq \frac{1}{\kappa}$ . Thus,  $\frac{1}{\kappa} x_{w^{(0)}} \leq x_{w^{(1)}} \leq \kappa x_{w^{(0)}}$ .

Similarly, to bound  $s_{w^{(0)}}$  and  $s_{w^{(1)}}$  we apply Lemma 4.39 with  $\mathbf{A}_1 = 0$ ,  $b_1 = 0$ ,  $\mathbf{A}_2 = \mathbf{A}^\top$ ,  $b_2 = c$  and  $b_3 = b$  so that  $s_w$  is as defined in Lemma 4.39. Lemma 4.39 implies that  $s_{w^{(0)}} + t(s_{w^{(1)}} - s_{w^{(0)}}) > 0$  for all  $t \in (-\gamma, 1 + \gamma)$ . Since this is the same statement that was shown for  $x_{w^{(0)}}$  and  $x_{w^{(1)}}$  in the preceding paragraph we also have  $\frac{1}{\kappa} s_{w^{(0)}} \leq s_{w^{(1)}} \leq \kappa s_{w^{(0)}}$ .

This allows us to bound the number of bits of precision required to appropriately represent all  $x, s$  as they occur throughout Algorithm 1, our main path following routine.

Lemma 4.41. Let  $x^{(\mathrm{init})}, s^{(\mathrm{init})}, \mu^{(\mathrm{init})}, \mu^{(\mathrm{end})}$  be the initial parameters for Algorithm 1 as in Lemma 4.4. Let  $W$  be a bound on the ratio of largest to smallest entry in both  $x^{(\mathrm{init})}$  and  $s^{(\mathrm{init})}$ . Let  $W'$  be a bound on the ratio of largest to smallest entry for all  $x$  and  $s$  at each Line 7 and the algorithm's termination. Then  $\log W' = \widetilde{O}(\log W + |\log \mu^{(\mathrm{init})} / \mu^{(\mathrm{end})}|)$  for  $\tau = \tau_{\mathrm{LS}}$  and  $\tau = \tau_{\mathrm{log}}$ .

Proof. Let  $(x,s,\mu)$  be the state of the algorithm at either Line 7 or the algorithm's termination. Lemma 4.4 implies that  $(x,s,\mu)$  is  $\epsilon$ -centered. Consequently,  $\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu}}\|\mathbf{A}^\top x - b\|_{(\mathbf{AXS}^{-1}\mathbf{A})^{-1}} = O(1)$ . By Lemma 4.38 this implies there is a feasible  $x'$  with  $x \approx_{O(1)} x'$  where for  $w \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} x's$  and  $x_w, s_w$  as defined in Lemma 4.40 we have  $x_w = x'$  and  $s_w = w$ . Further, the definition of  $\epsilon$ -centered implies that  $w = x's \approx_{O(1)} xs \approx_{O(1)} \mu \tau(x,s)$ . Now, for  $\tau(x,s) = \tau_{\log}(x,s) = \vec{1}$  we have

$$
\mu \cdot \tau (x, s) \geq \min \{\mu^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})}, \mu^ {(\mathrm {e n d})} \} \mathrm {a n d} \| \mu \cdot \tau (x, s) \| _ {1} \leq m \cdot \max \{\mu^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})}, \mu^ {(\mathrm {e n d})} \}.
$$

and for  $\tau (x,s) = \tau_{\mathrm{LS}}(x,s) = \sigma (x,s) + \frac{n}{m}\vec{1}$  we have

$$
\mu \cdot \tau (x, s) \geq \frac {n}{m} \cdot \min  \{\mu^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})}, \mu^ {(\mathrm {e n d})} \} \text {a n d} \| \mu \cdot \tau (x, s) \| _ {1} \leq 2 n \cdot \max  \{\mu^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})}, \mu^ {(\mathrm {e n d})} \}.
$$

In either case, applying Lemma 4.40 with

$$
\kappa = O \left(\left(m \cdot \frac {\max \{\mu^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})} , \mu^ {(\mathrm {e n d})} \}}{\min \{\mu^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})} , \mu^ {(\mathrm {e n d})} \}}\right) ^ {2}\right) = O \left(\left(m \cdot \max \left\{\frac {\mu^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})}}{\mu^ {(\mathrm {e n d})}}, \frac {\mu^ {(\mathrm {e n d})}}{\mu^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})}} \right\}\right) ^ {2}\right)
$$

yields that

$$
\frac {1}{\kappa} x _ {w ^ {\mathrm {(i n i t)}}} \leq x _ {w} \leq \kappa x _ {w ^ {\mathrm {(i n i t)}}} \text {a n d} \frac {1}{\kappa} s _ {w ^ {\mathrm {(i n i t)}}} \leq s _ {w} \leq \kappa s _ {w ^ {\mathrm {(i n i t)}}}.
$$

Since the largest ratio of the entries in  $x^{(\mathrm{init})}$  and  $s^{(\mathrm{init})}$  is bounded by  $W$  this implies that  $W'$  with  $\log W' = \widetilde{O} (\log W + |\log \mu^{(\mathrm{init})} / \mu^{(\mathrm{end})}|)$  is an upper bound on the largest ratio of entries in  $x$  and  $s$  as desired.

# 5 Heavy Hitters

This section concerns maintaining information about a matrix-vector product  $\mathbf{A}h$  for an incidence matrix  $\mathbf{A}\in \{-1,0,1\}^{m\times n}$  undergoing row scaling. Formally, let  $g\in \mathbb{R}^m$ . Note that  $\operatorname {Diag}(g)\mathbf{A}$  is the matrix obtained by scaling the  $i^{th}$  row of  $\mathbf{A}$  by a factor of  $g_{i}$ . We want to be able to update entries of  $g$  and compute certain information about  $\operatorname {Diag}(g)\mathbf{A}h$  for query vector  $h\in \mathbb{R}^n$ . The data structure constructed in this section will be used in Section 6 to create a data structure for efficiently maintaining the slack of the dual solution inside our IPM method.

If  $\mathbf{A}$  is an incidence matrix representing some graph  $G$ , then  $\mathbf{Diag}(g)\mathbf{A}h \in \mathbb{R}^m$  can be viewed as a vector of values of edges, defined as follows. View  $h \in \mathbb{R}^n$  as a vector of potentials on nodes and view  $g \in \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^m$  as a vector of edge weights (node  $v \in [n]$  has potential  $h_v$  and edge  $e \in [m]$  has weight  $g_e$ ). Define the value of each edge  $e = \{u, v\}$  as  $|(D_{\mathrm{diag}}(g)\mathbf{A}h)_e| = g_e|h(v) - h(u)|$ . We would like to maintain certain information about the edge values when the potential and edge weights change.

The data structure of this section (Lemma 5.1) can report edges with large absolute values, i.e. large  $|(\mathbf{Diag}(g)\mathbf{A}h)_e|$ . It supports updating  $g_{e}$  for some  $e\in [m]$  (SCALE operation). Moreover, given  $h\in \mathbb{R}^n$  and  $\epsilon \geq 0$ , it reports all edges (equivalently all entries in  $\mathbf{Diag}(g)\mathbf{A}h$ ) with values at least  $\epsilon$  (HEAVYQUERY operation). Additionally, it can sample each edge with probability roughly proportional to its value square (SAMPLE operation). Thus, each edge  $e = \{u,v\}$  is selected with probability roughly  $\frac{(g_e(h_u - h_v))^2}{\|\mathbf{Diag}(g)\mathbf{A}h\|_2^2}$ .

Our data structure requires near-linear preprocessing time and polylogarithmic time per SCALE and SAMPLE operation. It takes roughly  $O(\| \mathbf{Diag}(g)\mathbf{A}h\|_2^2\epsilon^{-2} + n)$  time to answer a query. (To make sense of this time complexity, note that reading the input vector  $h$  takes  $O(n)$  time, and there can be at most and as many as  $\| \mathbf{Diag}(g)\mathbf{A}h\|_2^2\epsilon^{-2}$  edges reported: For any  $x_{1},x_{2},\ldots ,x_{m}$ , there can be at most and as many as  $(\sum_{i}x_{i}^{2}) / \epsilon^{2}$  indices  $i$  such that  $x_{i}\geq \epsilon$ .) Note that the data structure is randomized but holds against an adaptive adversary. It can be made deterministic at the cost of  $m^{1 + o(1)}$  preprocessing time and  $n^{o(1)}$  time per SCALE (as opposed to polylog(n)), using  $[\mathrm{CGL}^{+}20]$ .

We discuss our data structure to query all large entries (in terms of absolute value) of vector  $\mathbf{Diag}(g)\mathbf{A}h$  and to sample entries of the vector proportional to the squared values. Formally, our data structure has the following guarantees.

Lemma 5.1 (HEAVYHITTER). There exists a data structure PROJECTIONHEAVYHITTER that supports the following operations.

- INITIALIZER  $(\mathbf{A} \in \{-1,0,1\}^{m \times n}, g \in \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m})$ : The data structure is given the edge incidence matrix  $\mathbf{A}$  of a graph  $G$  and a scaling vector  $g$ . It initializes in  $O(m \log^9 n)$  time.  
-  $\text{SCALE}(i \in [m], s \geq 0)$ : Updates  $g_i \gets s$  in  $O(\log^{14} n)$  amortized time.  
- HEAVYQUERY  $(h\in \mathbb{R}^n,\epsilon \in \mathbb{R}_{>0})$  : With high probability, the data structure returns all  $e\in [m]$  such that  $|(\mathbf{Diag}(g)\mathbf{Ah})_e|\geq \epsilon$  in running time

$$
O (\| \mathbf {D i a g} (g) \mathbf {A} h \| _ {2} ^ {2} \epsilon^ {- 2} \log^ {8} n + n \log^ {2} n \log W),
$$

where  $W$  is the ratio of the largest to the smallest non-zero entries in  $g$ .

-  $\text{SAMPLE}(h \in \mathbb{R}^n, K \in \mathbb{R}_{>0})$ : With high probability, in  $O(K \log n + n \log^2 n \log W)$  time the data-structure returns independently sampled indices of  $\mathbf{Diag}(g) \mathbf{A} h \in \mathbb{R}^m$  (i.e., edges in graph  $G$ ), where each edge  $e = (u, v)$  is sampled with some probability  $q_e$  which is at least

$$
\min  \left\{K \cdot \frac {\left(g _ {e} \left(h _ {u} - h _ {v}\right)\right) ^ {2}}{1 6 \left\| \mathbf {D i a g} (g) \mathbf {A} h \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \log^ {8} n}, 1 \right\},
$$

and with high probability there are at most  $O(K \log n)$  entries returned.

-  $\text{PROBABILITY}(I \subset [m], h \in \mathbb{R}^n, K \in \mathbb{R}_{>0})$ : Given a subset of edges  $I$ , this procedure returns for every  $e \in I$  the probabilities  $q_e$  that  $e$  would be sampled in the procedure  $\text{SAMPLE}(h, K)$ . The running time is  $O(|I| + n\log^2 n\log W)$ .  
- LEVERAGESCORESAMPLE  $(K' \in \mathbb{R}_{>0})$ : With high probability, in  $O(K'n \log^{11} n \log W)$  time LEVERAGESCORESAMPLE returns a set of sampled edges where every edge  $e$  is included independently with probability  $p_e \geq K' \cdot \sigma(\mathbf{Diag}(g)\mathbf{A})_e$ , and there are at most  $O(K'n \log^{11} n \log W)$  entries returned.  
- LEVERAGESCOREBOUND  $(K', I \subset [m])$ : Given a subset of edges  $I$ , this procedure returns for every  $e \in I$  the probabilities  $p_e$  that  $e$  would be sampled in the procedure LEVERAGESCORESAMPLE  $(K')$ . The running time is  $O(|I|)$ .

Our data structure exploits a classic spectral property of a graph, which is captured by the following simple known variant of Cheeger's inequality (see, e.g.  $\mathrm{[CGP^{+}18]}$  for its generalization).

Lemma 5.2. Let  $\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$  be an incidence matrix of an unweighted  $\phi$ -expander and let  $\mathbf{D} \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$  the degree matrix. Let  $\mathbf{L} = \mathbf{A}^{\top} \mathbf{A}$  be the corresponding Laplacian matrix, then for any  $y \in \mathbb{R}^n$  such that  $y \perp \mathbf{D}\vec{1}_n$ , we have

$$
y ^ {\top} \mathbf {L} y \geq 0. 5 \cdot y ^ {\top} \mathbf {D} y \cdot \phi^ {2}.
$$

Proof. Cheeger's inequality says that  $\frac{\phi^2}{2} \leq \lambda_2 (\mathbf{D}^{-1/2} \mathbf{L} \mathbf{D}^{-1/2})$  (see e.g. [ST11]). Since  $\mathbf{D}^{-1/2} \mathbf{L} \mathbf{D}^{-1/2}$  is PSD and  $\mathbf{D}^{1/2} \vec{1}$  is in its kernel, i.e. it is an eigenvector of value 0 we have

$$
\frac {\phi^ {2}}{2} \leq \lambda_ {2} (\mathbf {D} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {L D} ^ {- 1 / 2}) = \min _ {x \perp \mathbf {D} ^ {1 / 2} \vec {1}} \frac {x ^ {\top} \mathbf {D} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {L D} ^ {- 1 / 2} x}{x ^ {\top} x} = \min _ {y \perp \mathbf {D} \vec {1}} \frac {x ^ {\top} \mathbf {L} x}{x ^ {\top} \mathbf {D} x}.
$$

![](images/5a2708063738058bdc39e31391b3e247413ccc2348fccf02f80f29885db529a8.jpg)

To see the intuition of why we can efficiently implement the HEAVYQUERY and SAMPLE operations, consider for the simple case when there is no scaling (i.e.  $g = \vec{1}$ ) and the graph  $G$  is a  $\phi_G$ -expander. Since  $(\mathbf{A}h)_e = h_u - h_v$  for  $e = (u,v)$ , we can shift  $h$  by any constant vector without changing  $\mathbf{A}h$ , so we can shift  $h$  to make  $h\perp \mathbf{D}\vec{1}_n$ . For any edge  $e = (u,v)$  to have  $|h_u - h_v| \geq \epsilon$ , at least one of  $|h_u|$  and  $|h_v|$  has to be at least  $\epsilon /2$ . Thus, it suffices to check the adjacent edges of a node  $u$  only when  $|h_u|$  is large, or equivalently  $h_u^2\epsilon^{-2}$  is at least 1/4. Since checking the adjacent edges of any  $u$  takes time  $\deg (u)$ , the time over all such nodes is bounded by  $\sum_{u}\deg (u)h_u^2\epsilon^{-2}$ , which is  $O(h^{\top}\mathbf{D}h\epsilon^{-2})$ . By Lemma 5.2 this is  $O(h^{\top}\mathbf{L}h\phi_{G}^{-2}\epsilon^{-2})$ , and note  $h^\top \mathbf{L}h = \| \mathbf{A}h\| _2^2$ . The intuition behind SAMPLE is similar. Since we can approximate  $\| \mathbf{A}h\| _2^2$  (in the denominator of edge probabilities) within a factor of  $\phi_G^2$  by using  $h^\top \mathbf{D}h$ , and  $(h_u - h_v)^2$  (in the numerator) is at most  $2(h_u^2 +h_v^2)$ , this allows us to work mostly in the  $n$ -dimensional node space instead of the  $m$ -dimensional edge space.

The above discussion would give us the desired result when  $g$  is a constant vector and  $\phi_G$  is large, i.e.  $\Omega (\log^{-c}n)$  for some small constant  $c$ . To extend the argument to the general case with scaling vector  $g$  and graph of smaller conductance, we simply partition the edges of  $G$  to divide it into subgraphs, where each induced subgraph has roughly the same  $g$  values on its edges and large conductance. For the first part (i.e., roughly constant  $g$ ), we can bucket the edges by their  $g$  values and move edges between buckets when their  $g$  values get updated. To get subgraphs with large conductance, we utilize a dynamic expander decomposition algorithm stated in Lemma 5.3 as a blackbox to further partition the edges of each bucket into induced expanders and maintain these expanders dynamically as edges move between buckets. We use the dynamic expander decomposition described in [BBG+20] (building on tools developed for dynamic minimum spanning tree [SW19, CGL+20, NSW17, NS17, Wul17], especially [SW19]).

Lemma 5.3 (Dynamic expander decomposition,  $\left[\mathrm{BBG}^{+}20\right]$ ). For any  $\phi = O(1 / \log^4 n)$  there exists a dynamic algorithm against an adaptive adversary, that preprocesses an unweighted graph  $G$  with  $m$  edges in  $O(\phi^{-1}m\log^5 n)$  time (or  $O(1)$  time, if the graph is empty). The algorithm can then maintain<sup>12</sup> a decomposition of  $G$  into  $\phi$ -expanders  $G_1,\ldots,G_t$ , supporting both edge deletions and insertions to  $G$  in  $O(\phi^{-2}\log^6 n)$  amortized time. The subgraphs  $(G_i)_{1\leq i\leq t}$  partition the edges of  $G$ , and we have  $\sum_{i=1}^{t}|V(G_i)| = O(n\log^2 n)$ . The algorithm is randomized Monte-Carlo, i.e. with high probability every  $G_i$  is an expander.

Now we formally prove the guarantees of our data structure.

Proof of Lemma 5.1. We describe each of the operation independently.

Initialization. Let  $G = (V, E)$  be the graph corresponding to the given incidence matrix  $\mathbf{A}$  with weight  $g_{e}$  of each edge  $e$ . Partition the edges into subgraphs denoted as  $G_{i} = (V, E_{i})$ , where

$$
E _ {i} = \left\{e \mid g _ {e} \in \left[ 2 ^ {i}, 2 ^ {i + 1}\right) \right\} \tag {53}
$$

i.e. each  $G_{i}$  is an unweighted subgraph of  $G$  consisting of edges of roughly the same  $g_{e}$  values. Let  $G_{-\infty}$  be the subgraph induced by all zero-weight edges. Next, we choose  $\phi = 1 / \log^4 n$  and initialize the expander decomposition algorithm in Lemma 5.3 on each of the  $G_{i}$ , which results in  $\phi$ -expanders  $G_{i,1},\dots,G_{i,t_i}$  for each  $i$ . Note this can be implemented so the running time does not depend on the ratio  $W$  of largest and smallest non-zero entries in  $g$  if we only spend time on the non-empty  $G_{i}$ 's (and if a later update of  $g$  creates a new non-empty subgraph  $G_{i}$ , we attribute the time to initialize the expander decomposition of  $G_{i}$  to the SCALE operation. In total we spend  $O(m\log^9 n)$  time for the initialization since the average time for each edge is  $O(\log^9 n)$ .

Scale. Changing  $g_{e} \gets s$  means the edge weight of  $e$  is changed to  $s$ . Thus we may need to delete the given edge  $e$  from its current graph  $G_{i}$  and insert it into some other  $G_{i'}$ , so that (53) is maintained. By Lemma 5.3 it takes  $O(\log^{14} n)$  amortized time to update the expander decomposition of  $G_{i}$  and  $G_{i'}$ .

HeavyQuery. For every  $i \neq -\infty$  we iterate over each of the  $\phi$ -expander  $G_{i,j}$  and do the following. Let  $m'$  and  $n'$  denote the number of edges and nodes in  $G_{i,j}$  respectively. Let  $\mathbf{B}$  be the incidence matrix of  $G_{i,j}$ ; thus rows and columns of  $\mathbf{B}$  correspond to edges and nodes in  $G_{i,j}$ , respectively. To simplify notations, we pretend rows of  $\mathbf{B}$  are in  $\mathbb{R}^n$  instead of  $\mathbb{R}^{n'}$  (i.e. keep a ghost column even for nodes in  $G$  but not in  $G_{i,j}$ ).<sup>13</sup> Note that  $G_{i,j}$  is unweighted and each row of  $\mathbf{B}$  corresponds to an edge in  $G_{i,j}$  and also appears as a row in  $\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ ; thus  $\mathbf{B} \in \mathbb{R}^{m' \times n}$  for some  $m' \leq m$ . All  $m'$ -row/column (respectively  $n$ -row/column) matrices here have each row/column correspond to an edge (respectively node) in  $G_{i,j}$ , so we index them using edges  $e$  and nodes  $v$  in  $G_{i,j}$ ; for example, we refer to a row of  $\mathbf{B}$  as  $\mathbf{B}_e$  and an entry of  $h \in \mathbb{R}^n$  as  $h_v$ . To answer the query (finding all  $e$  such that  $|(D i a g(g)A h)_e| \geq \epsilon$ ), it suffices to find all edges  $e$  in  $G_{i,j}$  such that

$$
\left| (\operatorname {D i a g} (g) \mathbf {B} h) _ {e} \right| \geq \epsilon \tag {54}
$$

because rows of  $\mathbf{B}$  is a subset of rows of  $\mathbf{A}$ . Finding  $e$  satisfying (54) is done as follows. Step 1: Shift  $h$  so it is orthogonal to the degree vector of  $G_{i,j}$ :

$$
h ^ {\prime} \leftarrow h - \vec {1} _ {n} \cdot (\vec {1} _ {n} ^ {\top} \mathbf {D} h) / (\vec {1} _ {n} ^ {\top} \mathbf {D} \vec {1} _ {n}), \tag {55}
$$

where  $\mathbf{D} \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$  is the diagonal degree matrix with  $\mathbf{D}_{v,v} = \deg_{G_{i,j}}(v)$ .

Step 2: Let  $\delta = \epsilon /2^{i + 1}$ , and find all  $e$  with

$$
\left| \left(\mathbf {B} h ^ {\prime}\right) _ {e} \right| \geq \delta . \tag {56}
$$

as follows. For every node  $v$  with  $|h_v'| \geq 0.5\delta$ , find all edges  $e$  incident to  $v$  that satisfies (56). Among these edges, return those satisfying (54).

Correctness: To see we correctly return all edges satisfying (54), let  $e$  be any such edge, then we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} | (\mathbf {B} h ^ {\prime}) _ {e} | = | (\mathbf {B} h) _ {e} | \qquad \mathrm {(s i n c e} \mathbf {B} \vec {1} _ {n} = \vec {0} _ {m}) \\ \geq \epsilon / g _ {e} \quad (\text {s i n c e} | (\mathbf {D i a g} (g) \mathbf {B} h) _ {e} | = g _ {e} | (\mathbf {B} h) _ {e} |) \\ \geq \epsilon / 2 ^ {i + 1} = \delta \quad \text {(s i n c e} g _ {e} \leq 2 ^ {i + 1} \text {f o r e v e r y e d g e} e \text {i n} G _ {i, j}). \\ \end{array}
$$

Thus,  $e$  must satisfy (56). So if our algorithm discovers all edges satisfying (56), we will find all edges satisfying (54) as desired.

It is left to show that our algorithm actually discovers all edges satisfying (56). Note that an edge  $e = (u, v)$  satisfies (56) only if  $|h'(u)| \geq 0.5\delta$  or  $|h'(v)| \geq 0.5\delta$ : if  $|h'(u)| < 0.5\delta$  and  $|h'(v)| < 0.5\delta$ , then  $|(\mathbf{B}h')_e| = |h'(v) - h'(u)| \leq |h'(v)| + |h'(u)| < \delta$ . Since in step 2 we consider edges incident to every node  $u$  such that  $|h'(u)| > 0.5\delta$ , the algorithm discovers all edges satisfying (56).

Time Complexity: Step 1 (computing  $h^\prime$ ) can be easily implemented to take  $O(|V(G_{i,j})|)$  (see Footnote 13). For step 2 (finding  $e$  satisfying (56)), let  $\widehat{V} = \{v\in V(G_{i,j})\mid |h_v^{\prime}|\geq 0.5\delta \}$ , then the time we spend in step 2 is bounded by

$$
\begin{array}{l} O (\sum_ {v \in \widehat {V}} \deg_ {G _ {i, j}} (v)) = O (\sum_ {v \in \widehat {V}} \deg_ {G _ {i, j}} (v) (h _ {v} ^ {\prime}) ^ {2} / \delta^ {2}) \\ \leq O \left(\sum_ {v \in V (G _ {i, j})} \deg_ {G _ {i, j}} (v) \left(h _ {v} ^ {\prime}\right) ^ {2} / \delta^ {2}\right) \\ = O \left(\left(h ^ {\prime}\right) ^ {\top} \mathbf {D} h ^ {\prime} / \delta^ {2}\right). \tag {57} \\ \end{array}
$$

Above, the first equality is because  $(h_v')^2/\delta^2 \geq 0.25$  for every  $v \in \widehat{V}$ . To bound this time complexity further, observe that

$$
h ^ {\prime} \bot \mathbf {D} \vec {1} _ {n} \text {a n d} \| \mathbf {B} h \| _ {2} ^ {2} = \| \mathbf {B} h ^ {\prime} \| _ {2} ^ {2}, \tag {58}
$$

where the latter is because each row of the incidence matrix  $\mathbf{B}$  sums to zero, so  $\mathbf{B}h$  remains the same under constant shift. By Lemma 5.2,

$$
\| \mathbf {B} h \| _ {2} ^ {2} = \| \mathbf {B} h ^ {\prime} \| _ {2} ^ {2} = (\mathbf {B} h ^ {\prime}) ^ {\top} (\mathbf {B} h ^ {\prime}) = (h ^ {\prime}) ^ {\top} \mathbf {L} h ^ {\prime} \overset {\mathrm {L e m 5 . 2}} {\geq} (h ^ {\prime}) ^ {\top} \mathbf {D} h ^ {\prime} \phi^ {2},
$$

where here  $\mathbf{L} = \mathbf{B}^{\top}\mathbf{B}$  is the Laplacian matrix of  $G_{i,j}$ . Thus, the time complexity in (57) can be bounded by

$$
O \left(\| \mathbf {B} h \| _ {2} ^ {2} / \left(\phi^ {2} \delta^ {2}\right)\right) = O \left(\| \mathbf {B} h \| _ {2} ^ {2} \phi^ {- 2} 2 ^ {2 i} / \epsilon^ {2}\right).
$$

Summing the time complexity over all maintained expanders  $G_{i,j}$  with  $\mathbf{B}_{i,j}$  being the relevant incidence matrix  $\mathbf{B}^{14}$ , the total time for answering a query is

$$
O \left(\sum_ {i, j} \left(\underbrace {| V (G _ {i , j}) |} _ {\text {s t e p 1}} + \underbrace {\| \mathbf {B} _ {i , j} h \| _ {2} ^ {2} \phi^ {- 2} 2 ^ {2 i} / \epsilon^ {2}} _ {\text {s t e p 2}}\right)\right)
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} = O \left(\left(\sum_ {i, j} | V (G _ {i, j}) |\right) + \left(\sum_ {i} \sum_ {(u, v) \in E _ {i}} (h (u) - h (v)) ^ {2} 2 ^ {2 i} \phi^ {- 2} \epsilon^ {- 2}\right)\right) \\ = O \left(\left(\sum_ {i, j} | V (G _ {i, j}) |\right) + \left(\sum_ {i} \sum_ {(u, v) \in E _ {i}} g _ {(u, v)} ^ {2} (h (u) - h (v)) ^ {2} \phi^ {- 2} \epsilon^ {- 2}\right)\right) \\ = O \left(n \log^ {2} n \log W + \| \mathbf {D i a g} (g) \mathbf {A} h \| _ {2} ^ {2} \epsilon^ {- 2} \log^ {8} n\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

The second equality is because  $g_{(u,v)} \geq 2^i$  for all  $(u,v) \in E_i$  (by (53)). The third is because  $\sum_j |V(G_{i,j})| = O(n\log^2 n)$  for every  $i$  (by Lemma 5.3), and also because the  $E_i$ 's partition the edges in the original graph.

Sample. Again, let  $G_{i,j}$  be an expander of the maintained decomposition and denote  $\mathbf{D}^{(i,j)}$  its diagonal degree matrix. Then we define  $h^{(i,j)}$  as in (55) for each  $G_{i,j}$  which satisfies  $(h_v - h_u)^2 = (h_v^{(i,j)} - h_u^{(i,j)})^2$  and  $h^{(i,j)} \bot \mathbf{D}^{(i,j)}\vec{1}$ .

Next, let

$$
Q = \frac {K}{\sum_ {i , j} 2 ^ {2 i} \sum_ {v \in V (G _ {i , j})} (h _ {v} ^ {(i , j)}) ^ {2} \deg_ {G _ {i , j}} (v)}
$$

then we perform the following procedure for each expander  $G_{i,j}$  of our decomposition. For each node  $v$  in  $G_{i,j}$ , we sample each edge incident to  $v$  with probability

$$
\min  \{Q \cdot 2 ^ {2 i} \left(h _ {v} ^ {(i, j)}\right) ^ {2}, 1 \}.
$$

If an edge  $(u,v)$  is sampled twice (i.e. once for  $u$  and once for  $v$ ), then it is included only once in the output.

Computing all  $h^{(i,j)}$ 's and  $Q$  takes  $O(n\log^2 n\log W)$  time and the sampling of edges can be implemented such that the complexity is bounded by the number of included edges. For example by first sampling a binomial for each node and then picking the corresponding number of incident edges uniformly at random.

The expected number of included edges can be bounded by

$$
\sum_ {i, j} \sum_ {(u, v) \in E (G _ {i, j})} Q \cdot 2 ^ {2 i} ((h _ {v} ^ {(i, j)}) ^ {2} + (h _ {u} ^ {(i, j)}) ^ {2}) = 2 Q \sum_ {i, j} 2 ^ {2 i} \sum_ {u} \deg_ {G _ {i, j}} (u) (h _ {u} ^ {(i, j)}) ^ {2} = 2 K
$$

Thus the expected runtime is  $O(K + n\log^2 n\log W)$ , and for a w.h.p bound this increases to  $O(K\log n + n\log^2 n\log W)$ .

We are left with proving the claim, i.e., each edge  $(u,v)$  is sampled with probability at least

$$
\min  \left\{K \cdot \frac {g _ {(u , v)} ^ {2} (h _ {u} - h _ {v}) ^ {2}}{1 6 \| \mathbf {D i a g} (g) \mathbf {A} h \| _ {2} ^ {2} \log^ {8} n}, 1 \right\}.
$$

If  $Q \geq 2^{2i}(h_v^{(i,j)})^{-2}$  or  $Q \geq 2^{2i}(h_u^{(i,j)})^{-2}$ , then this is clear since then the sampling probability is just 1. So we consider the case of  $Q \leq \min (2^{2i}(h_v^{(i,j)})^{-2}, 2^{2i}(h_u^{(i,j)})^{-2})$ . Then the probability is

$$
\begin{array}{l} q _ {(u, v)} = Q \cdot 2 ^ {2 i} \left((h _ {v} ^ {(i, j)}) ^ {2} + (h _ {u} ^ {(i, j)}) ^ {2}\right) - Q ^ {2} 2 ^ {4 i} (h _ {v} ^ {(i, j)}) ^ {2} (h _ {u} ^ {(i, j)}) ^ {2} \\ \geq Q \cdot 2 ^ {2 i} \left(\left(h _ {v} ^ {(i, j)}\right) ^ {2} + \left(h _ {u} ^ {(i, j)}\right) ^ {2} - \left| \left(h _ {v} ^ {(i, j)}\right) \left(h _ {u} ^ {(i, j)}\right) \right|\right) \\ \geq 0. 5 Q \cdot 2 ^ {2 i} \left(\left(h _ {v} ^ {(i, j)}\right) ^ {2} + \left(h _ {u} ^ {(i, j)}\right) ^ {2}\right) \\ \geq 0. 2 5 Q \cdot 2 ^ {2 i} (h _ {v} ^ {(i, j)} - h _ {u} ^ {(i, j)}) ^ {2} \\ \geq 0. 2 5 Q \cdot g _ {u, v} ^ {2} (h _ {v} - h _ {u}) ^ {2} \\ \end{array}
$$

The first inequality is due to  $Q \leq 2^{2i}(h_v^{(i,j)})^{-2}$  and  $Q \leq 2^{2i}(h_u^{(i,j)})^{-2}$ .

Further, we can bound  $Q$  as

$$
Q = \frac {K}{\sum_ {i , j} 2 ^ {2 i} \sum_ {v \in V (G _ {i , j})} (h _ {v} ^ {(i , j)}) ^ {2} \deg_ {G _ {i , j}} (u)} \geq \frac {K \phi^ {2}}{4 \| \mathbf {D i a g} (g) \mathbf {A} h \| _ {2} ^ {2}},
$$

by applying the Cheeger-inequality to each  $\sum_{v\in V(G_{i,j})}(h_v^{(i,j)})^2\deg_{G_{i,j}}(u)$ . In summary we obtain that any edge  $(u,v)$  is included with probability at least

$$
K \frac {g _ {u , v} ^ {2} (h _ {v} - h _ {u}) ^ {2}}{1 6 \| \mathbf {D i a g} (g) \mathbf {A} h \| _ {2} ^ {2} \log^ {8} n}.
$$

Probability. As discussed in the analysis of SAMPLE, we can compute  $Q$  and all  $h^{(i,j)}$ 's in  $O(n\log^2 n\log W)$  time, then for each edge  $e \in I$ , we can look up the subgraph  $G_{i,j}$  it belongs to, and compute its probability  $q_e$  used in SAMPLE in  $O(1)$  time, which takes  $O(|I|)$  time in total for all edges in  $I$ .

LeverageScoreSample. We will sample the edges in each expander  $G_{i,j}$  separately. Let  $\mathbf{L}^{(i,j)}$  denote the Laplacian of the unweighted subgraph  $G_{i,j}$ , and we consider it as in the original dimension  $n \times n$ . Since  $g_e \in [2^i, 2^{i+1}]$  if  $e$  is an edge in  $G_{i,j}$ , we have

$$
\sum_ {(i, j)} 2 ^ {2 i} \mathbf {L} ^ {(i, j)} \preceq \widehat {\mathbf {L}} \preceq \sum_ {(i, j)} 2 ^ {2 i + 2} \mathbf {L} ^ {(i, j)}
$$

where  $\hat{\mathbf{L}} = \mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{Diag}(g)^{2}\mathbf{A}$  is the weighted Laplacian of the entire graph  $G$  with weights  $g_{e}^{2}$  on each edge  $e$ . Since  $\mathbf{L}^{(i,j)}\succeq 0$  for all  $i,j$ , we know  $2^{2i}\mathbf{L}^{(i,j)}\preceq \hat{\mathbf{L}}$  for all  $i,j$ , which gives  $\hat{\mathbf{L}}^\dagger \preceq 2^{-2i}(\mathbf{L}^{(i,j)})^\dagger$  since the null space of  $\mathbf{L}^{(i,j)}$  contains the null space of  $\hat{\mathbf{L}}$ . As a result, for an edge  $e = (u,v)$  in the expander  $G_{i,j}$ , we know

$$
\sigma (\mathbf {D i a g} (g) \mathbf {A}) _ {e} = g _ {e} ^ {2} \chi_ {e} ^ {\top} \widehat {L} ^ {\dagger} \chi_ {e} \leq g _ {e} ^ {2} 2 ^ {- 2 i} \chi_ {e} ^ {\top} (\mathbf {L} ^ {(i, j)}) ^ {\dagger} \chi_ {e} ^ {\top} \leq 4 \chi_ {e} ^ {\top} (\mathbf {L} ^ {(i, j)}) ^ {\dagger} \chi_ {e},
$$

where  $\chi_{e}$  is the row of  $A$  corresponding to  $e$ , so it has only two non-zero entries, 1 at  $u$  and  $-1$  at  $v$ . Thus, it suffices to have

$$
p _ {e} \geq 4 K ^ {\prime} \chi_ {e} ^ {\top} (\mathbf {L} ^ {(i, j)}) ^ {\dagger} \chi_ {e}.
$$

As  $G_{i,j}$  is a  $\phi$ -expander for  $\phi = 1 / \log^4 n$ , using Cheeger's inequality (i.e.  $\lambda_2(\mathbf{L}^{(i,j)}) \geq \phi^2 / 2$ ) we can derive that for all  $y \perp \vec{1}_n$ ,  $y^\top (\mathbf{L}^{(i,j)})^\dagger y \leq 2\phi^{-2}y^\top (\mathbf{D}^{(i,j)})^{-1}y$ , where  $\mathbf{D}^{(i,j)}$  is the diagonal degree matrix of  $G_{i,j}$ . Thus, we know

$$
\chi_ {e} ^ {\top} (\mathbf {L} ^ {(i, j)}) ^ {\dagger} \chi_ {e} \leq 2 \phi^ {- 2} \left(\frac {1}{\deg_ {u} ^ {(i , j)}} + \frac {1}{\deg_ {v} ^ {(i , j)}}\right).
$$

Similar to how we implement SAMPLE, we can go through each node  $v$  (with non-zero degree) in  $G_{i,j}$  and sample each edge incident to  $v$  with probability

$$
p _ {v} = \min \left\{\frac {1 6 K ^ {\prime} \phi^ {- 2}}{\deg_ {u} ^ {(i , j)}}, 1 \right\},
$$

and only include an edge once if it is sampled twice at both endpoints. Again this can be implemented so that the complexity is bounded by the number of included edges, and the expected number of sampled edges can be bounded by

$$
\sum_ {i, j} \sum_ {(u, v) \in E (G _ {i, j})} \frac {1 6 K ^ {\prime} \phi^ {- 2}}{\deg_ {u} ^ {(i , j)}} + \frac {1 6 K ^ {\prime} \phi^ {- 2}}{\deg_ {v} ^ {(i , j)}} = \sum_ {i, j} \sum_ {u} \deg_ {u} ^ {(i, j)} \cdot \frac {1 6 K ^ {\prime} \phi^ {- 2}}{\deg_ {u} ^ {(i , j)}}
$$

$$
= 1 6 K ^ {\prime} \phi^ {- 2} \sum_ {i, j} | V (G _ {i, j}) | \leq O (K ^ {\prime} n \log^ {1 0} n \log W),
$$

and we can include additional  $\log n$  factor to make the bound hold with high probability. To see the probability that an edge is sampled satisfies the requirement, note

$$
\begin{array}{l} p _ {e} \geq \max p _ {u}, p _ {v} \geq \frac {p _ {u} + p _ {v}}{2} \\ \geq \min \left\{8 K ^ {\prime} \phi^ {- 2} (\frac {1}{\deg_ {u} ^ {(i , j)}} + \frac {1}{\deg_ {v} ^ {(i , j)}}, 1 \right\} \\ \geq 4 K ^ {\prime} \chi_ {e} (\mathbf {L} ^ {(i, j)}) ^ {\dagger} \chi_ {e} \geq \sigma (\mathbf {D i a g} (g) \mathbf {A}) _ {e} \\ \end{array}
$$

LeverageScoreBound. We can get the exact probability an edge  $e$  is sampled in the previous implementation of LEVERAGESCORESAMPLE. Suppose  $e = (u, v)$  is in subgraph  $G_{i,j}$ , we can compute in constant time

$$
p _ {u} = \min \left\{\frac {1 6 K ^ {\prime} \phi^ {- 2}}{\deg_ {u} ^ {(i , j)}}, 1 \right\}, p _ {v} = \min \left\{\frac {1 6 K ^ {\prime} \phi^ {- 2}}{\deg_ {v} ^ {(i , j)}}, 1 \right\},
$$

and  $p_{e} = p_{u} + p_{v} - p_{u}p_{v}$ . Going through all edges in  $I$  takes  $O(|I|)$  time.

# 6 Dual Solution Maintenance

In this section we discuss how we efficiently maintain an approximation of the dual slack (i.e.  $s$ ) in our IPM. Recall that our dual slack vector starts with an initial value  $s^{(\mathrm{init})}$ , and in each iteration  $t$  accumulates an update with the generic form of  $\mathbf{A}\delta^{(t)}$  where  $\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$  is the edge incidence matrix. The actual computation  $\delta^{(t)}$  relies on other data structures such as gradient maintenance, which will be discussed in later sections. In this section, we focus on maintaining an approximation of the dual slack assuming  $\delta^{(t)}$  is given to us.

Theorem 6.1 (VECTORMAINTAINER). There exists a data-structure that supports the following operations

- INITIALIZER  $(\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}, v^{(\mathrm{init})} \in \mathbb{R}^m, \epsilon > 0)$  The data-structure stores the given edge incidence matrix  $\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ , the vector  $v^{(\mathrm{init})} \in \mathbb{R}^m$  and accuracy parameter  $0 < \epsilon \leq 1$  in  $\widetilde{O}(m)$  time.  
-  $ADD(h \in \mathbb{R}^n)$ : Suppose this is the  $t$ -th time the ADD operations is called, and let  $h^{(k)}$  be the vector  $h$  given when the ADD operation is called for the  $k^{th}$  time. Define  $v^{(t)} \in \mathbb{R}^m$  to be the vector

$$
v ^ {(t)} = v ^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})} + \mathbf {A} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {t} h ^ {(k)}.
$$

Then the data structure returns a vector  $\overline{v}^{(t)}\in \mathbb{R}^m$  such that  $\overline{v}^{(t)}\approx_{\epsilon}v^{(t)}$ . The output will be in a compact representation to reduce the size. In particular, the data-structure returns a pointer to  $\overline{v}$  and a set  $I\subset [m]$  of indices  $i$  where  $\overline{v}_i^{(t)}$  is changed compared to  $\overline{v}_i^{(t - 1)}$ , i.e., the result of the previous call to ADD. The total time after  $T$  calls to ADD is

$$
\tilde {O} \left(T n \log W + T \epsilon^ {- 2} \cdot \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {T} \| \left(v ^ {(t)} - v ^ {(t - 1)}\right) / v ^ {(t)} \| _ {2} ^ {2}\right).
$$

- COMPUTEEXACT(): Returns  $v^{(t)} \in \mathbb{R}^m$  in  $O(m)$  time, where  $t$  is the number of times  $ADD$  is called so far (i.e.,  $v^{(t)}$  is the state of the exact vector  $v$  after the most recent call to  $ADD$ ).

From the graph-algorithmic perspective, our data structure maintains a flow $^{15}$ $v \in \mathbb{R}^{E}$  on an unweighted oriented graph  $G = (V,E)$  (initially  $v = v^{\mathrm{(init)}}$ ). This flow is changed by the  $\mathrm{ADD}(h)$  operation, whose input is the vertex potential  $h \in \mathbb{R}^{E}$  inducing a new electrical flow to be augmented; in particular, the new flow on each edge  $(i,j)$  is  $v_{(i,j)} \gets v_{(i,j)} + h_j - h_i$ . The  $\mathrm{ADD}$  operation then returns necessary information so that the user can maintain an approximation of flow  $v$ . The user can query for the exact value of  $v$  by calling  $\mathrm{COMPUTERACT}$ . Note that the graph  $G$  never changes; though, our data structured can be easily modified to allow  $G$  to have edge resistances that may change over time.

To make sense of the running time for the ADD operations, note that reading the input over  $T$  ADD calls needs  $Tn$  time, and in total there can be as many as  $T\epsilon^{-2} \cdot \sum_{t=1}^{T} \left\| (v^{(t)} - v^{(t-1)}) / v^{(t)} \right\|_2^2$  changes to the output vector we need to perform to guarantee the approximation bound. Thus, ignoring the log  $W$  factor, the complexity in our theorem is optimal as it is just the bound on input and output size.

Throughout this section we denote  $h^{(t)}$  the input vector  $h$  of the  $t$ -th call to ADD (or equivalently referred to as the  $t$ -th iteration), and let  $v^{(t)} = v^{\mathrm{(init)}} + \mathbf{A}\sum_{k=1}^{t}h^{(k)}$  be the state of the exact solution  $v$  (as defined in Theorem 6.1) for the  $t$ -th call to ADD.

In our algorithm (see Algorithm 4) we maintain a vector  $\widehat{f}$  which is the sum of all past input vectors  $h$ , so we can retrieve the exact value of  $v_{i}^{(t)} = v_{i}^{\mathrm{(init)}} + (\mathbf{A}\widehat{f})_{i}$  for any  $i$  efficiently. This

Algorithm 4: Algorithm for Theorem 6.1  
1 members  
2  $D,\hat{f},\overline{v}\in \mathbb{R}^m,t\in \mathbb{N}$   
3  $D_{j},f^{(j)}\in \mathbb{R}^{n}$  and  $F_{j}\subset [m]$  for  $0\leq j\leq \log n$   
4 procedure INITIALIZE(A,  $v^{(\mathrm{init})},\epsilon)$   
5  $\overline{v}\gets v^{(\mathrm{init})},\widehat{f}\gets \overline{0}_n,t\gets 0$   
6 for  $j = 0,\dots,\log n$  do  
7  $D_{j}.\mathrm{INITIALize}(\mathbf{A},1 / \overline{v},0.2\epsilon /\log n)$  (HEAVYHITTER, Lemma 5.1)  
8  $f^{(j)}\gets \vec{0}_n,F_j\gets \emptyset$   
9 procedure FINDINDICES(h∈Rn)  
10  $I\gets \emptyset$   
11 for  $j = \log n,\dots,0$  do  
12  $f^{(j)}\gets f^{(j)} + h / /$  When  $2^{j}|t$  , then  $f^{(j)} = \sum_{k = t - 2^{j} + 1}^{t}h^{(k)}$   
13 if  $2^{j}|t$  then  
14  $I\gets I\cup D_{j}.\mathrm{HEAVYQUERY}(f^{(j)})$   
15  $f^{(j)}\gets \vec{0}_n$   
16 return I  
17 procedure VERIFYINDEX(i)  
18 if  $|\overline{v}_i - (v^{(\mathrm{init})} + \mathbf{A}\widehat{f})_i|\geq 0.2\varepsilon \overline{v}_i / \log n$  then  
19  $\overline{v}_i\gets (v^{(\mathrm{init})} + \mathbf{A}\widehat{f})_i$   
20 for  $j = 0,\dots,\log n$  do  
21  $F_{j}\gets F_{j}\cup \{i\} ,D_{j}.\mathrm{SCALE}(i,0)$  // Notify other  $D_{j}$ 's to stop tracking  
22 return True  
23 return False  
24 procedure ADD(h∈Rn)  
25  $t\gets t + 1,\widehat{f}\gets \widehat{f} +h,I\gets$  FINDINDICES(h)  
26  $I\gets \{i|i\in I$  and VERIFYINDEX(i)=True}  
27 for  $j:2^j |t$  do  $I\gets I\cup \{i|i\in F_j$  and VERIFYINDEX(i)=True} ;  
28 for  $j:2^j |t$  do  
29 for  $i\in I\cup F_j$  do  
30  $|D_{j}.\mathrm{SCALE}(i,1 / \overline{v}_{i})$   
31  $F_{j}\gets \emptyset$   
32 return I,  $\overline{v}$   
33 procedure COMPUTEEXACT()  
34 return  $v^{(\mathrm{init})} + \mathbf{A}\widehat{f}$

value is computed and assigned to  $\overline{v}_i$  whenever the approximation  $\overline{v}$  that we maintain no longer satisfies the error guarantee for some coordinate  $i$ . As to how we detect when this may happen, we know the difference between  $v^{(t)}$  and the state of  $v$  at an earlier  $t'$ -th ADD call is

$$
v ^ {(t)} - v ^ {(t ^ {\prime})} = \mathbf {A} \left(\sum_ {k = t ^ {\prime} + 1} ^ {t} h ^ {(t)}\right),
$$

and thus we can detect all coordinates  $i$  that changes above certain threshold from  $t'$  to  $t$ -th ADD call using the HEAVYHITTER data structure of Lemma 5.1 (by querying it with  $\sum_{k=t'+1}^{t} h^{(t)}$  as the parameter  $h$ ). Note since the error guarantee we want is multiplicative in  $\overline{v}$  (i.e.,  $\overline{v}_i^{(t)} \in 1 \pm \epsilon v_i^{(t)}$  for all  $i$ ), while the threshold  $\epsilon$  in Lemma 5.1 is absolute and uniform, we give  $1 / \overline{v}$  as the scaling vector to HEAVYHITTER to accommodate this.

Since the most recent updates on  $\overline{v}_i$  for different indices  $i$ 's happen at different iterations, we need to track accumulated changes to  $v_i$ 's over different intervals to detect the next time an update is necessary for each  $i$ . Thus, it is not sufficient to just have one copy of HEAVYHITTER. On the other hand, keeping one individual copy of HEAVYHITTER for each  $0 \leq t' < t$  will be too costly in terms of running time. We handle this by instantiating  $\log n$  copies of the HEAVYHITTER data structure  $D_j$  for  $j = 0, \dots, \log n$ , each taking charge of batches with increasing number of iterations. In particular, the purpose of  $D_j$  is to detect all coordinates  $i$  in  $v$  with large accumulated change over batches of  $2^j$  iterations (see how we update and reset  $f^{(j)}$  in FINDINDICES in Algorithm 4). Each  $D_j$  has its local copy of a scaling vector, which is initialized to be  $1/\overline{v}$ , we refer to it as  $\hat{g}^{(j)}$ , and the cost to query  $D_j$  is proportional to  $\|\mathbf{Diag}(\hat{g}^{(j)})\mathbf{A}f^{(j)}\|_2^2$ . Note  $f^{(j)}$  accumulates updates over  $2^j$  iterations, and  $\|\sum_{k=1}^{2^j} h^{(k)}\|_2^2$  can be as large as  $2^j \sum_{k=1}^{2^j} \|h^{(k)}\|_2^2$ . Since we want to bound the cost of our data structure by the sum of the squares of updates (which can in turn be bounded by our IPM method) instead of the square of the sum of updates, querying  $D_j$  incurs an additional  $2^j$  factor overhead. Thus for efficiency purposes, if  $v_i$  would take much less than  $2^j$  iterations to accumulate a large enough change, we can safely let  $D_j$  stop tracking  $i$  during its current batch since  $v_i$ 's change would have been detected by a  $D_{j'}$  of appropriate (and much smaller)  $j'$  so that  $\overline{v}_i$  would have been updated to be the exact value (see implementation of VERIFYINDEX). Technically, we keep a set  $F_j$  to store all indices  $i$  that  $D_j$  stops tracking for its current batch of iterations and set  $\hat{g}_i^{(j)}$  to 0 so we don't pay for coordinate  $i$  when we query  $D_j$ . At the start of a new batch of  $2^j$  iterations for  $D_j$ , we add back all indices in  $F_j$  to  $D_j$  (Line 30) and reset  $F_j$ . As a result, only those  $i$ 's that indeed would take (close to)  $2^j$  iterations to accumulate a large enough change are necessary to be tracked by  $D_j$ , so we can query  $D_j$  less often for large  $j$  to offset its large cost. In particular, we query each  $D_j$  every  $2^j$  iterations (see Line 14).

We start our formal analysis with the following lemma.

Lemma 6.2. Suppose we perform the  $t$ -th call to  $ADD$ , and let  $\ell$  be the largest integer with  $2^{\ell}|t$  (i.e.  $2^{\ell}$  divides  $t$ ). Then the call to FINDINDICES in Line 25 returns a set  $I \subset [m]$  containing all  $i \in [m]$  such that there exists some  $0 \leq j \leq \ell$  satisfying both  $i \notin F_j$  and  $|v_i^{(t - 2^j)} - v_i^{(t)}| \geq 0.2|\overline{v}|_i\epsilon / \log n$ .

Proof. Pick any  $j \in [0, \ell]$ , if  $|v_i^{(t - 2^j)} - v_i^{(t)}| \geq 0.2|\overline{v}_i|\epsilon/\log n$ , then

$$
\left| e _ {i} ^ {\top} \mathbf {A} \sum_ {k = t - 2 ^ {j} + 1} ^ {t} h ^ {(k)} \right| \geq 0. 2 | \overline {{v}} _ {i} | \epsilon / \log n.
$$

We will argue why FINDINDICES detect all  $i$ 's satisfying this condition.

Note that we have  $f^{(j)} = \sum_{k=t-2^j+1}^t h^{(k)}$ , and thus by guarantee of Lemma 5.1 when we call  $D_j$ . HEAVY QUERY( $f^{(j)}$ ) (in Line 14), we obtain for every  $j \in [0,\ell]$  all  $i \in [m]$  with

$$
\left| \widehat {g} _ {i} ^ {(j)} e _ {i} ^ {\top} \mathbf {A} \sum_ {k = t - 2 ^ {j} + 1} ^ {t} h ^ {(k)} \right| \geq 0. 2 \epsilon / \log n.
$$

Here  $\widehat{g}_i^{(j)} = 0$  if  $i\in F_j$  by Line 21, which happens whenever  $\overline{v}_i$  is changed in Line 19. Thus by Line 30 we have  $\widehat{g}_i^{(j)} = 1 / |\overline{v}_i|$  for all  $i\notin F_j$ . Equivalently, we obtain all indices  $i\notin F_j$  satisfying the following condition, which proves the lemma.

$$
\left| e _ {i} ^ {\top} \mathbf {A} \left(\sum_ {k = t - 2 ^ {j} + 1} ^ {t} h ^ {(k)}\right) \right| \geq 0. 2 | \overline {{v}} _ {i} | \epsilon / \log n
$$

![](images/d7282a2b7953bbb668b6f58fd8b1bc4460f4fec0443f32d5d137947eb5273472.jpg)

To guarantee that the approximation  $\overline{v}$  we maintain is within the required  $\epsilon$  error bound of the exact vector  $v$ , we need to argue that the  $D_{j}$ 's altogether are sufficient to detect all potential events that would cause  $\overline{v}_{i}$  to become outside of  $(1 \pm \epsilon)v$ . It is easy to see that if an index  $i$  is included in the returned set  $I$  of FINDINDICES (Line 25), then our algorithm will follow up with a call to VERIFYINDEX(i), which will guarantee that  $\overline{v}_{i}$  is close to the exact value  $v_{i}$  (or  $\overline{v}_{i}$  will be updated to be  $v_{i}$ ). Thus, if we are in iteration  $t$ , and  $\bar{t}$  is the most recent time VERIFYINDEX(i) is called, we know  $\overline{v}_{i} \approx v_{i}^{(\bar{t})}$ , the value of  $\overline{v}_{i}$  remains the same since iteration  $\bar{t}$ , and the index  $i$  is not in the result of FINDINDICES for any of the iterations after  $\bar{t}$ . We will demonstrate the last condition is sufficient to show  $v_{i}^{(\bar{t})} \approx v_{i}^{(t)}$ , which in turn will prove  $\overline{v}_{i} \approx v_{i}^{(t)}$ . To start, we first need to argue that for any two iterations  $\bar{t} < t$ , the interval can be partitioned into a small number of batches such that each batch is exactly one of the batches tracked by some  $D_{j}$ .

Lemma 6.3. Given any  $\bar{t} < t$ , there exists a sequence

$$
t = t _ {0} > t _ {1} > \ldots > t _ {k} = \bar {t}
$$

such that  $k \leq 2\log t$  and  $t_{z + 1} = t_z - 2^{\ell_z}$  where  $\ell_z$  satisfies  $2^{\ell_z}|t_z$  for all  $z = 0, \ldots, k - 1$ .

Proof. We can find such a sequence  $(t_z)_z$  of length at most  $2\log n$  as follows. Write both  $t, \bar{t}$  as  $\log t$ -bit binary, and start from the most significant bit.  $t$  and  $\bar{t}$  will have some common prefix, and the first bit where they differ must be a 1 for  $t$  and 0 for  $\bar{t}$  since  $t > \bar{t}$ . Suppose the bit they differ corresponds to  $2^\ell$ , then let  $t' = \lfloor t / 2^\ell \rfloor 2^\ell$ , i.e.  $t'$  is  $t$  but with everything to the right of  $2^\ell$  zeroed out. We can create the sequence in two halves. First from  $t$  to  $t'$  by iteratively subtracting the current least significant 1-bit. To go from  $t'$  down to  $\bar{t}$ , consider the sequence backwards, we start from  $\bar{t}$ , and iteratively add a number equal to the current least significant 1-bit. This will keep pushing the least significant 1 to the left, and eventually arrive at  $t'$ . Clearly this gives a sequence satisfying the condition that  $t_{z+1} = t_z - 2^{\ell_z}$  and  $2^{\ell_z}|t_z$  for all  $t_z$  created in our sequence. The length of the sequence is at most  $2\log t$ , since each half has length at most  $\log t$ . For an example see Figure 1.

Now we can argue  $\overline{v}$  stays in the desired approximation range around  $v$ .

Lemma 6.4 (Correctness of Theorem 6.1). Assume we perform the  $t$ -th call to  $ADD$ , the returned vector  $\overline{v}$  satisfies  $|v_i^{(t)} - \overline{v}_i| \leq \epsilon |v_i^{(t)}|$  for all  $i \in [m]$ , and  $I$  contains all indices that have changed since the  $(t-1)$ -th  $ADD$  call.

<table><tr><td>tk</td><td>binary representation of tk</td><td>lk</td><td>tk</td><td>binary representation of tk</td><td>lk</td></tr><tr><td>t=t0</td><td>101001101</td><td>l0=0</td><td>t4</td><td>100100000</td><td>l4=2</td></tr><tr><td>t1</td><td>101001100</td><td>l1=2</td><td>t5</td><td>100011100</td><td>l5=1</td></tr><tr><td>t2</td><td>101001000</td><td>l2=3</td><td>t6</td><td>100011010</td><td>l6=0</td></tr><tr><td>t3</td><td>101000000</td><td>l3=5</td><td>t̅ = t7</td><td>100011001</td><td></td></tr></table>

Figure 1: Example of transform  $t$  to  $\bar{t}$  in  $2\log t$  steps.  $t'$  in the proof of Lemma 6.3 is  $t_3$ . Here  $2^{\ell_k}|t_k$  and  $t_{k + 1} = t_k - 2^{\ell_k}$  for all  $k$ , and the bit (i.e.  $2^{\ell_k}$ ) is highlighted in each row.

Proof. By  $\widehat{f} = \sum_{j=1}^{t} h^{(j)}$  we have  $v^{(\mathrm{init})} + \mathbf{A}\widehat{f} = v^{(t)}$  in Line 18.
