So after a call to  $\mathrm{VERIFYINDEX}(i)$  we know that  $|\overline{v}_i - v_i^{(t)}| < 0.2\epsilon \overline{v}_j / \log n$ , either because the comparison  $|\overline{v}_i - (v^{(\mathrm{init})} + \mathbf{A}\widehat{f})_i| \geq 0.2\epsilon \overline{v}_i / \log n$  in Line 18 returned false, or because we set  $\overline{v}_i \gets (v^{(\mathrm{init})} + \mathbf{A}\widehat{f})_i$  in Line 19. Note this is also the only place we may change  $\overline{v}_i$ . So consider some time  $\bar{t} \leq t$  when  $\mathrm{VERIFYINDEX}(i)$  was called for the last time (alternatively  $\bar{t} = 0$ ). Then  $\overline{v}_i$  has not changed during the past  $t - \bar{t}$  calls to  $\mathrm{ADD}$ , and we know  $|\overline{v}_i - v_i^{(\bar{t})}| \leq 0.2\epsilon \overline{v}_i / \log n$ . We now want to argue that  $|v^{(\bar{t})} - v^{(t)}| \leq 0.2\epsilon \overline{v}_i$ , which via triangle inequality would then imply  $\overline{v}_i \approx_{\epsilon} v_i^{(t)}$ .

For  $t = \bar{t}$  this is obvious, so consider  $\bar{t} < t$ . We know from Lemma 6.3 the existence of a sequence

$$
t = t _ {0} > t _ {1} > \dots > t _ {k} = \bar {t}
$$

with  $2^{\ell_z} |t_z$  and  $t_{z+1} = t_z - 2^{\ell_z}$ . In particular, this means that the interval between iteration  $t_{z+1}$  and  $t_z$  corresponds to exactly a batch tracked by  $D_{l_z}$ . Thus, at iteration  $t_z$  when FINDINDICES is called,  $D_{l_z}$ . HEAVYQUERY is executed in Line 14. This gives us  $|v_i^{(t_z)} - v_i^{(t_{z+1})}| < 0.2\epsilon |\overline{v}_i| / \log n$  for all  $z$ , because by Lemma 6.2 the set  $I \cup (\bigcup_j F_j)$  contains all indices  $i$  which might have changed by  $0.2|\overline{v}_i| \epsilon / \log n$  over the past  $2^\ell$  iterations for any  $2^\ell |t$ , and because  $\mathsf{VERIFYINDEX}(i)$  is called for all  $i \in I \cup (\bigcup_j F_j)$  in Line 26 and Line 27.

Note we can assume  $\log t \leq \log n$  by resetting the data-structure after  $n$  iterations, and this bounds the length of the sequence  $k \leq 2\log n$ . This then yields the bound

$$
| v _ {i} ^ {(\overline {{t}})} - v _ {i} ^ {(t)} | = | v _ {i} ^ {(t _ {k})} - v _ {i} ^ {(t _ {0})} | \leq \sum_ {z = 1} ^ {k} | v _ {i} ^ {(t _ {z})} - v _ {i} ^ {(t _ {z - 1})} | \leq k \cdot 0. 2 \epsilon | \overline {{v}} _ {i} | / \log n \leq 0. 4 \epsilon | \overline {{v}} _ {i} |
$$

Thus we have  $|\overline{v}_i - v_i^{(t)}| \leq (0.4\epsilon + 0.2\epsilon / \log n)|\overline{v}_i|$ , which implies  $\overline{v}_i \approx_{\epsilon} v_i$ . It is also straightforward to check that when we return the set  $I$  at the end of ADD,  $I$  contains all the  $i$ 's where  $\mathrm{VERIFYINDEX}(i)$  is called and returned true in this iteration, which are exactly all the  $i$ 's where  $\overline{v}_i$ 's are changed in Line 19.

Now we proceed to the complexity of our data structure. We start with the cost of FIND-INDICES, which is mainly on the cost of querying  $D_{j}$ 's. As we discussed at the beginning, there can be a large overhead for large  $j$ , but this is compensated by querying large  $j$  less frequently.

Lemma 6.5. After  $T$  calls to ADD, the total time spent in FINDINDICES and VERIFYINDEX is bounded by

$$
\widetilde {O} \left(T \epsilon^ {- 2} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {T} \| \left(v ^ {(t)} - v ^ {(t - 1)}\right) / v ^ {(t)} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + T n \log W\right)
$$

Proof. We start with the cost of FINDINDICES. Every call to ADD invokes a call to FINDINDICES, so we denote the  $t$ -th call to FINDINDICES as the one associated with the  $t$ -th ADD. Fix any  $j$  and consider the cost for  $D_{j}$ . We update  $f^{(j)}$  once in each call, which takes  $O(n)$ . Every  $2^{j}$  calls would incur the cost to  $D_{j}$ . HEAVYQUERY( $f^{(j)}$ ). Without loss of generality we consider the cost of the first time this happens (at iteration  $2^{j}$ ) since the other batches follow the same

calculation. We denote  $\widehat{g}^{(j)}$  as the scaling vector in  $D_{j}$  when the query happens. We know  $\widehat{g}_i^{(j)} = 0$  if  $i\in F_j$ , and  $\widehat{g}_i^{(j)} = 1 / |\overline{v}_i|$  otherwise. Note here we can skip the superscript indicating the iteration number, since if  $i\notin F_j$  it must be that  $\overline{v}_i$  has not changed over the  $2^{j}$  iterations. The cost to query  $D_{j}$  in Line 14 can then be bounded by.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \tilde {O} \left(\left\| \mathbf {D i a g} \left(\widehat {g} ^ {(j)} \mathbf {A} f _ {k} ^ {(j)} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \epsilon^ {- 2} + n \log W\right)\right) \\ \leq \widetilde {O} (\| \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {2 ^ {j}} \mathbf {D i a g} (1 / v ^ {(t)}) \mathbf {A} h ^ {(t)} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \epsilon^ {- 2} + n \log W) \\ \leq \widetilde {O} \left(\left. \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {2 ^ {j}} \| \mathbf {D i a g} (1 / v ^ {(t)}) \mathbf {A} h ^ {(t)} \| _ {2}\right) ^ {2} \epsilon^ {- 2} + n \log W\right) \\ \leq \widetilde {O} \left(2 ^ {j} \cdot \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {2 ^ {j}} \| \mathbf {D i a g} (1 / v ^ {(t)}) \mathbf {A} h ^ {(t)} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \epsilon^ {- 2} + n \log W\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

The first line is by Lemma 5.1, and note we use  $0.2\epsilon/\log n$  as the error parameter in the call. The first inequality is by looking at  $\widehat{g}_i^{(j)}$  for each index  $i$  separately. The value is either 0 so replacing it by  $1/v_i^{(t)}$  only increase the norm, or we know  $i \notin F_j$ , so  $\widehat{g}_i^{(j)}$  is within a constant factor of  $1/v_i^{(t)}$  since  $v_i^{(t)} \approx v_i^{(1)} \approx \overline{v}_i$  for all  $t \in [1,2^j]$ . The second inequality uses the triangle inequality, and the third inequality uses Cauchy-Schwarz. The cost of all subsequent queries to  $D_j$  follows similar calculation, and as this query is only performed once every  $2^j$  iterations, the total time after  $T$  iterations is

$$
\begin{array}{l} \widetilde {O} \left(T \epsilon^ {- 2} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {T} \| \mathbf {D i a g} (1 / v ^ {(t)}) \mathbf {A} h ^ {(t)} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + T 2 ^ {- j} n \log W\right) \\ = \widetilde {O} \left(T \epsilon^ {- 2} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {T} \| \left(v ^ {(t)} - v ^ {(t - 1)}\right) / v ^ {(t)} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + T 2 ^ {- j} n \log W\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

Note that the equality follows the definition of  $v^{(t)}$  in Theorem 6.1. We can then sum over the total cost for all  $D_{j}$ 's for  $j = 1, \ldots, \log n$  to get the final running time bound in the lemma statement.

As to the cost of VERIFYINDEX, each call computes  $(v^{(\mathrm{init})} + \mathbf{A}\widehat{f})_i$  which takes  $O(1)$  time as each row of  $\mathbf{A}$  has only two non-zero entries. Further, the updates to  $F_{j}$ 's and calls to  $D_{j}$ . SCALE take  $\widetilde{O}(1)$  time. Now we need to bound the total number of times we call VERIFYINDEX, which can only happen in two cases. The first case (Line 26) is when  $i$  is returned by FINDINDICES in Line 25, and the number of times we call VERIFYINDEX is bounded by the size of  $I$ , which is in turn bounded by the running time of FINDINDICES. So the total cost over  $T$  iterations can be bounded by the total cost of FINDINDICES. The second case (Line 27) is when  $i$  is in some  $F_{j}$  because  $\overline{v}_i$  was updated due to  $v_i$  changing by more than  $0.2\epsilon/\log n$ , and the number of times this occurs can be bounded by

$$
\widetilde {O} \left(T \epsilon^ {- 2} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {T} \| (v ^ {(t)} - v ^ {(t - 1)}) / v ^ {(t)} \| _ {2} ^ {2}\right).
$$

Adding up the total cost of VERIFYINDEX and FINDINDICES proves the lemma.

![](images/662b4e310668d01a2c90eb7926aff6dd7b105df986c778e6b44790578ae339da.jpg)

We proceed to prove the complexity bounds in Theorem 6.1.

Initialize. The main work is to initialize the data structures  $D_{j}$  for  $j = 1, \dots, \log n$ , which takes  $\widetilde{O}(m)$  time in total by Lemma 5.1.

Add. The cost not associated with any FINDINDICES and VERIFYINDEX is  $O(n)$ . Together with Lemma 6.5 gives the bound of total time of  $T$  calls to ADD

$$
\tilde {O} \left(T \epsilon^ {- 2} \sum_ {t = 1} ^ {T} \| (v ^ {(t)} - v ^ {(t - 1)}) / v ^ {(t)} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + T n \log W\right)
$$

ComputeExact. This just takes  $O(\mathrm{nnz}(A))$  to compute the matrix-vector product, which is  $O(m)$  since  $A$  is an edge incidence matrix.

# 7 Gradient and Primal Solution Maintenance

In Section 4.2 and Section 4.3 we provided two IPMs. These IPMs try to decrease some potential function  $\Phi(v)$  for  $v \in \mathbb{R}^m$ , so they require the direction of steepest descent, which is typically given by the gradient  $\nabla \Phi(v)$ . However, the two IPMs allows for approximations and works with different norms, so they actually require the maximizer

$$
\bar {g} := \underset {w \in \mathbb {R} ^ {m}: \| w \| \leq 1} {\operatorname {a r g m a x}} \left\langle \nabla \Phi (\bar {v}), w \right\rangle , \tag {59}
$$

which gives the direction of steepest ascent with respect to some norm  $\| \cdot \|$  and some  $\overline{v} \approx v$ . The IPM of Section 4.2 uses the  $\ell_2$ -norm (in which case the solution of above problem is just  $\overline{g} = \nabla \Phi(\overline{v}) / \| \nabla \Phi(\overline{v}) \|_2$ ) whereas the IPM of Section 4.3 uses the norm  $\| \cdot \|_{\tau + \infty}$ .

In this section we describe a framework that is able to maintain the maximizer of (59) efficiently. Note that the maximizer is an  $m$ -dimensional vector, so writing down the entire vector in each iteration of the IPM would be too slow. Luckily, the IPM only requires the vector  $\mathbf{A}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{X}}\overline{g}$ , where  $\overline{x}\approx x$  is an approximation of the current primal solution and  $\mathbf{A}\in \mathbb{R}^{m\times n}$  is the constraint matrix of the linear program. This vector is only  $n$  dimensional, so we can afford to write it down explicitly. Hence our task is to create a data structure that can efficiently maintain  $\mathbf{A}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{X}}\overline{g}$ . This will be done in Section 7.1.

Note that the vector  $\overline{g}$  is closely related to the primal solution  $x$ . In each iteration of our IPM, the primal solution  $x$  changes by (see (5) and (6) in Section 3.1)

$$
x ^ {(\mathrm {n e w})} \gets x + \eta \overline {{\mathbf {X}}} \overline {{g}} - \mathbf {R} h,
$$

for some  $h \in \mathbb{R}^n$ ,  $\eta \in \mathbb{R}$ , random sparse diagonal matrix  $\mathbf{R} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times m}$ ,  $\overline{\mathbf{X}} = \mathbf{Diag}(\bar{x})$  for  $\bar{x} \approx x$ , and  $\bar{g}$  is as in (59). Because of the sparsity of  $\mathbf{R}$ , we can thus say that during iteration  $t$  the primal solution  $x^{(t)}$  is of the form

$$
x ^ {(t)} = x ^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})} + \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {t} \left(h ^ {\prime (k)} + \eta \overline {{\mathbf {X}}} ^ {(k)} \bar {g} ^ {(k)}\right), \tag {60}
$$

where  $h^{\prime (k)}$  is the vector  $\mathbf{R}h$  during iteration number  $k$ , and  $\overline{g}^{(k)},\overline{x}^{(k)}$  are the vectors  $\overline{g}$  and  $\overline{x}$  during iteration number  $k$ .

Thus in summary, the primal solution  $x$  is just the sum of (scaled) gradient vectors  $\overline{g}^{(k)}$  and some sparse vectors  $h^{\prime (k)}$ . The main result of this section will be data structures (Theorems 7.1 and 7.7) that maintain both  $\mathbf{A}^\top \overline{\mathbf{X}}\overline{g}$  and an approximation  $\overline{x}$  of the primal solution  $x$ .

We state the result with respect to the harder case, when using the  $\| \cdot \|_{\tau +\infty}$ -norm for (59). At the end of Section 7.2, when we finish this result, we also state what the variant would look like for the easier case of using the  $\ell_2$ -norm, where the vector  $\overline{g}$  is given by the simple expression  $\nabla \Phi (\overline{v}) / \| \nabla \Phi (\overline{v})\| _2$ .

Recall that for  $v \in \mathbb{R}^m$  we defined  $\Phi(v) := \sum_{i=1}^{m} \exp(\lambda(v_i - 1)) + \exp(-\lambda(v_i - 1))$  for some given fixed parameter  $\lambda$  of value polylog  $n$ . Our main result of this section is the following Theorem 7.1. When using Theorem 7.1 in our IPM, we will use  $g = \eta \overline{x}$  for some scalar  $\eta \in \mathbb{R}$ , so that QUERYSUM returns the desired approximation of (60).

Theorem 7.1. There exists a deterministic data-structure that supports the following operations

- INITIALIZIZE ( $\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ ,  $x^{\mathrm{(init)}} \in \mathbb{R}^m$ ,  $g \in \mathbb{R}^m$ ,  $\widetilde{\tau} \in \mathbb{R}^m$ ,  $z \in \mathbb{R}^m$ ,  $\epsilon > 0$ ): The data-structure preprocesses the given matrix  $\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ , vectors  $x^{\mathrm{(init)}}$ ,  $g$ ,  $\widetilde{\tau}$ ,  $z \in \mathbb{R}^m$ , and accuracy parameter  $\epsilon > 0$  in  $\tilde{O}(\mathrm{nnz}(\mathbf{A}))$  time. We denote  $\mathbf{G}$  the diagonal matrix  $\mathbf{Diag}(g)$ . The data-structure assumes  $0.5 \leq z \leq 2$  and  $n/m \leq \widetilde{\tau} \leq 2$ .  
- UPDATE  $(i \in [m], a \in \mathbb{R}, b \in \mathbb{R}, c \in \mathbb{R})$ : Sets  $g_i \gets a$ ,  $\widetilde{\tau}_i \gets b$  and  $z_i \gets c$  in  $O(\| e_i^\top \mathbf{A} \|_0)$  time. The data-structure assumes  $0.5 \leq z \leq 2$  and  $n/m \leq \widetilde{\tau} \leq 2$ .

- QUERYPRODUCT(): Returns  $\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{G}\nabla\Phi(\overline{z})^{b(\overline{\tau})} \in \mathbb{R}^{n}$  for some  $\overline{\tau} \in \mathbb{R}^{m}$ ,  $\overline{z} \in \mathbb{R}^{m}$  with  $\overline{\tau} \approx_{\epsilon} \widetilde{\tau}$  and  $\| \overline{z} - z \|_{\infty} \leq \epsilon$ , where

$$
x ^ {\flat (\overline {{\tau}})} := \operatorname * {a r g m a x} _ {\| w \| _ {\overline {{\tau}} + \infty}} \langle x, w \rangle .
$$

Every call to QUERYPRODUCT must be followed by a call to QUERYSUM, and we bound their complexity together (see QUERYSUM).

- QUERYSUM  $(h \in \mathbb{R}^m)$ : Let  $v^{(\ell)}$  be the vector  $\mathbf{G}\nabla \Phi (\overline{z})^{b(\overline{\tau})}$  used for the result of the  $\ell$ -th call to QUERYPRODUCT. Let  $h^{(\ell)}$  be the input vector  $h$  given to the  $\ell$ -th call to QUERYSUM. We define

$$
x ^ {(t)} := x ^ {(\text {i n i t})} + \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {t} v ^ {(\ell)} + h ^ {(\ell)}.
$$

Then the  $t$ -th call to QUERYSUM returns a vector  $\overline{x} \in \mathbb{R}^m$  with  $\overline{x} \approx_{\epsilon} x^{(t)}$ .

Assuming the input vector  $h$  in a sparse representation (e.g. a list of non-zero entries), then after  $T$  calls to QUERYSUM and QUERYPRODUCT the total time for all calls together is bounded by

$$
O \left(T n \epsilon^ {- 2} \log^ {2} n + \log n \cdot \sum_ {\ell = 0} ^ {T} \| h ^ {(\ell)} \| _ {0} + T \log n \cdot \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {T} \| v ^ {(\ell)} / x ^ {(\ell - 1)} \| _ {2} ^ {2} / \epsilon^ {2}\right).
$$

The output  $\overline{x} \in \mathbb{R}^m$  is returned in a compact representation to reduce the size. In particular, the data-structure returns a pointer to  $\overline{x}$  and a set  $J \subset [m]$  of indices which specifies which entries of  $\overline{x}$  have changed between the current and previous call to QUERYSUM.

- COMPUTEExactSUM(): Returns the exact  $x^{(t)}$  in  $O(m\log n)$  time.  
- POTENTIAL(): Returns  $\Phi(\overline{z})$  in  $O(1)$  time for some  $\overline{z}$  such that  $\| \overline{z} - z \|_{\infty} \leq \epsilon$ .

The main idea is that, although the exact gradient  $\nabla \Phi (z)^{b(\widetilde{\tau})}\in \mathbb{R}^m$  can be indeed  $m$ -dimensional and thus costly to maintain, if we perturb  $\widetilde{\tau}$  and  $z$  slightly we can show that  $\nabla \Phi (\overline{z})^{b(\overline{\tau})}$  will be in  $O(\epsilon^{-2}\log n)$  dimension. Here we say dimension in the sense that the  $m$  entries can be put into  $O(\epsilon^{-2}\log n)$  buckets, and entries in the same bucket share a common value. The proof consists of two steps, and we employ two sub data structures. First, we construct the  $O(\epsilon^{-2}\log n)$  dimensional approximation  $\nabla \Phi (\overline{z})^{b(\overline{\tau})}$  to the exact gradient. Using its low dimensional representation we are able to efficiently maintain  $\mathbf{AG}\nabla \Phi (\overline{z})^{b(\overline{\tau})}$ . The data structure that maintains this low dimensional representation and  $\mathbf{AG}\nabla \Phi (\overline{z})^{b(\overline{\tau})}$  is discussed in Section 7.1. The subsequent Section 7.2 presents a data structure that takes the low dimensional representation of the gradient as input and maintains the desired sum for QUERYSUM.

# 7.1 Gradient Maintenance

In this section we discuss the computation of the  $O(\epsilon^{-2}\log n)$  dimensional representation of the gradient  $\nabla \Phi (\overline{z})^{\flat (\overline{\tau})}$ , which in turn provides a good enough approximation to the real gradient  $\nabla \Phi (z)^{\flat (\widetilde{\tau})}\in \mathbb{R}^{m}$ . This representation is then used to efficiently maintain  $\mathbf{A}^\top \mathbf{G}\nabla \Phi (\overline{z})^{\flat (\overline{\tau})}$ . The formal result is as follows:

Lemma 7.2. There exists a deterministic data-structure that supports the following operations

- INITIALIZIZE  $(\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}, g \in \mathbb{R}^m, \widetilde{\tau} \in \mathbb{R}^m, z \in \mathbb{R}^m, \epsilon > 0)$ : The data-structure preprocesses the given matrix  $\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ , vectors  $g, \widetilde{\tau}, z \in \mathbb{R}^m$ , and accuracy parameter  $\epsilon > 0$  in  $O(\mathrm{nmz}(\mathbf{A}))$  time. The data-structure assumes  $0.5 \leq z \leq 2$  and  $n/m \leq \widetilde{\tau} \leq 2$ . The output is a partition  $\bigcup_{k=1}^{K} I_k = [m]$  with  $K = O(\epsilon^{-2} \log n)$ .

-  $\text{UPDATE}(i \in [m], a \in \mathbb{R}, b \in \mathbb{R}, c \in \mathbb{R})$ : Sets  $g_i = a$ ,  $\widetilde{\tau}_i = b$  and  $z_i = c$  in  $O(\| e_i^\top \mathbf{A})$  time. The data-structure assumes  $0.5 \leq z \leq 2$  and  $n/m \leq \widetilde{\tau} \leq 2$ . The index  $i$  might be moved to a different set, so the data-structure returns  $k$  such that  $i \in I_k$ .  
- QUERY(): Returns  $\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{G}\nabla\Phi(\overline{z})^{\flat(\overline{\tau})} \in \mathbb{R}^{n}$  for some  $\overline{\tau} \in \mathbb{R}^{m}$ ,  $\overline{z} \in \mathbb{R}^{m}$  with  $\overline{\tau} \approx_{\epsilon} \widetilde{\tau}$  and  $\|\overline{z}-z\|_{\infty} \leq \epsilon$ , where  $x^{\flat(\overline{\tau})} := \operatorname{argmax}_{\|w\|_{\overline{\tau}_{+\infty}} \leq 1} \langle x, w \rangle$ . The data-structure further returns the low dimensional representation  $s \in \mathbb{R}^{K}$  such that

$$
\sum_ {k = 1} ^ {K} s _ {k} \mathbf {1} _ {i \in I _ {k}} = \left(\nabla \Phi (\overline {{z}}) ^ {\flat (\overline {{\tau}})}\right) _ {i}
$$

for all  $i\in [m]$  , in  $O(n\epsilon^{-2}\log n)$  time.

- POTENTIAL() Returns  $\Phi(z)$  in  $O(1)$  time.

Before describing the low dimensional representation of  $\Phi (\bar{z}))^{\flat (\overline{\tau})}$ , we first state the following result which we will use to compute  $x^{\flat (\overline{\tau})}$ . Note this is just the steepest ascent direction of  $x$  with respect to a custom norm, and when  $w,v$  are two vectors of the same dimension we refer to  $wv$  as the entry-wise product vector.

Lemma 7.3 ([LS19, Algorithm 8]). Given  $x \in \mathbb{R}^n$ ,  $v \in \mathbb{R}^n$ , we can compute in  $O(n \log n)$  time

$$
u = \operatorname *{argmax}_{\| w\|_{2} + \| wv^{-1}\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\langle x,w\rangle .
$$

We have the following simple corollary of Lemma 7.3.

Corollary 7.4. Given  $x \in \mathbb{R}^n$ ,  $v \in \mathbb{R}^n$ , we can compute in  $O(n \log n)$  time

$$
u = \operatorname *{argmax}_{\| vw\|_{2} + \| w\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\langle x,w\rangle .
$$

Proof. We have

$$
\max  _ {\| v w \| _ {2} + \| w \| _ {\infty} \leq 1} \langle x, w \rangle = \max  _ {\| w v \| _ {2} + \| w \| _ {\infty}} \langle x v ^ {- 1}, w v \rangle = \max  _ {\| w ^ {\prime} \| _ {2} + \| w ^ {\prime} v ^ {- 1} \| _ {\infty}} \langle x v ^ {- 1}, w ^ {\prime} \rangle
$$

by substituting  $w' = wv$ . Thus for the maximizer we have

$$
\operatorname * {a r g m a x} _ {\| v w \| _ {2} + \| w \| _ {\infty} \leq 1} \langle x, w \rangle = v ^ {- 1} \operatorname * {a r g m a x} _ {\| w ^ {\prime} \| _ {2} + \| w ^ {\prime} v ^ {- 1} \| _ {\infty}} \langle x v ^ {- 1}, w ^ {\prime} \rangle ,
$$

where the maximizer of on the right hand side can be computed in  $O(n \log n)$  via Lemma 7.3 and entry-wise multiplying by  $v^{-1}$  takes only  $O(n)$  additional time.

Recall  $x^{\flat(\tau)} \coloneqq \operatorname{argmax}_{\|w\|_{\tau+\infty} \leq 1} \langle x, w \rangle$  and  $\|w\|_{\tau+\infty} \coloneqq \|w\|_{\infty} + C \|w\sqrt{\tau}\|_2$  for some constant  $C$ . Corollary 7.4 allows us to compute  $x^{\flat(\tau)}$  (using the corollary with  $v = C\sqrt{\tau}$ ) for any  $x$  in its original dimension. However, if  $x$  is an  $m$  dimensional vector such as  $\nabla \Phi(z)$ , then Corollary 7.4 is too slow to be used in every iteration of the IPM.

To address this, we first discretize  $z$  and  $\widetilde{\tau}$  by rounding the entries to values determined by the error parameter  $\epsilon$  to get  $\overline{z},\overline{\tau}$  which are in low dimensional representation (i.e. put the indices into buckets). In particular,  $\overline{z}$  will be the vector  $z$  with each entry rounded down to the nearest value of form  $0.5 + \ell \epsilon /2$  for non-negative integer  $\ell$ , and  $\overline{\tau}$  rounds down each entry of  $\widetilde{\tau}$  to the nearest power of  $(1 - \epsilon)$ . It is fairly straightforward to see from definition this in turn also gives low dimensional representation of  $\nabla \Phi (\overline{z})$ . In the next lemma we show when  $x$  and  $\tau$  are both low dimensional,  $x^{\flat (\tau)}$  also admits a low dimensional representation that can be computed efficiently, which let us to compute  $\nabla \Phi (\overline{z})^{\flat (\overline{\tau})}$ . In the following lemma, when we use a set (i.e. a bucket) of indices as a vector, we mean it's indicator vector with 1 at indices belong to the set and 0 elsewhere.

Algorithm 5: Algorithm for reducing the dimension of  $\nabla \Phi (\overline{z})^{\flat}$  and maintaining  $\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{G}\nabla \Phi (\overline{z})^{\flat}$  (Lemma 7.2)  
1 members   
2  $I^{(k,\ell)}$  partition of [m].   
3  $w^{(k,\ell)}\in \mathbb{R}^n$  // Maintained to be  $\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{G}\mathbf{1}_{i\in I^{(k,\ell)}}$    
4  $g,z\in \mathbb{R}^m,p\in \mathbb{R};$  //  $p$  is maintained to be  $\Phi (z)$    
5 procedure INITIALIZE(A  $\in \mathbb{R}^{m\times n},g\in \mathbb{R}^m,\widetilde{\tau}\in \mathbb{R}^m,z\in \mathbb{R}^m,\epsilon >0)$    
6 A  $\leftarrow$  A,  $g\gets g,z\gets z,p\gets \Phi (z)$    
7  $I^{(k,\ell)}\gets \emptyset ,w^{(k,\ell)}\gets \vec{0}\quad \forall k = 1,\ldots ,\log_{(1 - \epsilon)}^{-1}(n / m)$  and  $\ell = 0,\dots,(1.5 / \epsilon)$    
8 for  $i\in [1,m]$  do   
9 Find  $k,\ell$  such that  $0.5 + \ell \epsilon /2\leq z_i <   0.5 + (\ell +1)\epsilon /2$  and   
10  $(1 - \epsilon)^{k + 1}\leq \widetilde{\tau}_i\leq (1 - \epsilon)^k.$    
11 Add i to  $I^{(k,\ell)}$  and set  $\overline{\tau}_i\gets (1 - \epsilon)^{k + 1}$    
11  $w^{(k,\ell)}\gets w^{(k,\ell)} + \mathbf{A}^\top g_i e_i$    
12 return  $(I^{(k,\ell)})_{k,\ell \geq 0}$    
13 procedure UPDATE(i  $\in [m],a\in \mathbb{R},b\in \mathbb{R},c\in \mathbb{R})$    
14  $p\gets \exp (\lambda c) + \exp (-\lambda c) - (\exp (\lambda z_i) + \exp (-\lambda z_i))$    
15  $z_{i}\gets c$    
16 Find  $k,\ell$  such that  $i\in I^{(k,\ell)}$  , then remove i from  $I^{(k,\ell)}$    
17  $w^{(k,\ell)}\gets w^{(k,\ell)} - \mathbf{A}^\top g_i e_i$    
18 Find  $k,\ell$  such that  $0.5 + \ell \epsilon /2\leq c <   0.5 + (\ell +1)\epsilon /2$  and  $(1 - \epsilon)^{k + 1}\leq b\leq (1 - \epsilon)^k$  then insert i into  $I^{(k,\ell)}$    
19  $w^{(k,\ell)}\gets w^{(k,\ell)} + \mathbf{A}^\top a e_i$    
20  $g_{i}\gets a$    
21 return k, l   
22 procedure QUERY()   
23 // Construct scaled low dimensional representation of  $\nabla \Phi (\overline{z})$    
24 Let  $x_{k,\ell} = |I^{(k,\ell)}|$  ( $\lambda \exp (\lambda (0.5 + \ell \epsilon /2 - 1)) - \lambda \exp (-\lambda (0.5 + \ell \epsilon /2 - 1)))$  Interpret x as an  $O(\epsilon^{-2}\log n)$  dimensional vector. // Construct scaled low dimensional representation of  $\overline{\tau}$  , here C is the constant when define  $\| \cdot \|_{\tau +\infty}$  Let v be the vector with  $v_{k,\ell} = \sqrt{|I^{(k,\ell)}|(1 - \epsilon)^{k + 1}} /C.$    
25 s  $\leftarrow$  argmaxy:||vy||2+||y||∞≤1<x,y> via Corollary 7.4.   
26 return s and  $\sum_{k,l}s_{k,\ell}w^{(k,\ell)}$    
27 procedure POTENTIAL()   
28 return p

Lemma 7.5. Given  $\overline{x},\overline{v},\overline{g}\in \mathbb{R}^k$ $x,v\in \mathbb{R}^n$  , and a partition  $\bigcup_{i = 1}^{k}I^{(i)} = [n]$  , such that  $x = \sum_{i = 1}^{k}\overline{x}_iI^{(i)}$ $v = \sum_{i = 1}^{k}\overline{v}_{i}I^{(i)}$  and  $\overline{g}_i = \sqrt{|I^{(i)}|}$  , let  $\overline{u}\in \mathbb{R}^k$  be the maximizer of

$$
\overline {{u}} := \underset {\overline {{w}} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {k}: \| (\overline {{g v}}) \overline {{w}} \| _ {2} + \| \overline {{w}} \| _ {\infty} \leq 1} {\operatorname {a r g m a x}} \langle \overline {{x}}   \overline {{g}} ^ {2}, \overline {{w}} \rangle .
$$

We can define  $u \in \mathbb{R}^n$  via  $u = \sum_{i=1}^{k} \overline{u}_i I^{(i)}$ , then

$$
\langle v,u\rangle = \max_{w\in \mathbb{R}^{n}: \| vw\|_{2} + \| w\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\langle x,w\rangle .
$$

Proof. Consider  $\overline{w} \in \mathbb{R}^k$  and  $w \in \mathbb{R}^n$  with  $w = \sum_{i=1}^{k} \overline{w}_i I^{(i)}$ . Then  $\| w \|_{\infty} = \| \overline{w} \|_{\infty}$  and

$$
\| w v \| _ {2} ^ {2} = \sum_ {i} (w _ {i} v _ {i}) ^ {2} = \sum_ {j} \sum_ {i \in I ^ {(j)}} (\overline {{w}} _ {j} \overline {{v}} _ {j}) ^ {2} = \sum_ {j} | I ^ {(j)} | (\overline {{w}} _ {j} \overline {{v}} _ {j}) ^ {2} = \| (\overline {{g v}}) \overline {{w}} \| _ {2} ^ {2}.
$$

So  $\overline{w}$  satisfies  $\| (\overline{g v})\overline{w}\| _2 + \| \overline{w}\|_{\infty}\leq 1$  iff  $w$  satisfies  $\| vw\| _2 + \| w\|_{\infty}\leq 1$ . Further,

$$
\langle \overline {{x}} \overline {{g}} ^ {2}, \overline {{w}} \rangle = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k} | I ^ {(i)} | \overline {{x _ {i}}} \overline {{w}} _ {i} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} x _ {i} w _ {i} = \langle x, w \rangle .
$$

Thus

$$
\max _ {\overline {{w}} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {k}: \| (\overline {{g v}}) \overline {{w}} \| _ {2} + \| \overline {{w}} \| _ {\infty} \leq 1} \langle \overline {{x}} \overline {{g}} ^ {2}, \overline {{w}} \rangle \leq \max _ {w \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n}: \| v w \| _ {2} + \| w \| _ {\infty} \leq 1} \langle x, w \rangle
$$

which in turn means that  $u \in \mathbb{R}^k$  as defined in the lemma satisfies

$$
\langle x, u \rangle = \langle \overline {{x}} \overline {{g}} ^ {2}, \overline {{u}} \rangle \leq \max _ {w \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n}: \| v w \| _ {2} + \| w \| _ {\infty} \leq 1} \langle x, w \rangle .
$$

Let  $S \coloneqq \{w \in \mathbb{R}^n : \|vw\|_2 + \|w\|_\infty \leq 1$  and  $w_i = w_j$  for all  $i, j \in I^{(\ell)} \}$ , i.e. the set of  $n$  dimensional vectors  $w$  that has a low dimensional representation via some  $\overline{w} \in \mathbb{R}^k$  and  $w = \sum_{i=1}^k \overline{w}_i I^{(i)}$ . Then

$$
\langle x,u\rangle = \max_{w\in S}\langle x,w\rangle .
$$

by definition of  $u$ . We claim that

$$
\max_{w\in S}\langle x,w\rangle = \max_{w\in \mathbb{R}^{n}:||vw||_{2} + ||w||_{\infty}\leq 1}\langle x,w\rangle ,
$$

which would then conclude the proof of the lemma.

So assume there exists some maximizer  $w \in \mathbb{R}^n$  with  $\|vw\|_2 + \|w\|_\infty \leq 1$  and there are indices  $i, j, \ell$  with  $i, j \in I^{(\ell)}$  and  $w_i \neq w_j$ , i.e. the vector  $w \notin S$ . Then define  $w' \in \mathbb{R}^n$  with  $w_i' = w_j' = (w_i + w_j)/2$ . For this vector we have  $\|w'\|_\infty \leq \|w\|_\infty$  and

$$
\| v w ^ {\prime} \| _ {2} ^ {2} = \sum_ {k \notin \{i, j \}} v _ {k} ^ {2} w _ {k} ^ {2} + v _ {i} ^ {2} (w _ {i} + w _ {j}) ^ {2} / 2 \leq \sum_ {k \notin \{i, j \}} v _ {k} ^ {2} w _ {k} ^ {2} + v _ {i} ^ {2} w _ {i} ^ {2} + v _ {j} ^ {2} w _ {j} ^ {2} = \| v w \| _ {2} ^ {2},
$$

where the first equality is because  $v_{i} = v_{j}$  for  $i,j$  in same  $I^{\ell}$ . So we know  $w'$  also satisfies  $\| vw' \|_2 + \| w' \|_{\infty} \leq 1$ . On the other hand,

$$
\langle x, w \rangle = \sum_ {k \notin \{i, j \}} x _ {k} w _ {k} + x _ {i} w _ {i} + x _ {i} w _ {j} = \sum_ {k \notin \{i, j \}} x _ {k} w _ {k} + x _ {i} (w _ {i} + w _ {j}) / 2 + x _ {i} (w _ {i} + w _ {j}) / 2 = \langle x, w ^ {\prime} \rangle .
$$

Thus  $w'$  must also be a maximizer, and if we repeat this transformation we eventually have  $w' \in S$ . This concludes the proof.

We now have all tools to prove Lemma 7.2.

Proof of Lemma 7.2. The data-structure is given by Algorithm 5. It is straightforward to check that INITIalyze and UPDATE guarantees that the data-structure maintains the following invariants:

-  $\bigcup_{k,\ell}I^{(k,\ell)} = [m]$  is a partition of  $[m]$ .  
-  $i \in I^{(k,\ell)}$  if and only if  $0.5 + \ell \epsilon / 2 \leq z_i < 0.5 + (\ell + 1)\epsilon / 2$  and  $(1 - \epsilon)^{k+1} \leq \widetilde{\tau}_i \leq (1 - \epsilon)^k$ .

$w^{(k,\ell)} = \mathbf{A}^\top \mathbf{G}\cdot I^{(k,\ell)}\in \mathbb{R}^n.$  
$p = \sum_{i}\exp (\lambda z_{i}) + \exp (-\lambda z_{i}) = \Phi (z).$

Moreover, our rounding of  $z, \widetilde{\tau}$  gives  $O(\epsilon^{-1}\log_{1 - \epsilon}^{-1}(n / m))$  dimensional representations because of the assumption  $0.5\leq z\leq 2$  and  $n / m\leq \widetilde{\tau}\leq 2$ , and is simply  $O(\epsilon^{-2}\log n)$ .

Next, let us analyze the function QUERY. We can interpret the decomposition  $\bigcup_{k,\ell}I^{(k,\ell)} = [m]$  as a decomposition of the form  $\bigcup_t I^{(t)} = [m]$  by renaming the indices. Recall  $\overline{z},\overline{\tau}$  are low dimensional approximations of  $z,\widetilde{\tau}$  with each entry rounded. It is straightforward to see the vector  $x$  constructed in the first line of QUERY is just the low dimensional representation of  $\nabla \Phi (\overline{z})$  where each entry is scaled appropriately by the  $|I^{(k,\ell)}|$ 's, so the  $x$  we constructed corresponds to  $\overline{xg}$  in Lemma 7.5. Similarly, the  $v$  vector constructed in QUERY is the low dimensional representation of  $\sqrt{\overline{\tau}}$  scaled appropriately to serve as the  $\overline{gv}$  in Lemma 7.5. Thus by Lemma 7.5 the vector  $s$  obtained in QUERY is the vector such that  $s^{\prime}:= \sum_{t}s^{(t)}I^{(t)}\in \mathbb{R}^{m}$  satisfies

$$
\langle s ^ {\prime}, \nabla \Phi (\overline {{z}}) \rangle = \max _ {C \| \sqrt {\tau} y \| _ {2} + \| y \| _ {\infty} \leq 1} \langle \nabla \Phi (\overline {{z}}), y \rangle ,
$$

That is,  $s' = \nabla \Phi(\overline{z})^{\flat(\overline{\tau})}$ , and the returned  $s$  is a low dimensional representation of it. Moreover,  $\sum_{k,l} s_{k,\ell} w^{(k,\ell)}$  is exactly  $\mathbf{A}^\top \mathbf{G} \nabla \Phi(\overline{z})^{\flat(\overline{\tau})}$  because we maintained  $w^{(k,\ell)} = \mathbf{A}^\top \mathbf{G} \cdot I^{(k,\ell)} \in \mathbb{R}^n$ .

Complexity The complexity of INITIALIZE is dominated by the initialization of  $w^{(k,\ell)}$ 's, which takes  $O(\mathrm{nnz}(A))$  since we just partition the (scaled) rows of  $\mathbf{A}$  into buckets according to our rounding and sum up the vectors in each bucket. The complexity of  $\mathrm{UPDATE}(i,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)$  is  $O(\| e_i^\top \mathbf{A}\|_0)$ , i.e. the cost of maintaining  $w^{(k,\ell)} = \mathbf{A}^\top \mathbf{G} \cdot I^{(k,\ell)} \in \mathbb{R}^n$ . The time for POTENTIAL is  $O(1)$  since it just returns the value  $p$ .

As to the complexity of QUERY, as we work with  $O(\epsilon^{-2}\log n)$  dimensional vectors in QUERY, the time complexity according to Lemma 7.5 is  $O(\epsilon^{-2}\log n\log (\epsilon^{-2}\log n))$  for computing the maximizer  $s$ . Constructing  $\mathbf{A}^\top \mathbf{G}(\nabla \Phi (\overline{z}))^{\flat (\overline{\tau})} = \sum_{k,\ell}w^{(k,\ell)}s^{(k,\ell)}$  takes  $O(n\epsilon^{-2}\log n)$  time, which subsumes the first complexity, assuming  $\epsilon$  is polynomial in  $1 / n$ . Thus QUERY takes  $O(n\epsilon^{-2}\log n)$  time.

# 7.2 Primal Solution Maintenance

In this section we discuss the data structure that effectively maintains an approximation of the sum of all gradients computed so far, which is captured by Lemma 7.6. The input to the data structure is the  $O(\epsilon^{-2}\log n)$  dimensional representations of the gradients of each iteration, which is computed by the data structure of the previous subsection. At the end of this subsection we will combine Lemma 7.2 and the following Lemma 7.6 to obtain the main result Theorem 7.1, which allows us to efficiently maintain an approximation  $\overline{x}$  of the primal solution.

Lemma 7.6. There exists a deterministic data-structure that supports the following operations

- INITIALIZIZE  $(x^{(\mathrm{init})} \in \mathbb{R}^m, g \in \mathbb{R}^m, (I_k)_{1 \leq k \leq K}, \epsilon > 0)$ : The data-structure initialized on the given vectors  $x^{(\mathrm{init})}$ ,  $g \in \mathbb{R}^m$ , the partition  $\bigcup_{k=1}^{K} I_k = [m]$  where  $K = O(\epsilon^{-2} \log n)$ , and the accuracy parameter  $\epsilon > 0$  in  $O(m)$  time.  
-  $\text{SCALE}(i \in [m], a \in \mathbb{R})$ : Sets  $g_i \gets a$  in  $O(\log n)$  amortized time.  
-  $\text{MOVE}(i \in [m], k \in [1, K])$ : Moves index  $i$  to set  $I_k$  in  $O(\log n)$  amortized time.  
-  $QUERY(s \in \mathbb{R}^K, h \in \mathbb{R}^m)$ : Let  $g^{(\ell)}$  be the state of vector  $g$  during the  $\ell$ -th call to  $QUERY$  and let  $s^{(\ell)}$  and  $h^{(\ell)}$  be the input arguments of the respective call. The vector  $h$  will always be provided as a sparse vector so that we know where are the non-zeros in the vector.

Define  $y^{(\ell)} = \mathbf{G}^{(\ell)}\sum_{k = 1}^{K}I_{k}^{(\ell)}s_{k}^{(\ell)}$  and  $x^{(t)} = x^{\mathrm{(init)}} + \sum_{\ell = 1}^{t}h^{(\ell)} + y^{(\ell)}$ , then the  $t$ -th call to QUERY returns a vector  $\overline{x}\approx_{\epsilon}x^{(t)}$ . After  $T$  calls to QUERY, the total time of all  $T$  calls is bounded by

$$
O \left(T K + \log n \cdot \sum_ {\ell = 0} ^ {T} \| h ^ {(\ell)} \| _ {0} + T \log n \cdot \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {T} \| y ^ {(\ell)} / x ^ {(\ell - 1)} \| _ {2} ^ {2} / \epsilon^ {2}\right).
$$

The vector  $\overline{x} \in \mathbb{R}^m$  is returned as a pointer and additionally a set  $J \subset [m]$  is returned that contains the indices where  $\overline{x}$  changed compared to the result of the previous QUERY call.

- COMPUTEExactSum(): Returns the current exact vector  $x^{(t)}$  in  $O(m\log n)$  time.

The implementation of the data structure is given in Algorithm 6. At a high level, each iteration  $t$  (i.e. QUERY call) the vector  $x$  accumulates an update of  $h^{(t)} + y^{(t)}$ . For the  $h$  component, we always include the update to our approximation  $\overline{x}$  as soon as we see  $h$ . This is because the  $h$  provided by our IPM will be sparse, and we can afford to spend  $\tilde{O}(\|h\|_0)$  each iteration to carry out the update. For the  $y$  component in the update, we use a "lazy" update idea. In particular, we maintain a vector  $\hat{\ell} \in \mathbb{R}^m$  where  $\hat{\ell}_i$  stores the most recent iteration  $t$  (i.e. QUERY call) when  $\overline{x}_i$  is updated to be the exact value  $x_i^{(t)}$  for each index  $i$ . We update  $\overline{x}_i$  to be the exact value (in COMPUTEX) whenever  $h_i$  is non-zero,  $g_i$  is scaled, or  $i$  is moved to a different set  $I_k$ . If none of these events happen, the accumulated update to  $x_i$  since iteration  $\hat{\ell}_i$  will have a very simple form, so we just store the accumulated updates up to each iteration  $t$  in a vector  $f^{(t)}$ . To detect when the accumulated update on  $x_i$  has gone out of the approximation bound  $\epsilon \overline{x}_i$  since the last time  $\overline{x}_i$  is updated, we use  $\Delta_i^{(high)}$  and  $\Delta_i^{(low)}$  to store the upper and lower boundary of the approximation range around  $\overline{x}_i$ , and update  $\overline{x}_i$  once the accumulated updates goes beyond the range. We proceed with the proof of our result.

Proof of Lemma 7.6. We start by analyzing the correctness.

Invariant Let  $s^{(t)}, h^{(t)}, g^{(t)}, I_k^{(t)}$  be the state of  $s, h, g, I_k$  during the  $t$ -th call to QUERY and by definition of  $x^{(t)}$  we have for any index  $i$  that

$$
x _ {i} ^ {(t)} = x _ {i} ^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})} + \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {t} g _ {i} ^ {(\ell)} \left(\sum_ {k = 1} ^ {K} s _ {k} ^ {(\ell)} \mathbf {1} _ {i \in I _ {k} ^ {(\ell)}}\right) + h _ {i} ^ {(\ell)}.
$$

It is easy to check that  $\widehat{\ell}_i$  always store the most recent iteration when  $\overline{x}_i$  is updated by  $\mathrm{COMPUTEX}(i,h_i)$ . We first prove by induction that this update is always calculated correct, that is, the data-structure maintains the invariant  $\overline{x}_i = x_i^{(\widehat{\ell}_i)}$ .

We see from Line 27 that the data-structure maintains  $f^{(t)} = \sum_{k=1}^{t} s^{(k)}$ . Further note that  $\mathrm{COMPUTEX}(i, h_i)$  is called whenever  $h_i$  is non-zero (Line 29),  $g_i$  is changed, or  $i$  is moved to a different  $I_k$ . Thus if  $\widehat{\ell}_i < t$ , we know none of these events happened during iteration  $\ell \in (\widehat{\ell}_i, t]$  and the only moving part is the  $s^{(\ell)}$ 's over these iterations, which is exactly  $f^{(t)} - f^{(\widehat{\ell}_i)}$ . Thus, if  $k$  is the set  $I_k$  where  $i$  belongs to over iterations  $(\widehat{\ell}_i, t]$ , the execution of Line 11 gives

$$
\begin{array}{l} g _ {i} \cdot (f _ {k} ^ {(t)} - f _ {k} ^ {(\widehat {\ell_ {i}})}) + h _ {i} ^ {(t)} = g _ {i} \sum_ {\ell = \widehat {\ell_ {i}} + 1} ^ {t} s _ {k} ^ {(\ell)} + h _ {i} ^ {(t)} = h _ {i} ^ {(t)} + \sum_ {\ell = \widehat {\ell_ {i}} + 1} ^ {t} g _ {i} ^ {(\ell)} s _ {k} ^ {(\ell)} \\ = h _ {i} ^ {(t)} + \sum_ {\ell = \widehat {\ell_ {i}} + 1} ^ {t} g _ {i} ^ {(\ell)} \left(\sum_ {k = 1} ^ {K} s _ {k} ^ {(\ell)} \mathbf {1} _ {i \in I _ {k} ^ {(\ell)}}\right) = \sum_ {\ell = \widehat {\ell_ {i}} + 1} ^ {t} g _ {i} ^ {(\ell)} \left(\sum_ {k = 1} ^ {K} s _ {k} ^ {(\ell)} \mathbf {1} _ {i \in I _ {k} ^ {(\ell)}}\right) + h _ {i} ^ {(\ell)} \\ \end{array}
$$

where the first equality uses  $f^{(t)} = \sum_{\ell=1}^{t} s^{(\ell)}$  and the second equality uses  $g_i^{(\ell)} = g_i^{(t)}$  for all  $\widehat{\ell}_i < \ell \leq t$ , because  $\mathrm{COMPUTER}(i, h_i)$  is called whenever  $g_i$  is changed. The third equality is

Algorithm 6: Algorithm for accumulating  $\mathbf{G}\nabla \Phi (\overline{v})^{\flat}$  (Lemma 7.6)  
1 members   
2  $I_{1},\ldots ,I_{K}$  // Partition  $\bigcup_{k}I_{k} = [m]$    
3  $t\in \mathbb{N},\overline{x}\in \mathbb{R}^{m}$  // QUERY counter and approximation of  $x^{(t)}$    
4  $\widehat{\ell}\in \mathbb{N}^m$  //  $\widehat{\ell}_i$  is value of t when we last update  $\overline{x}_i\gets x_i$    
5  $f^{(t)}\in \mathbb{R}^{K}$  // Maintain  $f^{(t)} = \sum_{k = 1}^{t}s^{(k)}$    
6  $\Delta^{(high)},\Delta^{(low)}\in \mathbb{R}^m$  // Maintain  $\Delta_{i} = f_{k}^{(\widehat{\ell}_{i})}\pm |\epsilon \overline{x}_{i} / (10g_{i})|$  if  $i\in I_k$    
7 procedure INITIALIZE(xinit)  $\in \mathbb{R}^m$ $g\in \mathbb{R}^m,(I_k)_{1\leq k\leq K})$    
8  $\overline{x}\gets x^{\mathrm{(init)}}$ $(I_k)_{1\leq k\leq K}\gets (I_k)_{1\leq k\leq K},t\gets 0,f^{(0)}\gets \vec{0}_K,w\gets \vec{0}_m,g\gets g$    
9 private procedure COMPUTEX(i,hi)   
10 Let  $k$  be such that  $i\in I_k$    
11  $\overline{x}_i\gets \overline{x}_i + g_i\cdot (f_k^{(t)} - f_k^{(\widehat{\ell}_i)}) + h_i$    
12  $\widehat{\ell}_i\gets t$    
13 private procedure UPDATEDELTA(i)   
14 Let  $k$  be such that  $i\in I_k$    
15  $\Delta_{i}^{(high)}\gets f_{k}^{(\widehat{\ell}_{i})} + |\epsilon \overline{x}_{i} / (10g_{i})|$    
16  $\Delta_{i}^{(low)}\gets f_{k}^{(\widehat{\ell}_{i})} - |\epsilon \overline{x}_{i} / (10g_{i})|$    
17 procedure MOVE(i∈[m],k)   
18 COMPUTEX(i,0)   
19 Move index i to set  $I_{k}$    
20 UPDATEDELTA(i)   
21 private procedure SCALE(i,a)   
22 COMPUTEX(i,0)   
23  $g_{i}\gets a$    
24 UPDATEDELTA(i)   
25 procedure QUERY(s∈R^K,h∈R^m)   
26  $t\gets t + 1$ $J\gets \emptyset$    
27  $f^{(t)}\gets f^{(t - 1)} + s$    
28 for i such that  $h_i\neq 0$  do   
29 |COMPUTEX(i,hi),UPDATEDI(A(i),J←JU{i}   
30 for  $k = 1,\dots ,K$  do   
31 for  $i\in I_k$  with  $f_{k}^{(t)} > \Delta_{i}^{(high)}$  or  $f_{k}^{(t)} <   \Delta_{i}^{(low)}$  do   
32 |COMPUTEX(i,0),UPDATEDI(A(i),J←JU{i}   
33 return  $\overline{x}$  J   
34 procedure COMPUTEEXACTSUM()   
35 for  $i\in [m]$  and  $\widehat{\ell}_i <   t$  do COMPUTEX(i,0),UPDATEDI(A);   
36 return  $\overline{x}$

because  $\mathrm{COMPUTEX}(i, h_i)$  is called whenever  $i$  is moved to a different set, so  $i \in I_k^{(\ell)}$  for the same  $k$  for all  $\widehat{\ell}_i < \ell \leq t$ . The last equality uses  $h_i^{(\ell)} = 0$  for  $\widehat{\ell}_i < \ell < t$ , because  $\mathrm{COMPUTEX}(i, h_i)$  is called whenever  $h_i$  is non-zero. Thus by induction over the number of calls to  $\mathrm{COMPUTEX}(i, h_i)$ , when  $\widehat{\ell}_i$  is increased to  $t$  we have

$$
\overline {{x}} _ {i} = x _ {i} ^ {(t)} = x _ {i} ^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})} + \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {t} g _ {i} ^ {(\ell)} \left(\sum_ {k = 1} ^ {K} s _ {k} ^ {(\ell)} \mathbf {1} _ {i \in I _ {k} ^ {(\ell)}}\right) + h _ {i} ^ {(\ell)} = x _ {i} ^ {(t)},
$$

so the invariant is always maintained.

Correctness of Query. We claim that the function QUERY returns a vector  $\overline{x}$  such that for all  $i$

$$
\overline {{x}} _ {i} \approx_ {\epsilon} x _ {i} ^ {(t)} := x _ {i} ^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})} + \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {t} g _ {i} ^ {(\ell)} \left(\sum_ {k = 1} ^ {K} s _ {k} ^ {(\ell)} \mathbf {1} _ {i \in I _ {k} ^ {(\ell)}}\right) + h _ {i} ^ {(\ell)}.
$$

Given the invariant discussed above, we only need to guarantee  $\mathrm{COMPUTERACT}(i,h_i)$  is called whenever the approximation guarantee is violated for some  $i$ . Moreover, same as when we proved the invariant above, we only need to guarantee this in the case that since iteration  $\widehat{\ell}_i$ ,  $h_i$  is always 0,  $g_i$  remains constant and  $i$  remains in the same  $I_k$  for some  $k$ . Thus, the task is equivalent to detect whenever

$$
\left| \sum_ {\ell = \widehat {\ell} _ {i} + 1} ^ {t} g _ {i} ^ {(\ell)} s _ {k} ^ {(\ell)} \right| = | g _ {i} \cdot (f _ {k} ^ {(t)} - f _ {k} ^ {(\widehat {\ell} _ {i})}) | > \frac {\epsilon | \overline {{x}} _ {i} |}{1 0}.
$$

which is the same as

$$
f _ {k} ^ {(t)} \notin [ f _ {k} ^ {(\widehat {\ell} _ {i})} - | (\epsilon x _ {i}) / (1 0 g _ {i}) |, f _ {k} ^ {(\widehat {\ell} _ {i})} + | (\epsilon x _ {i}) / (1 0 g _ {i}) | ]
$$

Note that the lower and upper limits in the above range are exactly  $\Delta_i^{(inc)}$  and  $\Delta_i^{(dec)}$  as maintained by  $\mathrm{UPDATEDELTA}(i)$ , which will be called whenever any of the terms involved in the calculation of these limits changes. Thus Line 32 makes sure that we indeed maintain

$$
\overline {{x}} _ {i} \approx_ {\epsilon} x _ {i} ^ {(t)} \qquad \forall i.
$$

Also it is easy to check the returned set  $J$  contains all  $i$ 's such that  $\overline{x}_i$  changed since last QUERY.

Complexity The call to INITIALIZE takes  $O(m + K)$  as we initialize a constant number of  $K$  and  $m$  dimensional vectors, and this reduces to  $O(m)$  since there can be at most  $m$  non-empty  $I_k$ 's. A call to COMPUTEX takes  $O(1)$  time.

To implement Line 32 efficiently without enumerating all  $m$  indices, we maintain for each  $k \in [K]$  two sorted lists of the  $i$ 's in  $I_k$ , sorted by  $\Delta_i^{(high)}$  and  $\Delta_i^{(low)}$  respectively. Maintaining these sorted lists results in  $O(\log n)$  time per call for UPDATEDELTA. Hence MOVE and SCALE also run in  $O(\log n)$  time. To implement the loop for Line 32 we can go through the two sorted lists in order, but stop as soon as the check condition no longer holds. This bounds the cost of the loop by  $O(K)$  plus  $O(\log n)$  times the number of indices  $i$  satisfying  $f_k^{(t)} > \Delta_i^{(high)}$  or  $f(t) < \Delta_i^{(low)}$ , i.e.  $|f_k^{(t)} - f_k^{(\widehat{\ell}_i)}| > \Theta (\epsilon x_i^{(\widehat{\ell}_i)})$ . Note if a COMPUTEX and UPDATEDELTA is triggered by this condition for any  $i$ ,  $h_i$  must be 0 during  $(\widehat{\ell}_i, t]$  iterations. Thus, let  $z^{(t)} \coloneqq x^{(\mathrm{init})} + \sum_{\ell=1}^t \mathbf{G}^{(\ell)} \sum_k I_k^{(\ell)} s_k^{(\ell)}$ , we can rewrite that condition as  $|z_i^{(t)} - z_i^{(\widehat{\ell}_i)}| > \Theta (|\epsilon x_i^{(\widehat{\ell}_i)}|)$ . Throughout  $T$  calls to QUERY, we can bound the total number of times where  $i$  satisfies  $|z_i^{(t)} - z_i^{(\widehat{\ell}_i)}| > \Theta (|\epsilon x_i^{(\widehat{\ell}_i)}|)$  by

$$
O \left(T \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {T} \| \mathbf {G} ^ {(\ell)} \left(\sum_ {k} I _ {k} ^ {(\ell)} s _ {k} ^ {(\ell)}\right) / x ^ {(\ell - 1)} \| _ {2} ^ {2} / \epsilon^ {2}\right).
$$

The number of times COMPUTEX and UPDATEDELTA are triggered due to  $h_i^{(t)} \neq 0$  is  $\| h^{(t)} \|_0$  each iteration, and updating  $f^{(t)}$  takes  $O(K)$  time. So the total time for  $T$  calls to QUERY can be bounded by

$$
O (T K + \log^ {n} \cdot \sum_ {\ell = 0} ^ {T} \| h ^ {(\ell)} \| _ {0} + \log^ {n} \cdot T \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {T} \| \mathbf {G} ^ {(\ell)} (\sum_ {k} s _ {k} ^ {(\ell) I _ {k} ^ {(\ell)}}) / x ^ {(\ell - 1)} \| _ {2} ^ {2} / \epsilon^ {2}).
$$

The time for COMPUTEEXACTSUM is  $O(m \log n)$  since it just calls COMPUTEX and UPDAT-EDelta on all  $m$  indices.

Proof of Theorem 7.1. The data-structure for Theorem 7.1 follows directly by combining Lemma 7.2 and Lemma 7.6. The result for QUERYPRODUCT is obtained from Lemma 7.2, and the result for QUERYSUM is obtained from Lemma 7.6 using the vector  $s \in \mathbb{R}^K$  returned by Lemma 7.2 as input to Lemma 7.6.

Note that for our IPM variant using the steepest descent direction in  $\ell_2$  norm, we use a simple normalized  $\nabla \Phi (\overline{z}) / \| \nabla \Phi (\overline{z})\| _2$  instead of  $(\nabla \Phi (\overline{z}))^{\flat (\widetilde{\tau})}$ . Here

$$
\nabla \Phi (\overline{z}) / \| \nabla \Phi (\overline{z})\|_{2} = \operatorname *{argmax}_{\| w\|_{2}\leq 1}\langle \nabla \Phi (\overline{z}),w\rangle
$$

is analogous to

$$
\bigl (\nabla \Phi (\bar {z}) \bigr) ^ {\flat (\overline {{\tau}})} = \operatorname * {a r g m a x} _ {\| w \| _ {\overline {{\tau}} + \infty} \leq 1} \langle \nabla \Phi (\bar {z}), w \rangle ,
$$

as these are both steepest ascent directions of  $\nabla \Phi (\overline{z})$  but in different norms. It is straightforward to see how to simplify and extend the data structure of Theorem 7.1 to work for this setting. We state without proof the following variant. Note in this case we only need to round  $z$  so the low dimensional representation is in  $O(\epsilon^{-1})$  instead of  $O(\epsilon^{-2}\log n)$  dimension.

Theorem 7.7. There exists a deterministic data-structure that supports the following operations

- INITIALIZIZE  $(\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}, x^{\mathrm{(init)}} \in \mathbb{R}^m, g \in \mathbb{R}^m, z \in \mathbb{R}^m, \epsilon > 0)$ : The data-structure preprocesses the given matrix  $\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ , vectors  $x^{\mathrm{(init)}}, g, z \in \mathbb{R}^m$ , and accuracy parameter  $\epsilon > 0$  in  $\widetilde{O}(\mathrm{nnz}(\mathbf{A}))$  time. The data-structure assumes  $0.5 \leq z \leq 2$ .  
- UPDATE  $(i \in [m], a \in \mathbb{R}, c \in \mathbb{R})$ : Sets  $g_i \gets a$  and  $z_i \gets c$  in  $O(\| e_i^\top \mathbf{A} \|_0)$  time. The data-structure assumes  $0.5 \leq z \leq 2$ .  
- QUERYPRODUCT(): Returns  $\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{G}\nabla\Phi(\overline{z})/\|\nabla\Phi(\overline{z})\|_{2} \in \mathbb{R}^{n}$  for some  $\overline{z} \in \mathbb{R}^{m}$  with  $\|\overline{z}-z\|_{\infty} \leq \epsilon$ . Every call to QUERYPRODUCT must be followed by a call to QUERYSUM, and we bound their complexity together (see QUERYSUM).  
-  $\text{QUERYSUM}(h \in \mathbb{R}^m)$ : Let  $v^{(\ell)}$  be the vector  $\mathbf{G}\nabla\Phi(\overline{z}) / \| \nabla\Phi(\overline{z})\|_2$  used for the result of the  $\ell$ -th call to  $\text{QUERYPRODUCT}$ . Let  $h^{(\ell)}$  be the input vector  $h$  given to the  $\ell$ -th call to  $\text{QUERYSUM}$ . We define

$$
x ^ {(t)} := x ^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})} + \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {t} v ^ {(\ell)} + h ^ {(\ell)}.
$$

Then the  $t$ -th call to QUERYSUM returns a vector  $\overline{x} \in \mathbb{R}^m$  with  $\overline{x} \approx_{\epsilon} x^{(t)}$ .

Assuming the input vector  $h$  in a sparse representation (e.g. a list of non-zero entries), after  $T$  calls to QUERYSUM and QUERYPRODUCT the total time for all calls together is bounded by

$$
O \left(T n \epsilon^ {- 1} \log (1 / \epsilon) + \log n \cdot \sum_ {\ell = 0} ^ {T} \| h ^ {(\ell)} \| _ {0} + T \log n \cdot \sum_ {\ell = 1} ^ {T} \| v ^ {(\ell)} / x ^ {(\ell - 1)} \| _ {2} ^ {2} / \epsilon^ {2}\right).
$$

The output  $\overline{x} \in \mathbb{R}^m$  is returned in a compact representation to reduce the size. In particular, the data-structure returns a pointer to  $\overline{x}$  and a set  $J \subset [m]$  of indices which specifies which entries of  $\overline{x}$  have changed between the current and previous call to QUERYSUM.

- COMPUTEEXACTSUM() Returns the exact  $x^{(t)}$  in  $O(m\log n)$  time.  
- POTENTIAL() Returns  $\Phi(\overline{z})$  in  $O(1)$  time.

# 8 Minimum Weight Perfect Bipartite  $b$ -Matching Algorithms

In this section we prove the main result: a nearly linear time algorithm for minimum weight perfect bipartite  $b$  matching. The exact result proven in this section is the following theorem.

Theorem 8.1. For  $b, c \in \mathbb{Z}^m$ , we can solve minimum weight perfect bipartite  $b$ -matching for cost vector  $c$  in  $\widetilde{O}((m + n^{1.5})\log^2(\|c\|_\infty \|b\|_\infty))$  time.

We show how to combine the tools from Sections 4 to 7 to obtain our fast  $b$ -matching algorithm. In Section 4.2 and Section 4.3 we present two algorithms that each encapsulate a variant of the IPM. These algorithms only specify which computations must be performed, but they do not specify how they must be implemented. So our task is to use the data structures from Sections 5 to 7 to implement these IPMs.

This section is split into five subsections: We first consider the simpler, but slower IPMs from Section 4.2, based on the log-barrier method. This method is formalized via Algorithm 2.

Line 11 of Algorithm 2, requires random sampling to sparsify the solution of some linear system. Consequently, our first task (Section 8.1) is to use the data structure from Section 5 to implement this sampling procedure.

The next task is to implement the IPM of Algorithm 2, which is done in Section 8.2.

Note that Algorithm 2 only formalized a single step of the IPM, and the complete method is formalized by combining Algorithm 2 with Algorithm 1. So the next task (Section 8.3) is to implement Algorithm 1 efficiently using our data structures.

We then use our implementation of the IPM in Section 8.4 to obtain a  $\widetilde{O}(n\sqrt{m})$  time algorithm. At last, in Section 8.5, we show which small modification must be performed to replace the slower log-barrier based IPM with the faster leverage score based IPM of Section 4.3. This is also where we prove Theorem 8.1.

# 8.1 Primal Sampling

Our first task is to implement the random sampling of Line 11 of Algorithm 2. Let  $\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ ,  $g, g' \in \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^m$ , and  $h \in \mathbb{R}^n$ , then for the random sampling we want to sample each  $i \in [m]$  with some probability  $q_i$  such that

$$
q _ {i} \geq \min  \{1, \sqrt {m} ((\mathbf {G A} h) _ {i} ^ {2} / \| \mathbf {G A} h \| _ {2} ^ {2} + 1 / m) + C \cdot \sigma (\mathbf {G} ^ {\prime} \mathbf {A}) _ {i} \log (m / (\epsilon r)) \gamma^ {- 2} \},
$$

where  $C$  is some large constant and  $\gamma = 1/\mathrm{polylog}n$ . As this sampling is performed in each iteration of the IPM, it would be prohibitively expensive to explicitly compute the leverage scores  $\sigma(\mathbf{G}'\mathbf{A})$  or the vector  $\mathbf{G}\mathbf{A}h$ , as that would require  $\Omega(m)$  time. Consequently, instead we will use the data structure of Lemma 5.1 to efficiently perform this sampling.

Lemma 8.2. Consider a call to SAMPLEPRIMAL  $(K\in \mathbb{R}_{>0},D^{(\mathrm{sample},\sigma)},D^{(\mathrm{sample})},h\in \mathbb{R}^n)$  (Algo-1m 7). Let GA be the matrix represented by  $D^{(\mathrm{sample})}$  (Lemma 5.1), and  $\mathbf{G}'\mathbf{A}$  be the matrix represented by  $D^{(\mathrm{sample},\sigma)}$  (Lemma 5.1), then each  $\mathbf{R}_{i,i}$  is  $1 / q_{i}$  with some probability  $q_{i}$  and zero otherwise, where

$$
q _ {i} \geq \min \{1, K \sqrt {m} ((\mathbf {G A h}) _ {i} ^ {2} / \| \mathbf {G A h} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + 1 / m) + C \cdot \sigma (\mathbf {G ^ {\prime} A}) _ {i} \log (m / (\epsilon r)) \gamma^ {- 2} \}
$$

for some large constant  $C > 0$ . Further, w.h.p., there are at most

$$
\widetilde {O} (n \log (m / (\epsilon r)) \log W + K \sqrt {m})
$$

non-zero entries in  $\mathbf{R}$  which is also a bound on the complexity of SAMPLEPRIMAL. Here  $W$  is the ratio of largest to smallest (non-zero) entry in  $\mathbf{G}$  and in  $\mathbf{G}'$ .

Proof. We start by analyzing the vector  $u, v, w$ . Afterwards we analyze the distribution of the matrix  $\mathbf{R}$ . At last we bound the number of non-zero elements in  $\mathbf{R}$  and the complexity of the algorithm.

Algorithm 7: Sampling Algorithm for the IPM  
1 procedure  $S_{A M P L E}P R I M A L(K\in \mathbb{R}_{>0},D^{(\text{sample},\sigma)},D^{(\text{sample})},h\in \mathbb{R}^{n})$   
/*  $D^{(\text{sample},\sigma)},D^{(\text{sample})}$  instances of Lemma 5.1  
 $I_u\gets D^{(\text{sample},\sigma)}.LEVERAGESCORESAMPLE(3C\log (m / (\epsilon r))\gamma^{-2})$ $I_v\gets D^{(\text{sample})}.SAMPLE(h,3K\sqrt{m}\cdot (16\log^8 (n)))$ $I_w\subset [m]$ , where  $\mathbb{P}[i\in I_w] = 3K / \sqrt{m}$  independently for each  $i$ .  
 $I\gets I_u\cup I_v\cup I_w$   
Let  $u,v,w$  be  $\vec{0}_m$ .  
/* Set  $v_i = \mathbb{P}[i\in I_v]$ ,  $u_i = \mathbb{P}[i\in I_u]$ ,  $w_i = \mathbb{P}[i\in I_w]$  for  $i\in I$ $v_I\gets D^{(\text{sample})}.\text{PROBABILITY}(I,h,3K\sqrt{m}\cdot (16\log^8 (n)))$ $u_I\gets D^{(\text{sample},\sigma)}.LEVERAGESCOREBOUND(3C\log (m / (\epsilon r))\gamma^{-2},I)$ $w_i\gets 3K / \sqrt{m}$  for  $i\in I$ $\mathbf{R}\gets \mathbf{0}_{m\times m}$   
for  $i\in I$  do  
 $\mathbf{R}_{i,i}\gets 1 / \min \{1,u_i + v_i + w_i\}$  with probability  $\frac{\min\{1,u_i + v_i + w_i\}}{1 - (1 - u_i)(1 - v_i)(1 - w_i)}$   
return  $\mathbf{R}$

Distribution of vectors  $u, v, w$  By guarantee of Lemma 5.1 the methods  $D^{(\mathrm{sample})}$ .PROBABILITY and  $D^{(\mathrm{sample},\sigma)}$ .LEVERAGESCOREBOUND return the sampling probabilities used by  $D^{(\mathrm{sample})}$ .SAMPLE and  $D^{(\mathrm{sample},\sigma)}$ .LEVERAGESCORESAMPLE. Thus the vectors  $u, v$  satisfy

$$
u _ {i} = \mathbb {P} [ i \in I _ {u} ] \text {w i t h p r o b a b i l i t y} \mathbb {P} [ i \in I ] \text {a n d 0 o t h e r w i s e}.
$$

$$
v _ {i} = \mathbb {P} [ i \in I _ {v} ] \text {w i t h p r o b a b i l i t y} \mathbb {P} [ i \in I ] \text {a n d 0 o t h e r w i s e}.
$$

Further by Lemma 5.1 we have

$$
\mathbb {P} [ i \in I _ {u} ] \geq 3 C \cdot \sigma (\mathbf {G} ^ {\prime} \mathbf {A}) \log (m / (\epsilon r)) \gamma^ {- 2} \quad \text {a n d} \quad \mathbb {P} [ i \in I _ {v} ] \geq 3 K \sqrt {m} (\mathbf {G A h}) _ {i} ^ {2} / \| \mathbf {G A h} \| _ {2} ^ {2}.
$$

For the vector  $w$  note that we have  $w_{i} = 3K / \sqrt{m}$  for each  $i\in I$ , and  $w_{i} = 0$  otherwise.

Here the set  $I$  is distributed as follows

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {P} [ i \in I ] = \mathbb {P} [ i \in I _ {u} \cup I v \cup I _ {w} ] \\ = 1 - \left(1 - \mathbb {P} [ i \in I _ {u} ]\right) \left(1 - \mathbb {P} [ i \in I _ {v} ]\right) \left(1 - \mathbb {P} [ i \in I _ {w} ]\right) \\ = 1 - \left(1 - u _ {i}\right) \left(1 - v _ {i}\right) \left(1 - w _ {i}\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

Distribution of matrix R Note that we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {P} [ i \in I ] \geq \min  \{1, \max  \{\mathbb {P} [ i \in I _ {u} ], \mathbb {P} [ i \in I _ {v} ], \mathbb {P} [ i \in I _ {w} ] \} \} \\ \geq \min  \{1, \left(\mathbb {P} [ i \in I _ {u} ] + \mathbb {P} [ i \in I _ {v} ] + \mathbb {P} [ i \in I _ {w} ]) / 3 \right. \} \\ = \min  \{1, (u _ {i} + v _ {i} + w _ {i}) / 3 \}, \\ \end{array}
$$

thus the probability used in Line 12 is well defined (i.e. at most 1). Hence Line 12 ensures that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbb {P} \left[ \mathbf {R} _ {i, i} \neq 0 \right] = \mathbb {P} [ i \in I ] \cdot \frac {\min  \left\{1 , (u _ {i} + v _ {i} + w _ {i}) / 3 \right\}}{1 - (1 - u _ {i}) (1 - v _ {i}) (1 - w _ {i})} \\ = \min  \left\{1, \left(u _ {i} + v _ {i} + w _ {i}\right) / 3 \right\} \\ \end{array}
$$

Further, the non-zero  $\mathbf{R}_{i,i}$  are set to exactly the inverse of that probability and we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \min  \left\{1, \left(u _ {i} + v _ {i} + w _ {i}\right) / 3 \right\} \\ \geq \min  \left\{1, C \sigma (\mathbf {G} ^ {\prime} \mathbf {A}) _ {i} \log (m / (\epsilon r)) \gamma^ {- 2} + K / \sqrt {m} + K \sqrt {m} (\mathbf {G} \mathbf {A} h) _ {i} ^ {2} / \| \mathbf {G} \mathbf {A} h \| _ {2} ^ {2} \right\} \\ \end{array}
$$

so  $\mathbf{R}$  has the desired distribution.

Sparsity of  $\mathbf{R}$  and complexity bound The number of nonzero entries of  $\mathbf{R}$  can w.h.p. be bounded by

$$
\begin{array}{l} \tilde {O} (n \log (m / (\epsilon r)) \gamma^ {- 2} \log W) + O (K \sqrt {m} \log n) + O (K \sqrt {m} \log n) \\ = \tilde {O} (n \log (m / (\epsilon r)) \log W + K \sqrt {m}), \\ \end{array}
$$

via Lemma 5.1 and  $C = O(1)$

The complexity is dominated by the two sample methods of Lemma 5.1 whose complexity is bounded by

$$
\tilde {O} \left(n \log \left(m / (\epsilon r)\right) \log W + K \sqrt {m}\right).
$$

# 8.2 Log Barrier Short Step

Here we show how to efficiently implement the log barrier based IPM, formalized by Algorithm 2. Note that, Algorithm 2 does not specify how to perform the computations, rather it simply specifies a sufficient set of conditions for the method to perform as desired. Consequently, in this section we show how to implement these computations using our data structures.

To be more precise, consider Line 4 of Algorithm 2, which specifies to find some  $\overline{s} \approx_{\epsilon} s$ . The algorithm does not explain how to find such an approximation, so we must implement this.   
ourselves. Note that in Algorithm 2,  $s$  is defined incrementally via

$$
s ^ {(\mathrm {n e w})} \gets s + \gamma \mathbf {A} \overline {{\mathbf {H}}} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {X}}} \nabla \Phi (\overline {{v}}) / \| \nabla \Phi (\overline {{v}}) \| _ {2}
$$

for any  $\overline{\mathbf{H}}\approx \mathbf{A}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{S}^{-1}\mathbf{A}$  and  $\overline{v}\approx xs / \mu$ . However, here  $h^\prime \coloneqq \gamma \mathbf{A}^\top \overline{\mathbf{X}}\nabla \Phi (\overline{v}) / \| \nabla \Phi (\overline{v})\| _2$  is exactly the return value of QUERYPRODUCT, promised by the data structure of Theorem 7.7. So we can efficiently compute  $h^\prime$  in each iteration of the IPM by using Theorem 7.7.

Next, we must compute  $h'' \coloneqq \overline{\mathbf{H}}^{-1}h'$ . This can be done by first sparsifying the weighted Laplacian matrix  $\mathbf{L} \coloneqq (\mathbf{A}^\top \mathbf{X}\mathbf{S}^{-1}\mathbf{A})$  via leverage score sampling<sup>16</sup>. This sampling can be performed efficiently via the LEVERAGESCORESAMPLE method of the data structure of Lemma 5.1. After sparsifying the Laplacian  $\mathbf{L}$  we can apply a Laplacian solver (Lemma 2.1) to compute  $h''$  efficiently.

At last, we must compute  $s + \mathbf{A}h'$ . Note however, that Algorithm 2 never requires the exact vector  $s$ , and it always suffices to just know some element-wise approximation  $\overline{s} \approx s$ . Hence the problem we actually need to solve is to maintain an element-wise approximation of the sum  $s = s^{(\mathrm{init})} + \sum_{t=1}^{T} \mathbf{A}h''(t)$  (this represents the vector  $s$  after  $T$  steps of the IPM), for a sequence of vectors  $h'''(1), \ldots, h'''(T) \in \mathbb{R}^n$ . That is exactly the guarantee of the data structure of Theorem 6.1.

The only thing left to do is to maintain a similar approximation  $\overline{x} \approx x$  of the primal solution via Theorem 7.7. Then we have an efficient implementation of Algorithm 2.

A formal description of this implementation can be found in Algorithm 8 and we are left with proving that this algorithm does indeed implement all steps of Algorithm 2. It is difficult to analyze the complexity per call to Algorithm 8, as many complexity bounds of the used data structures only bound the overall time over several iterations. Consequently, we defer the complexity analysis to the next section, where we analyze the overall complexity of several consecutive calls to Algorithm 8. For now we only prove the correctness of Algorithm 8 in Lemma 8.3.

Lemma 8.3. Algorithm 8 implements Algorithm 2, assuming the data structures are initialized as stated in Algorithm 8.

Algorithm 8: Implementation of Algorithm 2  
1 global variables  
/* Imported from Algorithm 9  
2  $D^{(x,\nabla)}$  instance of Theorem 7.7, initialized on  $(\mathbf{A}, x^{(\mathrm{init})}, \gamma x^{(\mathrm{init})}, x^{(\mathrm{init})} s^{(\mathrm{init})} / \mu^{(\mathrm{init})}, \gamma / 16)$   
3  $D^{(s)}$  instance of Theorem 6.1, initialized on  $(\mathbf{A}, s^{(\mathrm{init})}, \gamma / 16)$   
4  $D^{(\mathrm{sample})}$  instance of Lemma 5.1, initialized on  $(\mathbf{A}, 1 / s^{(\mathrm{init})})$   
5  $D^{(\mathrm{sample}, \sigma)}$  instance of Lemma 5.1, initialized on  $(\mathbf{A}, \sqrt{x^{(\mathrm{init})} / s^{(\mathrm{init})}})$   
6  $\Delta \in \mathbb{R}^n$  maintained to be  $\mathbf{A}^\top x - b$   
7  $\mu, \overline{\mu} \in \mathbb{R}$  progress parameter  $\mu$  of the IPM and its approximation  $\overline{\mu} \approx_{\gamma / 8} \mu$   
8  $\overline{x}, \overline{s} \in \mathbb{R}^m$  element-wise approximations  $\overline{x} \approx_{\gamma / 8} x$  and  $\overline{s} \approx_{\gamma / 8} s$   
9  $\gamma$  accuracy parameter  
10 procedure SHORTSTEP( $\mu^{(\mathrm{new})} > 0$ )  
/* Update  $\overline{\mu}$  and data structures that depend on it.  
if  $\overline{\mu} \not\approx_{\gamma / 8} \mu^{(\mathrm{new})}$  then  
 $\overline{\mu} \leftarrow \mu^{(\mathrm{new})}$   
for  $i \in [m]$  do  $D^{(x,\nabla)}$ . UPDATE $(i, \gamma \overline{x}_i, (\overline{x}_i \overline{s}_i) / \overline{\mu})$ ;  
/* Leverage Score Sampling to sparsify  $(\mathbf{A}^\top \mathbf{X} \mathbf{S}^{-1} \mathbf{A})$  with  $\gamma / 2$  spectral approximation  
/* Perform SHORTSTEP (Algorithm 2)  
 $h' \leftarrow D^{(x,\nabla)}$ .QUERYSCORESAMPLE $(c\gamma^{-2} \log n)$  for some large enough constant  $c > 0$ $v \leftarrow D^{(\mathrm{sample}, \sigma)}$ .LEVERAGESCOREBOUND $(c\gamma^{-2} \log n, I_v)$ $v_i \leftarrow 1 / v_i$  for  $i$  with  $v_i \neq 0$   
/* Perform SHORTSTEP (Algorithm 2)  
 $h' \leftarrow D^{(x,\nabla)}$ .QUERYPRODUCT() //  $h' = \gamma A^\top \overline{\mathbf{X}} \nabla \Phi(\overline{v}) / \| \nabla \Phi(\overline{v})\|_2$ $h'' \leftarrow$  solve  $(\mathbf{A}^\top \mathbf{V} \mathbf{A})^{-1}(h' + \Delta)$  with  $\gamma / 2$  accuracy via Laplacian Solver.  
//  $h'' = \overline{\mathbf{H}}^{-1}(h' + (\mathbf{A}^\top x - b))$ ,  $\delta_r = S^{-1} A h''$ $\mathbf{R} \leftarrow$  SAMPLEPRIMAL $(1, D^{(\mathrm{sample})}, D^{(\mathrm{sample}, \sigma)}, h'')$ $x^{(\mathrm{tmp})}, I_x \leftarrow D^{(x,\nabla)}$ .QUERYSUM $(-R \overline{\mathbf{X}} \mathbf{S}^{-1} A h'')$ $s^{(\mathrm{tmp})}, I_s \leftarrow D^{(s)}$ .ADD $(\mathbf{A}^\top \mathbf{V} \mathbf{A})^{-1} h', \gamma / 16)$ $\Delta \leftarrow \Delta + h' - A^\top R \overline{\mathbf{X}} S^{-1} A h''$  // Maintain  $\Delta = A^\top x - b$   
/* Update  $\overline{x}, \overline{s}$  and data structures that depend on them.  
for  $i \in I_x \cup I_s$  do  
if  $x_i^{(\mathrm{tmp})} \not\approx_{\gamma / 16} \overline{x}_i$  or  $s_i^{(\mathrm{tmp})} \not\approx_{\gamma / 16} \overline{s}_i$  then  
 $\overline{x}_i \leftarrow x_i^{(\mathrm{tmp})}, \overline{s}_i \leftarrow s_i^{(\mathrm{tmp})}$ $D^{(x,\nabla)}$ .UPDATE $(i, \gamma \overline{x}_i, (\overline{x}_i \overline{s}_i) / \overline{\mu})$ $D^{(\mathrm{sample})}$ .SCALE $(i, 1 / \overline{s}_i)$ $D^{(\mathrm{sample}, \sigma)}$ .SCALE $(i, \sqrt{\overline{x}_i / \overline{s}_i})$

Proof. As Algorithm 8 uses many different data structures that share similar variable names (e.g. variable  $g$  occurs in Lemma 5.1 and Theorems 6.1 and 7.7), we will use the notation  $D$ . var to refer to variable  $var$  of data structure  $D$ . For example  $D^{\mathrm{(sample)}}$ .  $g$  refers to variable  $g$  of data structure  $D^{\mathrm{(sample)}}$  (which is an instance of Lemma 5.1).

Assumptions By Line 2 to Line 8 of Algorithm 8, we assume that the following assumptions hold true at the start of the first call to SHORTSTEP (Algorithm 8).

$$
\bar {x} \approx_ {\gamma / 8} x, \bar {s} \approx_ {\gamma / 8} s, \bar {\mu} \approx_ {\gamma / 8} \mu \tag {61}
$$

$$
\Delta = \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x - b \tag {62}
$$

$$
D ^ {(x, \nabla)}. g = \gamma \bar {x}, D ^ {(x, \nabla)}. z = \gamma \bar {x} s / (\bar {\mu}), D ^ {(x, \nabla)}. \epsilon = \gamma / 1 6 \tag {63}
$$

$$
D ^ {\left(\text {s a m p l e}, \sigma\right)}. g = \sqrt {\bar {x} s ^ {- 1}} \tag {64}
$$

$$
D ^ {(s a m p l e)}. g = \bar {s} ^ {- 1} \tag {65}
$$

We prove further below that these assumptions also hold true for all further calls to SHORTSTEP (Algorithm 8). However, first we prove that, if these assumptions are satisfied, then SHORTSTEP (Algorithm 8) performs the computations required by the IPM of Algorithm 2.

Algorithm 8 implements Algorithm 2 We argue the correctness line by line of Algorithm 2. Line 4 (Algorithm 2) is satisfied by assumption (61). Line 5 and Line 6 (Algorithm 2) asks us to compute

$$
g \leftarrow - \gamma \nabla \Phi (\bar {v}) / \| \nabla \Phi (\bar {v}) \|
$$

for  $\overline{v} \approx_{\gamma} xs / \mu$ . While we do not compute  $g$ , our implementation does compute  $\mathbf{A}\overline{\mathbf{X}} g$  as follows: Line 17 of Algorithm 8 computes

$$
h ^ {\prime} = \gamma \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {X}}} \nabla \Phi (\overline {{v}}) / \| \nabla \Phi (\overline {{v}}) \| _ {2} = - \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {X}}} g
$$

for some  $\overline{v} \approx_{\gamma/2} D^{(x,\nabla)}.z = \overline{xs}/(\overline{\mu}) \approx_{\gamma/2} xs/\mu$  by the guarantees of Theorem 7.7 and assumptions (63).

By Line 7 and Line 8 of Algorithm 2 we must obtain a matrix  $\overline{\mathbf{H}}\approx_{\gamma}\mathbf{A}^{\top}\overline{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{S}^{-1}\mathbf{A}$ . In our implementation Algorithm 8 this is done in Line 16, where a vector  $v$  is constructed with

$v_{i} = 1 / p_{i}$  with probability  $p_i\geq \min \{1,c\cdot \sigma (\overline{\mathbf{X}}^{1 / 2}\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{1 / 2}\mathbf{A})_i\log n\}$  and  $v_{i} = 0$  otherwise.

by the guarantee of Lemma 5.1 and assumption (64). Thus with high probability  $(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{V}\mathbf{A})\approx_{\gamma /2}(\mathbf{A}\overline{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{S}^{-1}\mathbf{A})$  , so by applying a  $\gamma /2$  -accurate Laplacian Solver (Lemma 2.1) (i.e. in Line 18) we are able to represents some

$$
\overline {{\mathbf {H}}} ^ {- 1} \approx_ {\gamma} (\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {X S}}} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1}
$$

For  $\overline{\mathbf{A}} := \overline{\mathbf{X}}^{1/2}\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{1/2}\mathbf{A}$ ,  $\mathbf{W} := \mu^{-1}\overline{\mathbf{X}}\overline{\mathbf{S}}$ , Line 9 of Algorithm 2 wants us to compute

$$
\begin{array}{l} \delta_ {r} = \overline {{\mathbf {W}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \overline {{\mathbf {A H}}} ^ {- 1} \overline {{\mathbf {A}}} ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {W}}} ^ {1 / 2} g \\ + \mu^ {- 1 / 2} \overline {{\mathbf {W}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \overline {{\mathbf {A H}}} ^ {- 1} (\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x - b) \\ \end{array}
$$

This is done implicitly in Line 18 of our implementation Algorithm 8. By assumption (62) we have  $\Delta = \mathbf{A}^{\top}x - b$ , thus Line 18 computes  $h''$  with

$$
h ^ {\prime \prime} = \overline {{\mathbf {H}}} ^ {- 1} (h ^ {\prime} + \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x - b),
$$

so

$$
\begin{array}{l} \overline {{\mathbf {S}}} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A} h ^ {\prime \prime} = \overline {{\mathbf {S}}} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A} \overline {{\mathbf {H}}} ^ {- 1} \left(h ^ {\prime} + \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x - b\right) \\ = \overline {{\mathbf {S}}} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A} \overline {{\mathbf {H}}} ^ {- 1} (\gamma \mathbf {A} \overline {{\mathbf {X}}} \nabla \Phi (\bar {v}) / \| \nabla \Phi (\bar {v}) \| + \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x - b) \\ = (\overline {{\mathbf {X S}}} / \mu) ^ {- 1 / 2} (\overline {{\mathbf {X}}} ^ {1 / 2} \overline {{\mathbf {S}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {A}) \overline {{\mathbf {H}}} ^ {- 1} (\overline {{\mathbf {X}}} ^ {1 / 2} \overline {{\mathbf {S}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {A}) ^ {\top} (\overline {{\mathbf {X S}}} / \mu) ^ {1 / 2} \nabla \Phi (\bar {v}) / \| \nabla \Phi (\bar {v}) \| \\ + \mu^ {- 1 / 2} (\overline {{\mathbf {X S}}} / \mu) ^ {- 1 / 2} (\overline {{\mathbf {X}}} ^ {1 / 2} \overline {{\mathbf {S}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {A}) \overline {{\mathbf {H}}} ^ {- 1} (\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x - b) \\ = \mathbf {W} ^ {- 1 / 2} \overline {{\mathbf {A} \mathbf {H}}} ^ {- 1} \overline {{\mathbf {A}}} ^ {\top} \mathbf {W} ^ {1 / 2} \nabla \Phi (\bar {v}) / \| \nabla \Phi (\bar {v}) \| \\ + \mu^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {W} ^ {- 1 / 2} \overline {{\mathbf {A H}}} ^ {- 1} (\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x - b) \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
=: \delta_ {r}.
$$

Thus we have access to  $\delta_r$  via  $\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{-1}\mathbf{A}h^{\prime \prime}$ .

Line 11 of Algorithm 2 wants us to construct a random diagonal matrix  $\mathbf{R}$  where  $\mathbf{R}_{i,i} = 1 / q_i$  with probability  $q_{i}$  and  $\mathbf{R}_{i,i} = 0$  otherwise, where

$$
q _ {i} \geq \min \{1, \sqrt {m} ((\delta_ {r}) _ {i} ^ {2} / \| \delta_ {r} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + 1 / m) + C \cdot \sigma_ {i} (\overline {{\mathbf {X}}} ^ {1 / 2} \overline {{\mathbf {S}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {A}) \log (m / (\epsilon r)) \gamma^ {- 2} \}
$$

By assumption (65) and (64) we have  $D^{\mathrm{(sample)}}.g = \overline{s}^{-1}$  and  $D^{\mathrm{(sample,}\sigma)}.g = \sqrt{\overline{x}\overline{s}^{-1}}$ , so by Line 19 Line 19 of Algorithm 8 returns  $\mathbf{R}$  with the desired properties.

Line 12 and Line 14 of Algorithm 2 wants us to compute

$$
x ^ {(\mathrm {n e w})} \gets x + \overline {{\mathbf {X}}} (g - \overline {{\mathbf {R}}} \delta_ {r}).
$$

By Theorem 7.7 and assumption (63) Line 20 of our implementation Algorithm 8 computes  $x^{\mathrm{(tmp)}}$  with

$$
x ^ {(\mathrm {t m p})} \approx_ {\gamma / 1 6} x + \overline {{\mathbf {X}}} g - \mathbf {R} \overline {{\mathbf {X S}}} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A} h ^ {\prime \prime} = x + \overline {{\mathbf {X}}} (g - \overline {{\mathbf {R}}} \delta_ {r}).
$$

Line 13 and Line 14 of Algorithm 2 asks us to compute

$$
s ^ {\mathrm {(n e w)}} \gets s + \overline {{\mathbf {S}}} \delta_ {p}
$$

$$
\delta_ {p} := \overline {{\mathbf {W}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \overline {{\mathbf {A H}}} ^ {- 1} \overline {{\mathbf {A}}} ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {W}}} ^ {1 / 2} g
$$

By Theorem 6.1 and assumption (61) Line 21 of our implementation Algorithm 8 computes  $s^{\mathrm{(tmp)}}$  with

$$
\begin{array}{l} s ^ {(\mathrm {t m p})} \approx_ {\gamma / 1 6} s + \mathbf {A} \overline {{\mathbf {H}}} ^ {- 1} h ^ {\prime} = s + \mathbf {A} \overline {{\mathbf {H}}} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {X}}} g \\ = s + \overline {{\mathbf {S}}} (\overline {{\mathbf {X S}}} / \mu) ^ {- 1 / 2} (\overline {{\mathbf {X}}} ^ {1 / 2} \overline {{\mathbf {S}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {A}) \overline {{\mathbf {H}}} ^ {- 1} (\overline {{\mathbf {X}}} ^ {1 / 2} \overline {{\mathbf {S}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \mathbf {A}) ^ {\top} (\overline {{\mathbf {X S}}} / \mu) ^ {1 / 2} g \\ = s + \overline {{\mathbf {S}}} \overline {{\mathbf {W}}} ^ {- 1 / 2} \overline {{\mathbf {A H}}} ^ {- 1} \overline {{\mathbf {A}}} ^ {\top} \overline {{\mathbf {W}}} ^ {1 / 2} g = s + \overline {{\mathbf {S}}} \delta_ {p} \\ \end{array}
$$

Assumption on  $\overline{x}$ ,  $\overline{s}$ ,  $\overline{\mu}$ : Previously we assumed  $\overline{x} \approx_{\gamma/8} x$ ,  $\overline{s} \approx_{\gamma/8} s$ , and  $\overline{\mu} \approx_{\gamma/16}$ . Here we show that this assumption is true by induction, that is, if it was true before calling SHORTSTEP, then it is also true after executing SHORTSTEP.

We previously argued that  $x^{\mathrm{(tmp)}} \approx_{\gamma / 16} x^{\mathrm{(new)}}$  and  $s^{\mathrm{(tmp)}} \approx_{\gamma / 16} s^{\mathrm{(new)}}$ . So by the FOR-loop of Line 23 we have  $\overline{x} \approx_{\gamma / 16} x^{\mathrm{(tmp)}}$ , and thus  $\overline{x} \approx_{\gamma / 8} x^{\mathrm{(new)}}$ . Likewise  $\overline{s} \approx_{\gamma / 8} s^{\mathrm{(new)}}$ .

The assumption  $\overline{\mu} \approx_{\gamma / 16}$  is true by Line 11.

Assumption on  $D^{(\mathrm{sample},\sigma)}$ ,  $D^{(\mathrm{sample})}$ , and  $D^{(x,\nabla)}$ : All data structures that depend on  $\overline{x}$  and  $\overline{s}$  are updated, whenever an entry of  $\overline{x}$  or  $\overline{s}$  changes (see Line 23). So the assumption that  $D^{(\mathrm{sample},\sigma)}.g = \sqrt{\overline{x}/\overline{s}}$ ,  $D^{(\mathrm{sample})}.g = 1/\overline{s}$ ,  $D^{(x,\nabla)}.z = \overline{x}\overline{z}/\overline{\mu}$  and  $D^{(x,\nabla)}.g = \gamma\overline{x}$ , are all still satisfied after the execution of SHORTSTEP.

Likewise, whenever  $\overline{\mu}$  changes we set  $D^{(x,\nabla)}.z = \overline{x} z / \overline{\mu}$ .

Assumption  $\Delta = \mathbf{A}^{\top}x - b$ : If  $\Delta = \mathbf{A}^{\top}x - b$  initially, then after Line 22 we have  $\Delta = \mathbf{A}^{\top}x^{(\mathrm{new})} - b$  for  $x^{(\mathrm{new})} = x + \overline{\mathbf{X}} g - \mathbf{R}\overline{\mathbf{X}}\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{-1}\mathbf{A}h''$ . Thus we always maintain  $\mathbf{A}^{\top}x - b$ , whenever  $x$  changes.
