Algorithm 9: Implementation of Algorithm 1  
1 global variables   
2  $D^{(x,\nabla)}$  instance of Theorem 7.7   
3  $D^{(s)}$  instance of Theorem 6.1   
4  $D^{(\mathrm{sample})}$  instance of Lemma 5.1   
5  $D^{(\mathrm{sample},\sigma)}$  instance of Theorem B.1   
6  $\mathbf{A}\in \mathbb{R}^{m\times n}$ $b\in \mathbb{R}^n$  linear constraints  $\mathbf{A}^{\top}x = b$    
7  $\Delta \in \mathbb{R}^n$  maintained to be  $\mathbf{A}^{\top}x - b$    
8  $\mu ,\overline{\mu}\in \mathbb{R}$  progress parameter  $\mu$  of the IPM and its approximation  $\overline{\mu}$    
9  $\overline{x},\overline{s}\in \mathbb{R}^m$  element-wise approximations of  $x$  and  $s$    
10  $\epsilon ,\lambda ,\gamma ,r\in \mathbb{R}_{+}$  accuracy parameters   
11 procedure PATHFOLLOWING(x(init),s(init),  $\mu (\mathrm{init}),\mu (\mathrm{end})$    
12  $\epsilon \leftarrow \frac{\log n}{80},\lambda \leftarrow \frac{36\log(240m / \epsilon^2)}{\epsilon},\gamma \leftarrow \frac{\epsilon}{100\lambda},r\leftarrow \frac{\epsilon\gamma}{\sqrt{m}}$    
13  $\Delta = \mathbf{A}^{\top}x^{(\mathrm{init})} - b$    
14  $\mu \leftarrow \mu^{(\mathrm{init})},\overline{\mu}\leftarrow \mu^{(\mathrm{init})},\overline{x}\leftarrow x^{(\mathrm{init})},\overline{s}\leftarrow \overline{s}^{(\mathrm{init})},\overline{\tau}\leftarrow \tau$  /\* Initialize data structures \*/   
15  $D^{(x,\nabla)}.\mathrm{INITIALIZE}(A,x^{(\mathrm{init})},x^{(\mathrm{init})},x^{(\mathrm{init})} s^{(\mathrm{init})} / \mu^{(\mathrm{init})},\gamma /16)$    
16  $D^{(s)}.\mathrm{INITIALIZE}(A,s^{(\mathrm{init})},\gamma /16)$    
17  $D^{(\mathrm{sample})}.\mathrm{INITIALIZE}(A,1 / s^{(\mathrm{init})})$    
18  $D^{(\mathrm{sample},\sigma)}.\mathrm{INITIALIZE}(A,\sqrt{x^{(\mathrm{init})} / s^{(\mathrm{init})}})$  /\* Follow the Central Path \*/   
19 while  $\mu \neq \mu^{(\mathrm{end})}$  do   
20 for  $i = 1,2,\ldots ,\frac{\epsilon}{r}$  do   
21  $\mu \leftarrow$  median  $(\mu^{\mathrm{(end)}}),(1 - r)\mu ,(1 + r)\mu)$    
22 SHORTSTEP(μ)   
23  $p_1\gets D^{(x,\nabla)}.POTENTIAL()$    
24  $p_2\gets \Delta^\top (\mathbf{A}^\top \mathbf{G}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\Delta$  , where  $g$  is a leverage score sample from  $D^{(\mathrm{sample},\sigma)}$  .   
25 if  $p_1 > \exp (\frac{3\lambda\epsilon}{4} -\frac{1}{3})$  or  $p_2 > \gamma \epsilon \sqrt{\mu}\exp (-1 / 3)$  then break;   
26 if  $p_1 > \exp (\frac{\lambda\epsilon}{4} -\frac{1}{3})$  or  $p_2 > \gamma \epsilon \sqrt{\mu}\exp (-1 / 3)$  then Revert iterations of previous for loop   
27 /\* Compute and return exact result   
28  $x\gets D^{(x,\nabla)}.COMPUTERXACTSUM(),s\gets D^{(s)}.COMPUTERXACT()$    
29 return  $x,s$

# 8.3 Path Following Algorithm

Here we implement the path following procedure Algorithm 1 using our data structures. Note that Algorithm 1 consists of essentially a single FOR-loop, which calls SHORTSTEP (Algorithm 8), so the main task for the implementation is just the initialization of the data structures used in Algorithm 8. Lemma 8.4 then analyzes the complexity of Algorithm 9, i.e. our implementation of Algorithm 1.

Lemma 8.4. The time complexity of Algorithm 9 is

$$
\widetilde {O} \left(n \sqrt {m} \cdot | \log \mu^ {\mathrm {(e n d)}} / \mu^ {\mathrm {(i n i t)}} | \cdot (\log W + | \log \mu^ {\mathrm {(e n d)}} / \mu^ {\mathrm {(i n i t)}} |\right),
$$

where  $W$  is an upper bound on the ratio of largest to smallest entry in both  $x^{(\mathrm{init})}$  and  $s^{(\mathrm{init})}$ .

Proof. We analyze the overall complexity by first bounding the time for the initialization. Then

we bound the total time spend on the most inner FOR-loop, which also includes all calls to SHORTSTEP (Algorithm 8).

Initialization Algorithm 9 starts by computing  $\Delta = \mathbf{A}^{\top}x - b$ , which takes  $\widetilde{O}(m)$  time. The subsequent initializations of the data structures of Theorems 6.1 and 7.1 and lemma 5.1, require  $\widetilde{O}(m)$  as well.

Last line In the last line of Algorithm 9, we compute the exact  $x$  and  $s$  values, which by Theorem 7.7 and Theorem 6.1 require  $\widetilde{O}(m)$  time.

FOR-loop The most inner loop (Line 20) is repeated a total of

$$
K := O (r ^ {- 1} | \log \mu^ {(\mathrm {e n d})} / \mu^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})} |) = \tilde {O} (\sqrt {m} | \log \mu^ {(\mathrm {e n d})} / \mu^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})} |)
$$

times by Lemma 4.4. We now analyze the cost per iteration.

The cost for  $D^{(x,\nabla)}$ . POTENTIAL() is bounded by  $O(1)$ . Computing  $\Delta^{\top}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{G}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\Delta$  can be implemented via Laplacian Solver (Lemma 2.1), so the complexity is bounded by obtaining the leverage score sample  $g$ , which also bound the number of nonzero entries. By Lemma 5.1 the complexity is bounded by  $\widetilde{O}(n\log W')$ , where  $W'$  is the ratio of largest to smallest entry in  $\sqrt{x/s}$ . By Lemma 4.41 we can bound  $\log W'$  by  $\widetilde{O}(\log W + |\log \mu^{(\mathrm{end})} / \mu^{(\mathrm{init})}|)$ . Thus computing  $\Delta^{\top}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{V}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\Delta$  requires  $\widetilde{O}(n(\log W + |\log \mu^{(\mathrm{end})} / \mu^{(\mathrm{init})}|))$  time.

We are left with analyzing the cost of SHORTSTEP (Algorithm 8).

Updating the data structures Performing  $D^{(x,\nabla)}$ . UPDATE for all  $i \in [m]$  in Line 11 of Algorithm 8 takes  $O(m)$  time, but it happens only once every  $O(r / \gamma) = \tilde{O}(\sqrt{m})$  calls to SHORTSTEP, because  $\mu$  changes by an  $(1 \pm r)$  factor between any two calls to SHORTSTEP. The total cost of this call to  $D^{(x,\nabla)}$ . UPDATE over all calls to SHORTSTEP together can thus be bounded by  $\tilde{O}(\sqrt{m}K)$ .

Next consider the calls to UPDATE and SCALE of our various data structures in Line 24 of Algorithm 8. Note that throughout  $K$  calls to SHORTSTEP, the entries of  $s$  can change at most  $O(K^2 /\gamma^2)$  times by a factor of  $(1\pm \gamma /16)$ , because  $\| \mathbf{S}^{-1}\delta_1\| _2^2 < 1 / 2$  by Lemma 4.6. On the other hand, the entries of  $x$  can change at most  $\widetilde{O} (Kn(\log (W) + |\log (\mu^{(\mathrm{init})} / \mu^{(\mathrm{end})})|) + K^2 /\gamma^2)$  times by a factor of  $(1\pm \gamma /16)$ . This is because  $x$  changes by  $\gamma \overline{\mathbf{X}}\nabla \Phi (\overline{v}) / \| \nabla \Phi (\overline{v})\| _2 - \mathbf{R}\overline{\mathbf{X}}\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{-1}\mathbf{A}h''$ , where for the first term we have  $\| \mathbf{X}^{-1}\gamma \overline{\mathbf{X}}\nabla \Phi (\overline{v}) / \| \nabla \Phi (\overline{v})\| _2\| _2 < 2\gamma$  and the second term has at most  $\widetilde{O} (n(\log (W) + |\log (\mu^{(\mathrm{init})} / \mu^{(\mathrm{end})})|))$  non-zero entries.

So the total number of times the condition in Line 24 is satisfied can be bounded by  $\widetilde{O}(Kn(\log(W) + |\log(\mu^{(\mathrm{init})} / \mu^{(\mathrm{end})}|) + K^2 / \gamma^2)$ . Thus the cost for all calls to SCALE and UPDATE of the data structures is bounded by  $\widetilde{O}(K^2 + Kn(\log(W) + |\log(\mu^{(\mathrm{init})} / \mu^{(\mathrm{end})}|))$ , as each call to SCALE and UPDATE of any of the used data structures costs  $\widetilde{O}(1)$ , and  $\gamma = \widetilde{\Omega}(1)$ .

$D^{(\mathrm{sample})}$  and  $D^{(\mathrm{sample},\sigma)}$  data structure As argued before, the ratio of largest to smallest weight of  $x$  and  $s$  can be bounded via Lemma 4.41. Hence performing the leverage score sample to obtain  $v$  in Line 14 to Line 16 of Algorithm 8 requires at most  $\tilde{O}(n(\log(W) + |\log(\mu^{(\mathrm{init})} / \mu^{(\mathrm{end})}|))$  time by Lemma 5.1. Likewise, each call to SAMPLEPRIMAL in Line 19 of Algorithm 8 requires at most  $\tilde{O}(n(\log(W) + |\log(\mu^{(\mathrm{init})} / \mu^{(\mathrm{end})}|))$  time by Lemma 8.2, which is also a bound on the number of non-zero entries in matrix  $\mathbf{R}$ . This results in a total of  $\tilde{O}(nK(\log(W) + |\log(\mu^{(\mathrm{init})} / \mu^{(\mathrm{end})}|))$  time after all  $K$  calls to SHORTSTEP.

$D^{(x,\nabla)}$  data structure The total cost of  $D^{(x,\nabla)}$ .QUERYPRODUCT and QUERYSUM is bounded by

$$
\tilde {O} (K n + K n (\log (W) + | \log (\mu^ {(\text {i n i t})} / \mu^ {(\text {e n d})}) |) + K ^ {2})
$$

by Theorem 7.7. This is because (i) the number of non-zero entries in  $-\mathbf{R}\overline{\mathbf{X}}\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{-1}\mathbf{A}h''$  is bounded by  $\widetilde{O}(Kn(\log(W) + |\log(\mu^{(\mathrm{init})} / \mu^{(\mathrm{end})}|))$ , and (ii) the vector  $v^{(\ell)}$  and  $x^{(\ell-1)}$  in Theorem 7.7 are exactly the vectors  $\gamma \overline{\mathbf{X}}\nabla \Phi(\overline{v}) / \| \nabla \Phi(\overline{v})\|_2$  and  $x$  in Algorithm 8 during the  $\ell$ -th call to SHORT-STEP. By  $\overline{x} \approx_{\gamma/8} x$  we have  $\| \mathbf{X}^{-1}\gamma \overline{\mathbf{X}}\nabla \Phi(\overline{v}) / \| \nabla \Phi(\overline{v})\|_2 = O(\gamma)$  which yields the complexity bound above.

Algorithm 10: Minimum cost perfect  $b$ -matching algorithm  
1 global variables   
2 procedure MINIMUMCOSTPERFECTBMATCHING  $(G = (U,V,E),b\in \mathbb{Z}^{U\cup V},c\in \mathbb{Z}^{E})$ $^ { / * }$  Given minimum cost perfect  $b$  -matching instance on bipartite graph  $G$  with cost vector  $c$  . \*/   
3 Construct  $G^{\prime} = (U\cup V\cup \{z\} ,E^{\prime})$ $x,s,c^{\prime}$ $d^{\prime}$  as in Lemma 8.7.   
4 Let  $c''\in \mathbb{R}^{E'}$  with  $c_{u,v}^{\prime \prime} = c_{u,v}$  and  $c_{u,z}^{\prime \prime} = c_{z,v}^{\prime \prime} = \| b\| _1\| c\|_{\infty}$  for all  $u\in U$ $v\in V$    
5 Modify cost vector  $c''$  as in Lemma 8.10 to create unique optimum.   
6 Let A be the incidence matrix of  $G^{\prime}$  and rename  $d^{\prime}$  to  $b$    
/\* Solve the linear program min  $c^{\prime \prime \top}x$  for  $\mathbf{A}^{\top}x = b$  and  $x\geq 0$  \*/   
7  $x,s\gets$  PATHFOLLOWING(x,s,1,  $45\| c^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\| b\|_{\infty})$    
8  $s\gets s + c^{\prime \prime} - c^{\prime}$    
9  $x,s\gets$  PATHFOLLOWING(x,s,45||c'||∞||b||∞,  $\frac{1}{24n\cdot 12m^2(||b||_\infty||c''||_\infty)^3}$    
10  $x\gets$  feasible solution via Lemma 8.9.   
/\*  $x$  is now a feasible flow with  $c^{\prime \prime \top}x\leq \mathrm{OPT} + \frac{1}{12m^2(||b||_\infty||c''||_\infty)^3}$  \*/   
11 Round entries of  $x$  to the nearest integer.   
12 return  $x$

$D^{(s)}$  data structure The total of all calls to  $D^{(s)}$ . Add require at most

$$
\begin{array}{l} \widetilde {O} \left(K ^ {2} \| \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \delta_ {s} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + K n (\log W + | \log (\mu^ {(\text {i n i t})} / \mu^ {(\text {e n d})}) |\right) \\ = \widetilde {O} \left(K ^ {2} + K n \left(\log W + \left| \log \left(\mu^ {\text {(i n i t)}} / \mu^ {\text {(e n d)}}\right) \right|\right)\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

by Theorem 6.1 and  $\| \mathbf{S}^{-1}\delta_s\| _2 = O(1)$  by Lemma 4.6.

Updating  $\Delta$  Updating  $\Delta$  in Line 22 takes  $O(n + \| \mathbf{R}\| _0) = \widetilde{O} (n(\log (W) + |\log (\mu^{(\mathrm{init})} / \mu^{(\mathrm{end})})|))$  time, where we bound  $\| \mathbf{R}\| _0$  via Lemma 8.2. The same bound holds for computing  $\mathbf{R}\overline{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{S}^{-1}\mathbf{A}h^{\prime \prime}$  in Line 20. After all calls to SHORTSTEP this contributes a total cost of  $\widetilde{O} (Kn(\log (W) + |\log (\mu^{(\mathrm{init})} / \mu^{(\mathrm{end})})|))$ .

Total complexity In summary, the time for all calls to SHORTSTEP together is bounded by

$$
\begin{array}{l} \tilde {O} (K ^ {2} + K n (\log W + | \log (\mu^ {\mathrm {(i n i t)}} / \mu^ {\mathrm {(e n d)}}) |) \\ = \widetilde {O} (n \sqrt {m} \cdot | \log (\mu^ {\mathrm {(e n d)}} / \mu^ {\mathrm {(i n i t)}}) | \cdot (\log W + | \log (\mu^ {\mathrm {(e n d)}} / \mu^ {\mathrm {(i n i t)}}) |)). \\ \end{array}
$$

This also subsumes the initialization cost.

# 8.4  $\tilde{O}(n\sqrt{m})$ -Time Algorithm

In this section we use the implemented IPM (Algorithm 9) to obtain an algorithm that solves minimum weight bipartite  $b$ -matching in  $\widetilde{O}((n\sqrt{m})\log^2 W)$  time. More accurately, we will prove the following theorem.

Theorem 8.5. Algorithm 10 finds a minimum weight perfect bipartite  $b$ -matching. On a graph with  $n$  nodes,  $m$  edges, and edge cost vector  $c \in \mathbb{Z}^m$ , the algorithm runs in  $\widetilde{O}(n\sqrt{m}\log^2(\|c\|_\infty \|b\|_\infty))$  time.

Outline of Theorem 8.5 Algorithm 10 can be outlined as follows: first we reduce the problem to uncapacitated minimum cost flows, so that we can represent the problem as a linear program of the form

$$
\min_{\mathbf{A}^{\top}x = b,x\geq 0}c^{\top}x,
$$

where  $\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$  is an incidence matrix and  $b \in \mathbb{R}^n$  are a demand vector.

We must then construct an initial feasible flow  $x \in \mathbb{R}^m$  and a feasible slack  $s \in \mathbb{R}^m$  of a dual solution, such that  $x_i s_i \approx \mu$  for all  $i \in [m]$  and some  $\mu \in \mathbb{R}$ . Unfortunately, we are not able to construct such an initial pair  $(x, s)$  for the given edge cost vector  $c \in \mathbb{R}^m$ . So instead, we construct an initial pairs for some other cost vector  $c' \in \mathbb{R}^m$  (Lemma 8.7).

We then use the IPM (Algorithm 9) to move this initial solution away from the optimal solution towards the center of the polytope. Once our intermediate solution pair  $(x,s)$  is sufficiently close to the center, then it does no longer matter which cost vector was used. So we are then able to switch the cost vector  $c'$  with the actual cost vector  $c$  (Lemma 8.8). After switching the cost vector, we then use the IPM (Algorithm 9) again to move our solution pair  $(x,s)$  towards the new optimal solution induced by  $c$ .

Note that the obtained primal solution  $x$  is not feasible. Lemma 4.4 only guarantees that  $x$  is close to a feasible solution. So we then use Lemma 8.9 to turn  $x$  into a nearby feasible solution  $x'$ .

The obtained solution  $x'$  is an almost optimal fractional solution. Via the Isolation-Lemma 8.10, we can simply round each entry of  $x'$  to the nearest integer to obtain an optimal integral solution.

Reduction to uncapacitated minimum flow We start the formal proof of Theorem 8.5 by proving the reduction of minimum-cost bipartite  $b$ -matching to uncapacitated minimum cost flows. Given a bipartite graph  $G = (U, V, E)$  and vector  $b \in \mathbb{N}^V$ , we construct the following flow instance.

Definition 8.6. Let  $G = (U, V, E)$  be a bipartite graph and  $b \in \mathbb{Z}^{U \cup V}$  a vector with  $\sum_{u \in U} b_u - \sum_{v \in V} b_v = 0$  (i.e., a  $b$ -matching instance).

We define the corresponding starred flow graph as  $G' = (V', E')$  with nodes  $V' = V \cup \{z\}$ , and directed edges  $E' = E \cup \{(u, z), (z, v) \mid u \in U, v \in V\}$  and demand vector  $d' \in \mathbb{R}^{V'}$  with  $d_v = b_v$  for  $v \in V$ ,  $d_u = -b_u$  for  $u \in U$  and  $d_z = 0$ .

The reduction for minimum cost  $b$ -matching to uncapacitated minimum cost flow is now trivially given. Given a cost vector  $c \in \mathbb{R}^E$  for the bipartite  $b$ -matching instance, we can define a cost vector  $c' \in \mathbb{R}^{E'}$  for the starred flow graph  $G'$ , where  $c_{u,v}' = c_{u,v}$ ,  $c_{u,z}' = c_{z,v}' = \|b\|_1 \|c\|_\infty$  for all  $u \in U$  and  $v \in V$ . Then any integral minimum cost flow on  $G'$  with cost less than  $\|b\|_1 \|c\|_\infty$  for cost vector  $c'$  results in a minimum weight perfect  $b$  matching on  $G$ . On the other hand, if no such flow exists, then  $G$  has no perfect  $b$ -matching.

Initial point Next, we use the following Lemma 8.7 to construct an initial point  $(x,s)$  for the IPM Algorithm 9 with  $x_{i}s_{i} = \mu$  for some  $\mu \in \mathbb{R}$  and all  $i\in [m]$ . Lemma 8.7 is proven in Appendix A.1.

Lemma 8.7. We are given a minimum weight perfect  $b$ -matching instance on graph  $G = (U, V, E)$  with cost vector  $c \in \mathbb{R}^E$ .

Let  $G' = (V', E')$  with demand vector  $d'$  be the corresponding starred flow graph (see Definition 8.6) and let  $n$  be the number of nodes of  $G'$  and  $m$  the number of edges.

For  $\tau(x, s) \coloneqq 1$ , we can construct in  $O(m)$  time a cost vector  $c' \in \mathbb{R}^{E'}$  for  $G'$  and a feasible primal dual solution pair  $(x, s)$  for the minimum cost flow problem on  $G'$  with cost vector  $c'$ , where the solution satisfies  $xs = \tau(x, s)$ . The cost vector  $c'$  satisfies

$$
\left\| b \right\| _ {\infty} ^ {- 1} \leq c ^ {\prime} \leq n.
$$

Switching the cost vector Lemma 8.7 returns some cost vector  $c'$  that differs from  $c$ . As outlined before and as can be seen in Line 8 of Algorithm 10, we will switch the cost vectors once our point  $(x, s)$  is far enough away from the optimum. Lemma 8.8, which formally shows that this switching is possible, is proven in Appendix A.2.

Lemma 8.8. Consider the function  $\tau(x, s)$  and a uncapacitated min-cost-flow instance as constructed in Lemma 8.15 or Lemma 8.7 and let  $\mathbf{A}$  be the incidence matrix of the underlying graph  $G'$ . Let  $c$  be the cost vector constructed in Lemma 8.15 and let  $c'$  be any other cost vector with  $\|c'\|_{\infty} \geq 1$ .

Assume we have a primal dual solution pair  $(x,s)$  for cost vector  $c$  and demand  $d$  with  $\| \mathbf{A}x - d\|_{(\mathbf{AXS}^{-1}\mathbf{A})^{-1}}\leq \sqrt{\mu} /4$  and  $xs\approx_{\epsilon /3}\mu \tau (x,s)$  for any  $\epsilon \leq 1$  and  $\mu \geq 45\| c'\|_{\infty}\| d\|_{\infty}\epsilon$

If we replace the cost vector  $c$  with cost vector  $c'$  then  $(x, s + c' - c)$  is a primal dual solution pair for the new min-cost-flow instance with cost vector  $c'$  and we have  $x(s + c' - c) \approx_{\epsilon/2} \mu \tau(x, s + c' - c)$ .

Rounding to an optimal integral solution After running the IPM for the cost vector  $c$  (i.e. Line 9 of Algorithm 10) we obtain a near optimal and near feasible fractional solution  $x$ . The following Lemma 8.9 shows that we can convert this near feasible solution to a truly feasible solution that is still near optimal. The proof is deferred to Appendix A.3.

Lemma 8.9. Consider any  $\epsilon >0$  and an uncapacitated min-cost flow instance on a starred flow graph (Definition 8.6) with cost vector  $c\in \mathbb{R}^E$ , demand vector  $d\in \mathbb{R}^V$  and the property that any feasible flow  $f$  satisfies  $f\leq \| d\|_{\infty}$ .

Assume we are given a primal dual solution pair  $(x,s)$ , with  $xs\approx_{1 / 2}\mu \tau (x,s)$  and

$$
\frac {1}{\mu} \cdot \| \mathbf {A} x - d \| _ {(\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X} \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1}} ^ {2} \leq \frac {1}{1 0}
$$

for  $\mu \leq \frac{\epsilon}{24n}$ .

Let  $\delta = \frac{\epsilon^2}{12(\|c\|_{\infty}\|d\|_{\infty}m)^2}$ , then in  $\widetilde{O}(m\log \delta^{-1})$  time we can construct a feasible flow  $f$  with  $c^\top f \leq \mathrm{OPT} + \epsilon$ , where  $\mathrm{OPT}$  is the optimal value of the min-cost flow instance.

At last, we convert the feasible near optimal fractional solution  $x$  to a truly optimal integral solution via the Isolation-Lemma of [DS08]. Lemma 8.10 below shows that, if the flow instance is integral and the set of optimal flows has congestion at most  $W$  (where the congestion of a flow refers to the maximum flow value over all edges), then we can obtain an optimal feasible flow by (i) slightly perturbing the problem, (ii) solving the perturbed problem approximately with  $1 / \mathrm{poly}(mW)$  additive error, and (iii) rounding the flow on each edge to nearest integer. We show in Appendix A.4 how Lemma 8.10 is obtained via the Isolation-Lemma of [DS08, KS01].

Lemma 8.10. Let  $\Pi = (G, b, c)$  be an instance for minimum-cost flow problem where  $G$  is a directed graph with  $m$  edges, the demand vector  $b \in \{-W, \dots, W\}^V$  and the cost vector  $c \in \{-W, \dots, W\}^E$ . Further assume that all optimal flows have congestion at most  $W$ .

Let the perturbed instance  $\tilde{\Pi} = (G, b, \tilde{c})$  be such that  $\tilde{c}_e = c_e + z_e$  where  $z_e$  is a random number from the set  $\left\{\frac{1}{4m^2W^2}, \ldots, \frac{2mW}{4m^2W^2}\right\}$ . Let  $\tilde{f}$  be a feasible flow for  $\tilde{\Pi}$  whose cost is at most  $\mathrm{OPT}(\tilde{\Pi}) + \frac{1}{12m^2W^3}$  where  $\mathrm{OPT}(\tilde{\Pi})$  is the optimal cost for problem  $\tilde{\Pi}$ . Let  $f$  be obtained by rounding the flow  $\tilde{f}$  on each edge to the nearest integer. Then, with probability at least  $1/2$ ,  $f$  is an optimal feasible flow for  $\Pi$ .

Proof of Theorem 8.5 We start by proving the correctness of Algorithm 10 in Lemma 8.11 by using the previously stated Lemmas 8.7 to 8.10. Then we analyze the complexity of Algorithm 10 in Lemma 8.12. Lemma 8.11 and Lemma 8.12 together form the proof of Algorithm 10.

Lemma 8.11. With high probability Algorithm 10 returns an optimal minimum weight perfect bipartite  $b$ -matching.

Proof. Given the bipartite graph  $G$  and vector  $b$ , Lemma 8.7 constructs the corresponding starred flow graph with demand vector  $d \in \mathbb{R}^n$ , a cost vector  $c' \in \mathbb{R}^m$ , and a feasible primal dual pair  $(x, s)$  with  $xs = 1$ . Thus PATHFOLLOWING in Line 7 returns  $x, s$  with

$$
x s \approx_ {\epsilon / 3} 4 5 \| c ^ {\prime} \| _ {\infty} \| b \| _ {\infty} =: \mu , \mathrm {a n d}
$$

$$
\mu^ {- 1 / 2} \| \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x - b \| _ {(\mathbf {A X S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1}} \leq \epsilon \gamma <   1 / 4
$$

by Lemma 4.4 and Lemma 4.5. By Lemma 8.8 we can switch the cost vector  $c'$  with  $c''$ , such that  $xs \approx_{\epsilon/2} \tau(x, s)$ . Then PATHFOLLOWING in Line 9 returns  $x, s$  with

$$
x s \approx_ {\epsilon / 3} \frac {1}{2 4 n \cdot 1 2 m ^ {2} (\| b \| _ {\infty} \| c ^ {\prime \prime} \| _ {\infty}) ^ {3}} =: \mu^ {\prime}, \mathrm {a n d}
$$

$$
\mu^ {\prime - 1 / 2} \| \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x - b \| _ {(\mathbf {A X S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1}} \leq \epsilon \gamma <   1 / 1 0
$$

by Lemma 4.4 and Lemma 4.5. Lemma 8.9 turns the given  $x$  into a feasible flow on graph  $G'$ , with

$$
c ^ {\prime \prime \top} x \leq \mathrm {O P T} + \frac {1}{1 2 m ^ {2} (\| b \| _ {\infty} \| c ^ {\prime \prime} \| _ {\infty}) ^ {3}}.
$$

Further we know that every optimal flow on that graph can have congestion at most  $\| b\|_{\infty}$ , as the congestion is bounded by the demand of the incident nodes. Thus by Lemma 8.10, we can round the entries of  $x$  to the nearest integer and obtain an optimal solution for the minimum cost flow with cost vector  $c''$ . Since  $c_{u,z}^{\prime \prime} = c_{z,v}^{\prime \prime} = n\| c\|_{\infty}$ , the optimal solution will not use any of the edges  $(u,z)$  or  $(z,v)$  for any  $u\in U$  or  $v\in V$ . Thus the obtained flow  $x$  can be interpreted as a perfect  $b$ -matching, because the demands  $d_u^\prime = -b_u$  and  $d_v^\prime = b_v$  match the given  $b$ -vector. Note that we only obtain the optimal solution with probability at least  $1 / 2$ , but we can repeat the algorithm  $O(\log n)$  times and return the minimum result, which is the optimal solution with high probability.

Lemma 8.12. Algorithm 10 runs in  $\widetilde{O}(n\sqrt{m}\log^2(\|c\|_\infty \|b\|_\infty))$  time.

Proof. Constructing the initial point via Lemma 8.15 takes  $O(m)$  time. Next, moving away from the optimal solution, switching the cost vector, and then moving towards the optimal solution takes  $O(n\sqrt{m}\log^2(\|c\|_\infty \|b\|_\infty))$  time by Lemma 8.4, as both  $|\log \mu^{(\mathrm{end})} / \mu^{(\mathrm{init})}|$  and  $\log W$  are bounded by  $\widetilde{O}(\log(\|c\|_\infty \|b\|_\infty))$ . At last, we construct a feasible  $x$  via Lemma 8.9 in  $\widetilde{O}(m\log(\|c\|_\infty \|b\|_\infty))$  time and rounding each entry of  $x$  to the nearest integer takes only  $O(m)$  time.

# 8.5 Nearly Linear Time Algorithm for Moderately Dense Graphs

In this section we improve the previous  $\tilde{O}(n\sqrt{m})$ -time algorithm of Theorem 8.5 to run in  $\tilde{O}(m + n^{1.5})$ -time instead. For that we will modify the SHORTSTEP algorithm (Algorithm 8) to use the faster improved IPM of Section 4.3 (Algorithm 3).

Note that Algorithm 3 (leverage score based IPM) is very similar to Algorithm 2 (log barrier based IPM). Hence when implementing Algorithm 3, we can start with the implementation of Algorithm 2 from Section 8.2 and perform a few modifications. The main difference is that Algorithm 3 uses  $(\nabla \Phi(\overline{v}))^{\flat(\overline{\tau})}$  for  $\overline{v} \approx xs / (\mu \tau(x,s))$  where  $\tau(x,s) = \sigma(\sqrt{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{S}^{-1}}\mathbf{A}) + n/m$ , while Algorithm 2 uses  $\nabla \Phi(\overline{v}) / \| \nabla \Phi(\overline{v}) \|_2$  for  $\overline{v} \approx xs$ . Previously, Algorithm 8 (the implementation of Algorithm 2) used Theorem 7.7 to maintain  $\nabla \Phi(\overline{v}) / \| \nabla \Phi(\overline{v}) \|_2$ . We now replace this data structure by Theorem 7.1 to maintain  $(\nabla \Phi(\overline{v}))^{\flat(\overline{\tau})}$  instead.

The next difference in implementation is, that we must have an accurate approximation  $\overline{\tau} \approx \tau(x,s)$ . Note that, while Lemma 5.1 does provide upper bounds on the leverage scores, it does not yield a good approximation. So we must use a different data structure for maintaining  $\overline{\tau} \approx \tau(x,s)$ . Such a data structure was previously given by [BLSS20], and we state these results in Appendix B. This data structure requires that  $\overline{x},\overline{s}$  are very accurate approximations of  $x,s$ , so another difference is that we use much smaller accuracy parameters than before. However,

the accuracy is smaller by only some polylog(n)-factor, so it will not affect the complexity besides an additional  $\widetilde{O}(1)$ -factor.

Algorithm 11 gives a formal description of our implementation of Algorithm 3 and Lemma 8.13 shows that this implementation is indeed correct.

Afterward, we show in Lemma 8.14 that the complexity of Algorithm 9 improves when using this new implementation of the faster IPM.

# Lemma 8.13. Algorithm 11 implements Algorithm 3.

Proof. Note that the only difference between Algorithm 2 and Algorithm 3 is the gradient. While in Algorithm 2 we use  $\nabla \Phi (\overline{v}) / \| \nabla \Phi (\overline{v})\| _2$  for  $\overline{v}\approx_{\gamma}xs / \mu$ , the faster Algorithm 3 uses  $(\nabla \Phi (\overline{v}))^{b(\widetilde{\tau})}$  for  $\overline{v}\approx_{\gamma}xs / (\mu \tau (x,s))$  and  $\widetilde{\tau}\approx_{\gamma}\tau (x,s)$ .

Likewise Algorithm 11 is almost the same as Algorithm 8. The main difference being that we replaced the data structure of Theorem 7.7 for maintaining  $\nabla \Phi (\overline{v}) / \| \nabla \Phi (\overline{v})\| _2$  by the data structure of Theorem 7.1 for maintaining  $(\nabla \Phi (\overline{v}))^{\flat (\overline{\tau})}$ . The next difference is that we now use Theorem B.1 to maintain the leverage scores.

Given that the rest of the implementation (Algorithm 11) of the LS-barrier method is identical to the implementation (Algorithm 8) of the log-barrier method, we only need to verify that these modifications maintain the desired values with sufficient accuracy.

Leverage Scores Note that Line 22 makes sure that Theorem B.1 always uses  $g = (\overline{x} / \overline{s})^{1 - 2\alpha}$ . As  $D^{(x, \nabla)}$  and  $D^{(s)}$  return  $\gamma / (10^5 \log^2 n)$  approximations and we change  $\overline{x}, \overline{s}$  whenever these approximations change by some  $\gamma / (10^5 \log^2 n)$  factor, we have  $(\overline{x} / \overline{s})^{1 - 2\alpha} \approx_{4\gamma / (10^5 \log^2 n)} (x / s)^{1 - 2\alpha}$ . This then implies

$$
\overline {{\tau}} \approx_ {\gamma / 2} \sigma (\overline {{\mathbf {X}}} ^ {1 / 2 - \alpha} \overline {{\mathbf {S}}} ^ {- 1 / 2 - \alpha} \mathbf {A}) \approx_ {4 \gamma / (1 0 ^ {5} \log^ {2} n)} \sigma (\overline {{\mathbf {X}}} ^ {1 / 2 - \alpha} \overline {{\mathbf {S}}} ^ {- 1 / 2 - \alpha} \mathbf {A}) =: \tau (x, s),
$$

assuming  $D^{(\tau)}$  (Theorem B.1) is initialized with accuracy  $\gamma / 4$ . Hence Theorem B.1 maintains  $\overline{\tau} \approx_{3\gamma / 4} \tau(x, s)$ .

Gradient In Line 10, Line 22 and Line 30 we make sure that Theorem 7.1 uses  $z = \overline{x s} /(\overline{\mu\tau})$  Hence the data structure maintains  $(\nabla \phi (\overline{v}))^{\flat (\widetilde{\tau})}$  for  $\overline{v}\approx_{\gamma /16}z = \overline{x s} /\left(\overline{\mu\tau}\right)\approx_{15\gamma /16}xs / \tau (x,s)$ , so  $\overline{v}\approx_{\gamma}xs / (\tau (x,s))$ . Further,  $\widetilde{\tau}\approx_{\gamma /(10^{5}\log^{2}n)}\overline{\tau}\approx_{3\gamma /4}\tau (x,s)$ , so  $\widetilde{\tau}\approx_{\gamma}\tau (x,s)$ .

![](images/e7b22e4d77292b5886c58043621764afe3b96c3c83649a43293815ed22d6e357.jpg)

We now show that the complexity of Algorithm 9 improves when using Algorithm 11 for the SHORTSTEP function.

Lemma 8.14. We can improve Algorithm 9 to run in

$$
\widetilde {O} \left((m + n ^ {1. 5}) \cdot | \log \mu^ {(\mathrm {e n d})} / \mu^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})} | \cdot (\log W + | \log \mu^ {(\mathrm {e n d})} / \mu^ {(\mathrm {i n i t})} |)\right)
$$

time, where  $\log W$  is a bound on the ratio of largest to smallest entry in  $x^{(\mathrm{init})}$  and  $s^{(\mathrm{init})}$ .

Proof. The main difference is that we run Algorithm 11 (the LS-barrier) for SHORTSTEP instead of Algorithm 8 (the log-barrier). For that we must first initialize Theorem B.1 via  $D^{(\tau)}$ . INITIaLize  $((x^{\mathrm{(init)}} / s^{\mathrm{(init)}})^{1 / 2 - \alpha}, \gamma /4)$ . This additional initialization takes  $\widetilde{O}(m)$  time. We also run Theorem 7.1 instead of Theorem 7.7 to maintain  $(\nabla \Phi(\overline{v}))^{\flat(\widetilde{\tau})}$  instead of  $\nabla \Phi(\overline{v}) / \| \nabla \Phi(\overline{v}) \|_2$ , but the complexities of these two data structures are the same. Note that we must initialize  $D^{(x,\nabla)}$  and  $D^{(s)}$  for a higher accuracy  $(\gamma / (10^5 \log^2 n))$  than in the previous log-barrier implementation (which had accuracy  $\gamma / 16$ ). However, this increases the complexity by only a  $\widetilde{O}(1)$  factor.

Complexity of Algorithm 11 The main difference to the previous analysis from Lemma 8.4 is that now SHORTSTEP is called only  $K = \widetilde{O}(\sqrt{n}|\log(\mu^{(\mathrm{init})} / \mu^{(\mathrm{end})})|)$  times. We will now list all the differences compared to the previous analysis of Lemma 8.4. Note that Algorithm 11 assumes a global variable  $T \geq \sqrt{n}$ , which is an upper bound on how often SHORTSTEP is called.

So we modify Algorithm 9 to set  $T = K = \widetilde{O}(\sqrt{n}|\log(\mu^{(\mathrm{init})}/\mu^{(\mathrm{end})})|)$ . Further note that as before, we can bound the ratio  $W'$  of largest to smallest entry in  $x$  and  $s$  throughout all calls to SHORTSTEP by  $\log W' = \widetilde{O}(\log W + |\log(\mu^{(\mathrm{init})}/\mu^{(\mathrm{end})})|)$  via Lemma 4.41.

Sampling the primal We scale the sampling probability for SAMPLEPRIMAL by  $\widetilde{O} (\sqrt{m} K / n)$  The complexity per call thus increases to

$$
\tilde {O} (n (\log W + | \log (\mu^ {\mathrm {(i n i t)}} / \mu^ {\mathrm {(e n d)}}) |) + K m / n)
$$

by Lemma 8.2. Further note that by Lemma 4.37 the sampling probability is large enough for our sequence of primal solutions  $x^{(1)}, x^{(2)}, \ldots$  to be close to some sequence  $\widehat{x}^{(1)}, \widehat{x}^{(2)}, \ldots$ , such that  $x^{(t)} \approx_{\gamma/(10^5 \log^2 n} \widehat{x}^{(t)}$  and  $\| (\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{(t-1)})^{-1} (\widehat{x}^{(t)} - \widehat{x}^{(t-1)}) \|_\tau$  for all  $t$ .

$D^{(x,\nabla)}$  data structure The number of times  $\overline{\mu}$  changes is now bounded by  $\widetilde{O}(K/\sqrt{n})$ , so the total cost of updating  $D^{(x,\nabla)}$  in Line 10 is now bounded by  $\widetilde{O}(Km/\sqrt{n})$ . The cost of  $D^{(x,\nabla)}$ .QUERYPRODUCT and QUERYSUM is bounded by

$$
\begin{array}{l} \tilde {O} (K n + K n (\log W + | \log (\mu^ {(\text {i n i t})} / \mu^ {(\text {e n d})}) |) + K \cdot \| \overline {{\mathbf {X}}} (\nabla \Phi (\bar {v})) ^ {\flat (\bar {\tau})} / x \| _ {2} ^ {2}) \\ = \tilde {O} (K n + K n (\log W + | \log (\mu^ {(\text {i n i t})} / \mu^ {(\text {e n d})}) |) + K \cdot m / n), \\ \end{array}
$$

by Theorem 7.1, where we use that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \overline {{\mathbf {X}}} (\nabla \Phi (\bar {v})) ^ {\flat (\bar {\tau})} / x \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} = O (1) \cdot \| \nabla \Phi (\bar {v})) ^ {\flat (\bar {\tau})} \| _ {2} ^ {2} = O (m / n) \cdot \| \nabla \Phi (\bar {v})) ^ {\flat (\bar {\tau})} \| _ {\bar {\tau}} \\ = \tilde {O} (m / n) \cdot \| \nabla \Phi (\bar {v}) ^ {\flat (\bar {\tau})} \| _ {\bar {\tau} + \infty} = \tilde {O} (m / n) \\ \end{array}
$$

$D^{(s)}$  data structure The complexity of calls to  $D^{(s)}$ . Add (Theorem 6.1) is now bounded by  $\widetilde{O}(K^2 \cdot m/n + Kn(\log W + |\log(\mu^{(\mathrm{init})} / \mu^{(\mathrm{end})})|))$ , because  $\|\mathbf{S}^{-1}\delta_s\|_2^2 \leq O(m/n)\|\mathbf{S}^{-1}\delta_s\|_{\tau(x,s)}^2 = O(m/n)$ .

$D^{(\tau)}$  data structure Any change to  $\overline{\tau}$  causes  $\widetilde{O}(1)$  cost in Line 30. The number of times any entry of  $\overline{\tau}$  changes is bounded by the QUERY complexity of Theorem B.1. This complexity can be bounded by  $\widetilde{O}(K^2m/n+Kn(\log W+|\log(\mu^{(\mathrm{init})}/\mu^{(\mathrm{end})}|))$ , when applying Lemma 4.41 and the fact that we scale the sampling probability for SAMPLEPROBABILITY by  $\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{m}K/n)$ . Note that this complexity bound only holds when for the given input sequence  $g^{(t)} \coloneqq (\overline{x}^{(t)}/\overline{s}^{(t)})^{1-\alpha}$ , there exists sequence  $\widehat{g}^{(t)} \approx_{\gamma/(2\cdot 16\cdot 144\log^2n)} g^{(t)}$  which satisfies (70) and (71). This is satisfied because of the accuracy of  $\overline{x} \approx_{2\gamma/(10^5\log^2n)} x$ ,  $\overline{s} \approx_{2\gamma/(10^5\log^2n)} s$ , the stability property  $\| \mathbf{S}^{-1}\delta_s\|_{\tau} \leq 2\gamma$  (Lemma 4.19), and the stable sequence  $\widehat{x}^{(t)} \approx_{\gamma/(10^5\log^2n)} x^{(t)}$  that exists according to Lemma 4.37. So the sequence  $\widehat{g}^{(t)} \coloneqq (\widehat{x}^{(t)}/s^{(t)})^{1-2\alpha}$  satisfies  $\widehat{g}^{(t)} \approx_{\gamma/(2\cdot 16\cdot 144\log^2n)} g^{(t)} = (x^{(t)}/s^{(t)})^{1-2\alpha}$ , (70) and (71).

Updating the data structure The number of times that any entry of  $\overline{s}$  changes now bounded by  $\tilde{O}(K^2 m/n)$ , because  $\|\mathbf{S}^{-1}\delta_s\|_2^2 = O(m/n)$ . Likewise the number of times that any entry of  $\overline{s}$  changes is bounded by  $\tilde{O}(Kn(\log W + |\log(\mu^{(\mathrm{init})} / \mu^{(\mathrm{end})})| + K^2m/n)$  because  $x$  changes by  $\overline{\mathbf{X}}(\nabla\Phi(\overline{v}))^{\flat(\overline{\tau})} - \mathbf{R}\overline{\mathbf{X}}\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{-1}\mathbf{A}h''$ , where for the first term we have  $\|\overline{\mathbf{X}}(\nabla\Phi(\overline{v}))^{\flat(\overline{\tau})}/x\|_2^2 = O(m/n)$  and the second term as only at most  $\tilde{O}(n(\log W + |\log(\mu^{(\mathrm{init})} / \mu^{(\mathrm{end})})|) + Km/n)$  non-zero entries. So updating all the data structures via calls to UPDATE and SCALE in Line 22 now takes  $\tilde{O}(Kn(\log W + |\log(\mu^{(\mathrm{init})} / \mu^{(\mathrm{end})})|) + K^2 \cdot m/n)$  time.

Total Complexity The total complexity is thus

$$
\begin{array}{l} \tilde {O} \left(m + K ^ {2} m / n + K n \left(\log W + \left| \log \left(\mu^ {\text {(i n i t)}} / \mu^ {\text {(e n d)}}\right) \right|\right)\right) \\ = \tilde {O} \left(\left(m + n ^ {1. 5}\right) \left| \log \left(\mu^ {\text {(i n i t)}} / \mu^ {\text {(e n d)}}\right) \right| \left(\log W + \left| \log \left(\mu^ {\text {(i n i t)}} / \mu^ {\text {(e n d)}}\right) \right|\right)\right). \\ \end{array}
$$

![](images/2d7d43c3f1a861dc78ac9b4dc0b54f08a679215b6a2270f642afd5a5ce0dc261.jpg)

Note that Lemma 8.7, as used in our  $\widetilde{O}(n\sqrt{m})$ -time algorithm, only provides an initial point  $(x,s)$  with  $xs = \mu$  for some  $\mu \in \mathbb{R}$ . For the faster IPM, however, we require  $xs \approx \mu \tau(x,s)$  with  $\tau(x,s) := \sigma(x,s) + n/m$  as defined in Definition 4.13. The following Lemma 8.15 shows that such a point can be constructed, and it is proven in Appendix A.1.

Lemma 8.15. We are given a minimum weight perfect  $b$ -matching instance on graph  $G = (U, V, E)$  with cost vector  $c \in \mathbb{R}^E$ .

Let  $G' = (V', E')$  with demand vector  $d'$  be the corresponding starred flow graph (see Definition 8.6) and let  $n$  be the number of nodes of  $G'$  and  $m$  the number of edges.

For  $\tau(x,s) \coloneqq \sigma(x,s) + m/n$  and for any  $\epsilon > 0$  we can construct in  $\tilde{O}(m\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon^{-1}))$  time a cost vector  $c' \in \mathbb{R}^{E'}$  for  $G'$  and a feasible primal dual solution pair  $(x,s)$  for the minimum cost flow problem on  $G'$  with cost vector  $c'$ , where the solution satisfies

$$
x s \approx_ {\epsilon} \tau (x, s). \tag {66}
$$

The cost vector  $c'$  satisfies

$$
\frac {n}{4 m \left(1 + \| b \| _ {\infty}\right)} \leq c ^ {\prime} \leq 3 n.
$$

We now obtain Theorem 8.1 by replacing the slow  $\sqrt{m}$ -iteration IPM, used in Algorithm 10, with the faster  $\sqrt{n}$ -iteration IPM.

Theorem 8.1. For  $b, c \in \mathbb{Z}^m$ , we can solve minimum weight perfect bipartite  $b$ -matching for cost vector  $c$  in  $\widetilde{O}((m + n^{1.5})\log^2(\|c\|_\infty \|b\|_\infty))$  time.

Proof. This follows directly from Algorithm 10 (Theorem 8.5), when we use the faster variant of Algorithm 9 (Lemma 8.14 instead of Lemma 8.4). To construct the initial point we now use Lemma 8.15 instead of Lemma 8.7.  $\square$

# 8.6 More Applications to Matching, Flow, and Shortest Paths

In this section, we apply known reductions and list applications of our main results (see Figure 2). In Section 8.6.1, we show that we can obtain exact algorithms for many fundamental problems when all the numbers from the problem instance are integral. For a history overview for some of these problems see Tables 4 and 5. Then, in Section 8.6.2, we show that even when the numbers are not integral, we can still  $(1 + \epsilon)$ -approximately solve all the problems with  $\log^2(1/\epsilon)$  dependency in the running time.

# 8.6.1 Exact Algorithms for Integral Problems

In this section, we define several problems such that the numbers from the problem instance are integral, and show how to solve them exactly. We will later allow fractions in the problem instance in Section 8.6.2, and show how to solve those problems approximately.

Definition 8.16 (b-matching). For any graph  $G = (V, E)$ , a demand vector  $b \in \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{V}$ , and a cost vector  $c \in \mathbb{Z}^{E}$ , a b-matching is an assignment  $x \in \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{E}$  where  $\sum_{e \ni v} x_{e} \leq b_{v}$  for all  $v \in V$ . We say that  $x$  is perfect if  $\sum_{e \ni v} x_{e} = b_{v}$  for all  $v \in V$ . The cost/weight of  $x$  is  $c^{\top}x = \sum_{e \in E} c_{e}x_{e}$ . When  $b$  is an all-one vector, the b-matching is referred to simply as a matching. We say that  $x$  is fractional if we allow  $x \in \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{E}$ . Similarly,  $b$  is fractional if  $b \in \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{V}$ .

In the maximum weight bipartite  $b$ -matching problem, given a bipartite graph  $G$ , a demand vector  $b$ , and a cost vector  $c$ , we need to find a  $b$ -matching with maximum weight. In the minimum/maximum weight bipartite perfect  $b$ -matching problem, we need to find a perfect  $b$ -matching with minimum/maximum weight or report that no perfect  $b$ -matching exists. Below, we show a relation between these problems. We let  $W$  denote the largest absolute value used

for specifying any value in the problem (i.e.  $b_v \leq W$  for all  $v \in V$  and  $-W \leq c_{u,v} \leq W$  for all  $(u,v) \in E$ ).

Equivalence of variants of perfect  $b$ -matching. Most variants of the problems for perfect  $b$ -matching are equivalent. First, we can assume that the cost is non-negative by working with the cost vector  $c'$  where  $c'(e) = c(e) + W$  for all  $e \in E$ . This is because the cost of every perfect  $b$ -matching is increased by exactly  $W \cdot \|b\|_1 / 2$ . Second, the maximization variant is equivalent with the minimization variant by working with the cost  $c'(e) = -c(e)$  because the cost of every perfect  $b$ -matching is negated.

Maximum weight non-perfect  $b$ -matching  $\rightarrow$  Maximum weight perfect  $b$ -matching. We can reduce the non-perfect variant to the perfect one. Given a problem instance  $(G, b, c)$  of maximum weight bipartite  $b$ -matching where  $G = (U, V, E)$  is a bipartite graph,  $b$  is a demand vector and  $c$  is a cost vector, we construct an instance  $(G', b', c')$  of maximum weight bipartite perfect  $b$ -matching as follows. First, note that we can assume that all entries in  $c$  are non-negative since we never add edges of negative cost to the optimal maximum weight non-perfect  $b$ -matching. Next, we add new vertices  $u_0$  into  $U$  and  $v_0$  into  $V$ . Set  $b_{u_0}' = \sum_{v \in V} b_v$ ,  $b_{v_0}' = \sum_{u \in U} b_u$ , and  $b_x' = b_x$  for all  $x \in U \cup V$ . We add edges connecting  $u_0$  to all vertices in  $V$  (including  $v_0$ ) and connecting  $v_0$  to all vertices in  $U$ . The cost of all these edges incident to  $u_0$  or  $v_0$  is 0. It is easy to see that, given a perfect  $b'$ -matching  $x'$  in  $G'$ , we can obtain a  $b$ -matching  $x$  with the same cost in  $G$  simply by deleting all edges incident to  $u_0$  and  $v_0$ . Also, given any  $b$ -matching  $x$  in  $G$ , there is a perfect  $b'$ -matching  $x'$  in  $G'$  with the same cost that is obtained from  $x$  by "matching the residual demand" on vertices in  $U$  and  $V$  to  $v_0$  and  $u_0$  respectively. More formally, we set  $x_e' = x_e \forall e \in E$ ,  $x_{(u_0, v)}' = b_v - \sum_{(u, v) \in E} x_{(u, v)} \forall v \in V$ ,  $x_{(u, v_0)}' = b_u - \sum_{(u, v) \in E} x_{(u, v)} \forall u \in U$ , and  $x_{(u_0, v_0)}' = \sum_{(u, v) \in E} x_{(u, v)}$ . It is easy to verify that  $x'$  is a perfect  $b'$ -matching with the same cost as  $x$ .

From the above discussion, we conclude:

Proposition 8.17. Consider the following problems when the input graph has  $n$  vertices and  $m$  edges, and let  $W$  denote the largest number in the problem instance:

- minimum weight bipartite perfect  $b$ -matching (with positive cost vector),  
- minimum weight bipartite perfect  $b$ -matching, and  
- maximum weight bipartite perfect  $b$ -matching.

If one of the above problems can be solved in  $T(m,n,W)$  time, then we can solve the other problems in  $T(m,n,2W)$  time. Additionally, we can solve the maximum weight bipartite b-matching problem in  $T(m + O(n),n,O(Wn))$  time.

Definition 8.18 (Flow). For any directed graph  $G = (V, E)$ , a demand vector  $b \in \mathbb{Z}^V$  where  $\sum_{v \in V} b_v = 0$ , and a cost vector  $c \in \mathbb{Z}^E$ , a flow (w.r.t.  $b$ ) is an assignment  $f \in \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^E$  such that, for all  $v \in V$ ,  $\sum_{(u,v) \in E} f_{(u,v)} - \sum_{(v,u) \in E} f_{(v,u)} = b_v$ . The cost of  $f$  is  $c^\top f = \sum_{e \in E} c_e f_e$ . We say that  $f$  is fractional if we allow  $f \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^E$ .

An  $s$ -flow  $f$  is a flow such that  $\overline{b}_v = 0$  for all  $v \in V \setminus \{s, t\}$ . So  $b_s = k = -b_t$  for some  $k \in \mathbb{R}$ . We say that  $k$  is the value of the  $s$ -flow  $f$ . Let  $\vec{\mathrm{cap}} \in \mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{V \setminus \{s, t\}}$  denote vertex capacities. We say that  $f$  respects the vertex capacity if  $\sum_{(u,v) \in E} f_{(u,v)} \leq \vec{\mathrm{cap}}_v$  for all  $v \in V \setminus \{s, t\}$ .

In the uncapacitated minimum-cost flow problem (a.k.a. the transshipment problem), given a directed graph  $G = (V, E)$ , a demand vector  $b$ , and a cost vector  $c$ , we need either (1) report that no flow w.r.t.  $b$  exists, (2) report that the optimal cost is  $-\infty$  (this can happen if there is a negative cycle w.r.t.  $c$ ) or (3) find such flow with minimum cost. We will need the following observation:

Claim 8.19. If there is a feasible solution for the transshipment problem and the optimal cost is not  $-\infty$ , then the optimal cost is between  $[-W^{2}n^{3}, W^{2}n^{3}]$ .

Proof. If the optimal cost is not  $-\infty$ , there is no negative cycle. So an optimal flow  $f$  can be decomposed into a collection of paths. The total flow value summing overall flow paths is exactly  $\sum_{v \in V} \frac{||b||_1}{2}$ . So the flow value on every edge is at most  $\sum_{v \in V} \frac{||b||_1}{2}$ . Therefore, the cost of  $f$  is at least  $\sum_{v \in V} \frac{||b||_1}{2} \times \min_{e \in E} c_e \times |E| \geq -W^2 n^3$  and at most  $\sum_{v \in V} \frac{||b||_1}{2} \times \max_{e \in E} c_e \times |E| \leq W^2 n^3$ .

In the vertex-capacitated minimum-cost  $s$ -flow problem, given a directed  $G$  and a pair of vertices  $(s,t)$ , a target value  $k$ , a vertex capacity vector  $\vec{c\mathrm{ap}}$ , and a cost vector  $c$ , we either report that no such  $s$ -flow with value  $k$  respecting vertex capacities  $\vec{c\mathrm{ap}}$  exists, or find such flow with minimum cost.

Below, we show the equivalence between these two problems and the minimum weight perfect  $b$ -matching problem.

Minimum weight perfect  $b$ -matching  $\rightarrow$  Transshipment. Observe that minimum weight bipartite perfect  $b$ -matching is a special case of the transshipment problem when we only allow edges that direct from vertices with positive demands (for the transshipment problem) to vertices with negative demands.

Transshipment  $\rightarrow$  Vertex-capacitated minimum-cost  $s$ -flow. Given a transshipment instance  $(G = (V,E),b,c)$ , we can construct an instance  $(G',k,\vec{\mathrm{cap}},c')$  for vertex-capacitated  $s$ -flow as follows. First, we add a special source  $s$  and  $t$  into the graph. For each vertex  $v$  with positive demand  $b_v > 0$ , we add a dummy vertex  $s_v$  and two edges  $(s,s_v)$  and  $(s_v,v)$ . For each vertex  $v$  with positive demand  $b_v < 0$ , we add a dummy vertex  $t_v$  and two edges  $(v,t_v)$  and  $(t_v,t)$ . Let  $G'$  denote the resulting graph. The vertex capacity of each  $s_v$  is  $\vec{\mathrm{cap}}_{s_v} = b_v$ . The vertex capacity of each  $t_v$  is  $\vec{\mathrm{cap}}_{t_v} = -b_v$ . For original vertices  $v \in V$ , we set  $\vec{\mathrm{cap}}_v = 3n^3 W^2 + n||b||_1/2$ . We set the cost  $c_e' = c_e$  for all original edges  $e \in E$ , otherwise  $c_e' = 0$  for all new edges  $e$ . Lastly, we set the target value  $k = \sum_{v:b_v > 0} b_v = ||b||_1/2$ .

First, we claim there is an  $s$ -flow in  $G'$  with value  $k$  if and only if there is a flow in  $G$  satisfying the demand  $b$ . To see this, consider any  $s$ -flow  $f'$  with value  $k$  respecting vertex capacities in  $G'$ . Observe that the vertex capacities of all  $s_v$  must be exactly saturated, because there are  $k$  units of flow going out from  $s$ , but the total capacities of all  $s_v$  are at most  $k$ . This similarly holds for all  $t_v$ . Therefore, for all original vertices  $v \in V$ ,  $\sum_{(u,v) \in E} f_{(u,v)}' - \sum_{(v,u) \in E} f_{(v,u)}' = b_v$ . Observe that, by deleting all non-original vertices in  $G'$ ,  $f'$  corresponds to a flow  $f$  (w.r.t.  $b$ ) in  $G$  with the same cost as  $f$ .

Therefore, if the algorithm for solving vertex-capacitated  $s-t$  flow reports that there is no  $s-t$  flow in  $G'$  with value  $k$ , then we just report that there is no flow in  $G$  satisfying the demand  $b$ . Otherwise, we obtain an  $s-t$  flow  $f'$  in  $G'$  with value  $k$ .

We claim that the cost of  $f'$  is less than  $-W^2 n^3$  if and only if there is a negative cycle w.r.t.  $c$  in  $G$ . If the cost of  $f'$  is less than  $-W^2 n^3$ , then there is a flow in  $f$  satisfying the demand  $b$  with the same cost by the above argument. So, by Claim 8.19, the optimal cost of flow in  $G$  must be  $-\infty$  and, hence, there is a negative cycle in  $G$ . In the other direction, suppose there is a negative cycle  $C$  in  $G$ , then it suffices to construct a  $s-t$  flow  $f_1'$  in  $G'$  with value  $k$  with cost less than  $-W^2 n^3$ . Start with an arbitrary  $s-t$  flow  $f_2'$  in  $G'$  with value  $k$  such that  $f_2'$  can be decomposed into a collection of paths only (no cycle). We know that  $f_2'$  exists because  $f'$  exists. Observe that the flow value in  $f_2'$  is at most  $k$  on every edge and at most  $kn$  on every vertex. So the cost of  $f_2'$  is at most  $k \times \max_{e \in E} c_e \times |E| \leq n^3 W^2$ . Now,  $f_1'$  is obtained from  $f_2'$  by augmenting a flow along the negative cycle  $C$  with value  $3n^3 W^2$ . Note that this augmentation is possible because the capacity of every original vertex  $v \in V$  is  $nk + 3n^3 W^2$ . So the cost of  $f_1'$  is at most  $n^3 W^2 - 3n^3 W^2 \leq -2n^3 W^2$ .

By the above argument, we simply check the cost of  $f'$ . If it is less than  $-W^2 n^3$ , we report that the optimal cost for the transshipment problem is  $-\infty$ . Otherwise, we return the corresponding flow  $f$  from the discussion above.

Vertex-capacitated minimum-cost  $s-t$  flow  $\rightarrow$  Minimum weight perfect  $b$ -matching. We show a reduction that directly generalizes the well-known reduction from minimum-cost vertex-disjoint  $s-t$  paths to minimum weight perfect matching (see e.g. Section 3.3 of [San09] and Chapter 16.7c of [Sch03]).

Suppose we are given an instance  $(G = (V, E), k, \vec{\mathrm{cap}}, c)$  for the vertex-capacitated minimum-cost  $s$ -flow problem (possibly with negative costs). First, we assume that  $s$  has no incoming edges and  $t$  has no outgoing edges. To assume this, we can add vertices  $s_0$  and  $t_0$ , and edges  $(s_0, s)$  and  $(t, t_0)$  both with zero cost. Set  $\vec{\mathrm{cap}}_s = \vec{\mathrm{cap}}_t = \sum_{v \in V \setminus \{s, t\}} \vec{\mathrm{cap}}_v$ . By treating  $s_0$  and  $t_0$  as the new source and sink, our assumption is justified.

Next, we construct a bipartite graph  $G' = (L, R, E')$ , a cost vector  $c' \in \mathbb{Z}^{E'}$ , a demand vector  $b' \in \mathbb{Z}^{L \cup R}$  as follows. For each  $v \in V \setminus \{t\}$ , we create a copy  $v_{l} \in L$ . For each  $v \in V \setminus \{s\}$ , we create a copy  $v_{r} \in R$ . For  $v \in V \setminus \{s, t\}$ , add an edge  $(v_{l}, v_{r}) \in E'$  with cost  $c_{(v_{l}, v_{r})}' = 0$ . For each  $e = (u, v) \in E$ , add  $(u_{l}, v_{r}) \in E'$  with cost  $c_{e}' = c_{e}$ . (Note that here we exploit the assumption that  $s/t$  has no incoming/outgoing edges.) We assign the demand vector  $b'$  as follows:  $b_{sl}' = k$ ,  $b_{tr}' = k$ , and, for every  $v \in V \setminus \{s, t\}$ ,  $b_{vl}' = b_{vr}' = \vec{\mathrm{cap}}_v$ .

Observe that every perfect  $b'$ -matching  $x$  in  $G'$  corresponds to a set  $\mathcal{C}$  of cycles in  $G \cup \{(t, s)\}$  where each vertex  $v$  is part of at most  $\vec{\mathrm{cap}}_v$  cycles and  $(t, s)$  is part of exactly  $k$  cycles. Hence,  $\mathcal{C}$  in turn corresponds to an  $s-t$  flow  $f$  in  $G$  of value  $k$  that respects the vertex capacities  $\vec{\mathrm{cap}}$ . Moreover, the cost of  $x$  is exactly the same as the cost of  $f$ .

From the above three reductions, we conclude:

Proposition 8.20. Consider the following problems when the input graph has  $n$  vertices and  $m$  edges, and  $W$  denotes the largest number in the problem instance:

- minimum weight bipartite perfect  $b$ -matching,  
- uncapacitated minimum-cost flow (a.k.a. transshipment), and  
- vertex-capacitated minimum-cost  $s$ -t flow.

If one of the above problems can be solved in  $T(m, n, W)$  time, then we can solve the other problems in  $T(m + O(n), O(n), O(n^{3}W^{2}))$  time.

Negative-weight single-source shortest paths  $\rightarrow$  Transshipment / Dual of minimum weight perfect  $b$ -matching. We also consider the negative-weight single-source shortest path problem. In this problem, we are given a graph  $G = (V,E)$ , a source  $s \in V$ , and a cost vector  $c \in \mathbb{Z}^E$ , the goal is to compute a shortest path tree rooted at  $s$  w.r.t. the cost  $c$ , or detect a negative cycle.

The problem can be reduced to the transshipment problem as follows. First, by performing depth first search, we assume that the source  $s$  can reach all other vertices. (The unreachable vertices have distance  $\infty$  and can be removed from the graph.) Next, we make sure that the shortest path is unique by randomly perturbing the weight of each edge (exploiting the Isolation lemma as in, e.g., [CCE13, Section 6]). Then, we set the demand vector  $b_{s} = n - 1$  and  $b_{v} = -1$  for all  $v \neq s$ . As  $s$  can reach all other vertices, we know that there exists a flow satisfying the demand  $b$ . Finally, we invoke the algorithm for solving the transshipment instance  $G$  with this demand  $b$ . If the algorithm reports that the optimal cost is  $-\infty$ , then we report that there is a negative cycle. Otherwise, we obtain a flow  $f$  that can be decomposed into  $s - v$  paths  $P_{v}$  for all  $v \in V \setminus \{s\}$ . Observe that  $P_{v}$  must be a shortest  $s - v$  path. Since the shortest paths are unique, we have that the union of  $P_{v}$  over all  $v \in V \setminus \{s\}$ , which is exactly the support of  $f$ , form a shortest path tree. Therefore, we simply return the support of  $f$  as the answer.

Minimum mean cycle, deterministic MDP  $\rightarrow$  Negative-weight single-source shortest path. Given a cost function  $c$  on edges, the mean cost of a directed cycle  $\sigma$  is  $\bar{c}(\sigma) = \frac{1}{|\sigma|} \cdot \sum_{e \in \sigma} c(e)$ . The minimum mean cycle problem is to find a cycle of minimum mean cost. The deterministic markov decision process (MDP) problem [Mad02] is the same problem except that we want to find a cycle with maximum mean cost. These two problems are clearly equivalent by negating all the edge cost.

Given a subroutine for detecting a negative cycle, Lawler [Law72] shows that we can also solve the minimum mean cycle problem on a graph of size  $n$  by calling the negative cycle subroutine  $O(\log(nW))$  times on graphs with the same order.

Plugging Algorithms to Reductions. Fianlly, by plugging the main algorithm from Theorem 8.1 into the above reductions, we immediately obtain several applications.

Corollary 8.21 (Exact Algorithms for Integral Problems). There are algorithms with running time  $\tilde{O}((m + n^{1.5})\log^2 W)$  for solving the following problems exactly:

- minimum weight bipartite perfect  $b$ -matching,  
- maximum weight bipartite perfect  $b$ -matching,  
maximum weight bipartite  $b$ -matching,  
- uncapacitated minimum-cost flow (a.k.a. transshipment),  
- vertex-capacitated minimum-cost  $s$ -flow, and  
- negative-weight single-source shortest path,  
- minimum mean cycle, and  
- deterministic MDP

when the input graph has  $n$  vertices and  $m$  edges, and  $W$  denotes the largest absolute value used for specifying any value in the problem. Here, we assume that all numbers used for specifying the input are integral.

# 8.6.2 Approximation Algorithms for Fractional Problems

Generally, for each the problem considered in the previous section, we can consider the same problem where the values that describe the input might not be integral. We say that an algorithm solves a problem with additive approximation factor of  $\epsilon$  if it always returns a solution whose cost is at most  $\mathrm{OPT} + \epsilon$  where OPT is the cost of the optimal solution.

Corollary 8.22 (Approximation Algorithms for Fractional Problems). There are algorithms with running time  $\widetilde{O}((m + n^{1.5})\log^2\frac{W}{\epsilon})$  for solving the following problems with additive approximation factor of  $\epsilon$ :

- minimum weight bipartite perfect  $b$ -matching (a.k.a. optimal transport),  
maximum weight bipartite perfect  $b$ -matching,  
maximum weight bipartite  $b$ -matching,  
- uncapacitated minimum-cost flow (a.k.a. transshipment),  
- vertex-capacitated minimum-cost  $s$ -flow, and  
- negative-weight single-source shortest path,  
- minimum mean cycle, and  
- deterministic MDP

when the input graph has  $n$  vertices and  $m$  edges, and  $W$  denotes the ratio of the largest nonzero absolute value to the smallest non-zero absolute value used for specifying any value in the problem.

We note that the optimal transport problem (a.k.a. the transportation problem) is the same as the minimum weight bipartite perfect  $b$ -matching problem except that we allow all vectors

to be fractional, including the demand vector  $b$ , the cost vector  $c$ , and the solution vector  $x$ . Several papers in the literature assume that the input graph is a complete bipartite graph. We do not assume this.

The idea of the proof above is to round up all the values from the description of the input to (integral) multiple of  $1 / \mathrm{poly}(nW / \epsilon)$  which takes  $O(\log (nW / \epsilon))$  bits. Then, we solve the rounded problem instance exactly using the algorithm Corollary 8.21 for integral problems. Consider an optimal solution  $S$  with cost OPT before rounding. Observe that after rounding, the rounded cost of this optimal solution  $S$  can increase by at most  $n^2\times \max_{e\in E}c(e)\times \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(nW / \epsilon)}\leq \epsilon$ . So the optimal cost in the rounded instance has cost at most  $\mathrm{OPT} + \epsilon$ .
