# Appendix

# A Initial Point and Solution Rounding

In this section we discuss how to construct the initial point for our IPM and how to convert fractional to integral solutions.

In Appendix A.1 we show how to construct a feasible primal solution  $x$  and a feasible dual slack  $s$ , such that  $xs \approx \tau(x, s)$ , as required by the IPM. Unfortunately, for a given minimum cost  $b$ -matching instance we are not able to construct such an initial point. Instead, we replace the given cost vector  $c$  by some alternative cost vector  $c'$ . The idea is to then run the IPM in reverse, so that our solution pairs  $(x, s)$  becomes less tailored to  $c'$  and further away from the constraints, i.e. the points move closer towards the center of the polytope. Once we are sufficiently close to the center it does not matter if we use cost vector  $c$  or  $c'$ , so we can simply switch the cost vector. That switching the cost vector is indeed possible is proven in Appendix A.2. After switching the cost vector, we then run the IPM again as to move the point  $(x, s)$  closer towards the optimal solution.

Our IPM is not able to maintain a truly feasible primal solution  $x$ , instead the solution is only approximately feasible. Thus we must discuss how to turn such an approximately feasible solution into a truly feasible solution, which is done in Appendix A.3. Further, the solution we obtain this way might be fractional, so at last in Appendix A.4 we show how to obtain an integral solution.

# A.1 Finding an Initial Point (Proof of Lemmas 8.3 and 8.13)

We want to solve minimum cost  $b$ -matching instances. In order to construct an initial point, we reduce this problem to uncapacitated minimum cost flows. For the reduction we first construct the underlying graph for the flow instance from a given bipartite  $b$ -matching instance as described in Definition 8.6. We are left with constructing a feasible primal solution  $x$ , a cost vector  $c'$  and a feasible slack  $s$  of the dual w.r.t. the cost vector  $c'$ , such that  $xs \approx \tau(x, s)$  as required by our IPM.

In Section 4.2 and Section 4.3 we present two different IPMs, where the first uses  $\tau(x,s) = 1$  and the second uses  $\tau(x,s) = \sigma(x,s) + n/m$  where  $\sigma(x,s) \coloneqq \sigma(\mathbf{X}^{1/2-\alpha}\mathbf{S}^{-1/2-\alpha}\mathbf{A})$  for  $\alpha = \frac{1}{4\log(4m/n)}$ . Hence we will also present two lemmas for construct the initial point, one for each option of  $\tau(x,s)$ .

Lemma 8.7. We are given a minimum weight perfect  $b$ -matching instance on graph  $G = (U, V, E)$  with cost vector  $c \in \mathbb{R}^E$ .

Let  $G' = (V', E')$  with demand vector  $d'$  be the corresponding starred flow graph (see Definition 8.6) and let  $n$  be the number of nodes of  $G'$  and  $m$  the number of edges.

For  $\tau(x, s) \coloneqq 1$ , we can construct in  $O(m)$  time a cost vector  $c' \in \mathbb{R}^{E'}$  for  $G'$  and a feasible primal dual solution pair  $(x, s)$  for the minimum cost flow problem on  $G'$  with cost vector  $c'$ , where the solution satisfies  $xs = \tau(x, s)$ . The cost vector  $c'$  satisfies

$$
\| b \| _ {\infty} ^ {- 1} \leq c ^ {\prime} \leq n.
$$

Proof. We first construct the primal solution  $x$ , then we construct the dual slack  $s$ , and at last we construct a cost vector  $c$  for which there is a dual solution  $y$  with slack  $s$ .

Primal solution (feasible flow) Let  $n, m$  be the number of nodes and edges in  $G'$ . For our primal solution  $x \in \mathbb{R}^{E'}$  be define the following flow

$$
x _ {u, v} = \frac {1}{n} \text {f o r} u \in U, v \in V, (u, v) \in E,
$$

$$
x _ {u, z} = b _ {u} - \frac {\deg_ {G} (u)}{n} \text {f o r} u \in U,
$$

$$
x _ {z, v} = b _ {v} - \frac {\deg_ {G} (v)}{n} \text {f o r} v \in V.
$$

Here we have  $x > 0$  because  $b_v \geq 1$  and  $\deg_G(v) < n$  for all  $v \in V \cup U$ . The flow also satisfies the demand  $d$ , because for every  $u \in U$  there is a total amount of  $\sum_{(u,v) \in E} 1 / n + b_u - \deg_G(u) / n = b_u = d_u$  leaving  $u$ , and likewise a total flow of  $b_v$  reaching  $v \in V$ . Further, the flow reaching and leaving  $z$  is 0, because

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {u \in U} (b _ {u} - \deg_ {G} (u) / n) = (\sum_ {u \in U} b _ {u}) + | E | / n \\ = \left(\sum_ {v \in V} b _ {v}\right) + | E | / n \\ = \sum_ {v \in V} \left(b _ {v} - \deg_ {G} (v) / n\right), \\ \end{array}
$$

where the first term is the flow reaching  $z$  and the last term is the flow leaving  $z$ . Thus in summary, the flow  $x$  is a feasible flow.

Dual slack and cost vector Now, we define the slack  $s$  and the cost vector  $c'$ . The slack of the dual are of the form  $s_{u,v} = c'_u v - y_u + y_v$  for  $(u,v) \in E'$ . We simply set  $y = \vec{0}_n$  and  $s_{u,v} = c'_u v = 1 / x_{u,v}$  for every  $(u,v) \in E'$ , so then  $xs = 1$ . As  $1/n \leq x_{u,v} \leq b_u - \deg_G(u)/n \leq \|b\|_\infty$ , we get  $\|b\|_\infty^{-1} \leq c' \leq n$ .

![](images/60351d3ab880506d901b2d973c402105569a6ef902dee9e0c728793a0ebd5e2f.jpg)

Lemma A.1 ([CP15], Theorem 12 of [BLSS20]). Given  $p \in (0,4)$ ,  $\eta > 0$  and incidence matrix  $\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$  w.h.p. in  $n$ , we can compute  $w \in \mathbb{R}_{>0}^{m}$  with  $w \approx_{\epsilon} \sigma(\mathbf{W}^{\frac{1}{2} - \frac{1}{p}}\mathbf{A}) + \eta \vec{1}$  in  $\widetilde{O}(m \text{ poly}(1 / \epsilon))$  time.

We remark that the complexity stated in [BLSS20] is  $\widetilde{O}((\mathrm{nnz}(\mathbf{A}) + n^{\omega})\mathrm{poly}(1 / \epsilon))$ , because it is stated for general matrices. However, when using a Laplacian Solver (Lemma 2.1) the  $n^\omega$  term becomes  $\widetilde{O}(m)$ .

Lemma 8.15. We are given a minimum weight perfect  $b$ -matching instance on graph  $G = (U, V, E)$  with cost vector  $c \in \mathbb{R}^E$ .

Let  $G' = (V', E')$  with demand vector  $d'$  be the corresponding starred flow graph (see Definition 8.6) and let  $n$  be the number of nodes of  $G'$  and  $m$  the number of edges.

For  $\tau(x,s) \coloneqq \sigma(x,s) + m/n$  and for any  $\epsilon > 0$  we can construct in  $\widetilde{O}(m\mathrm{poly}(\epsilon^{-1}))$  time a cost vector  $c' \in \mathbb{R}^{E'}$  for  $G'$  and a feasible primal dual solution pair  $(x,s)$  for the minimum cost flow problem on  $G'$  with cost vector  $c'$ , where the solution satisfies

$$
x s \approx_ {\epsilon} \tau (x, s). \tag {66}
$$

The cost vector  $c'$  satisfies

$$
\frac {n}{4 m (1 + \| b \| _ {\infty})} \leq c ^ {\prime} \leq 3 n.
$$

Proof. We first construct the primal solution  $x$ , then we construct the dual slack  $s$ , and at last we construct a cost vector  $c$  for which there is a dual solution  $y$  with slack  $s$ .

Primal solution (feasible flow) The construction of the primal solution  $x$  is the same as the one constructed in the proof of Lemma 8.7, and its feasibility follows the same argument.

Dual slack Let  $\mathbf{A}$  be the incidence matrix of graph  $G'$  and define  $\mathbf{A}' \coloneqq \mathbf{X}\mathbf{A}$ . We construct a vector  $s'$  with the property

$$
s ^ {\prime} \approx_ {\epsilon} \sigma (\mathbf {S} ^ {\prime - 1 / 2 - \alpha} \mathbf {A} ^ {\prime}) + \frac {n}{m},
$$

This construction is done via Lemma A.1.

Next, define  $s \coloneqq s' / x$ , so the vectors  $x$  and  $s$  satisfy

$$
\begin{array}{l} x s = s ^ {\prime} \approx_ {\epsilon} \sigma (\mathbf {S} ^ {\prime - 1 / 2 - \alpha} \mathbf {A} ^ {\prime}) + \frac {n}{m} = \sigma (\mathbf {S} ^ {\prime - 1 / 2 - \alpha} \mathbf {X A}) + \frac {n}{m} \\ = \sigma ((\mathbf {X S}) ^ {- 1 / 2 - \alpha} \mathbf {X A}) + \frac {n}{m} = \sigma (\mathbf {X} ^ {1 / 2 - \alpha} \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1 / 2 - \alpha} \mathbf {A}) + \frac {n}{m} \\ = \tau (x, s) \\ \end{array}
$$

Thus the vector  $s$  satisfies  $xs \approx_{\epsilon} \tau(x, s)$  and we are only left with making sure that  $s$  is indeed a slack vector of the dual.

Constructing cost vector We define the cost vector  $c' := s$  and consider the dual solution  $y = \vec{0}_n$ . The dual problem of the uncapacitated min-cost-flow is

$$
c _ {u, v} ^ {\prime} \geq y _ {u} - y _ {v} \text {f o r} (u, v) \in E ^ {\prime}.
$$

So by setting  $y = \vec{0}_n$  the dual slack is exactly  $c' = s$ , i.e. the vector  $s$  is indeed a slack of the dual problem. As  $c' := s$ , we can bound

$$
c _ {i} ^ {\prime} \leq e ^ {\epsilon} \tau (x, s) / x _ {i} \leq 3 n \text {a n d}
$$

$$
c _ {i} ^ {\prime} \geq e ^ {- \epsilon} \tau (x, s) / x _ {i} \geq \frac {n}{3 m \| d \| _ {\infty}},
$$

where  $x \leq \| d \|_{\infty}$  comes from the fact that any edge incident to  $u \in U$  or  $v \in V$  can carry at most  $|d_u|$  or  $|d_v|$  units of flow, because all edges are directed away from  $u$  and towards  $v$ .

# A.2 Switching the Cost Vector (Proof of Lemma 8.8)

The construction of our initial point replaces the given cost vector by some other cost vector. We now show that it is possible to revert this replacement, provided that our current point  $(x,s)$  is far enough away from the optimal solution. Below we restate Lemma 8.8.

Lemma 8.8. Consider the function  $\tau(x, s)$  and a uncapacitated min-cost-flow instance as constructed in Lemma 8.15 or Lemma 8.7 and let  $\mathbf{A}$  be the incidence matrix of the underlying graph  $G'$ . Let  $c$  be the cost vector constructed in Lemma 8.15 and let  $c'$  be any other cost vector with  $\|c'\|_{\infty} \geq 1$ .

Assume we have a primal dual solution pair  $(x,s)$  for cost vector  $c$  and demand  $d$  with  $\| \mathbf{A}x - d\|_{(\mathbf{AXS}^{-1}\mathbf{A})^{-1}}\leq \sqrt{\mu} /4$  and  $xs\approx_{\epsilon /3}\mu \tau (x,s)$  for any  $\epsilon \leq 1$  and  $\mu \geq 45\| c'\|_{\infty}\| d\|_{\infty}\epsilon$

If we replace the cost vector  $c$  with cost vector  $c'$  then  $(x, s + c' - c)$  is a primal dual solution pair for the new min-cost-flow instance with cost vector  $c'$  and we have  $x(s + c' - c) \approx_{\epsilon/2} \mu \tau(x, s + c' - c)$ .

Proof. Let  $y$  be the dual solution with  $\mathbf{A}y + s = c$ , and let  $s' := s + c' - c$  be the slack vector when replacing cost vector  $c$  by  $c'$ . Then  $\mathbf{A}y + s' = \mathbf{A}y + s + c' - c = c'$ , so if  $s' \geq 0$ , then it is indeed a valid slack vector.

In order to show that  $s' \geq 0$  and  $xs' \approx_{\epsilon/2} \mu \tau(x, s')$ , we will bound the relative difference between  $s$  and  $s'$ , i.e. we want an upper bound on

$$
{\frac {s ^ {\prime} - s}{s}} = {\frac {c ^ {\prime} - c}{s}}.
$$

This relative difference is small, when  $s$  is large. So we want to lower bound  $s$ . By  $xs \approx_{1/2} \mu \tau(x, s)$  we have

$$
s _ {i} \geq \frac {\mu \tau (x , s) _ {i}}{2 x _ {i}} \geq \frac {\mu n}{2 m x _ {i}} \mathrm {f o r a l l} i, \tag {67}
$$

where we used that  $\tau(x, s) \geq m / n$ . In order to bound this term, we must first find an upper bound on  $x$ .

By Lemma 4.38 and the assumption  $\| \mathbf{A}x - d\|_{(\mathbf{AXS}^{-1}\mathbf{A})^{-1}}\leq \sqrt{\mu} /4$  we have that there is a feasible  $x^{\prime}$  with  $\| x^{-1}(x' - x)\|_{\infty}\leq 1 / 2$ . For this feasible  $x^{\prime}$  we know that  $x^{\prime}\leq \| d\|_{\infty}$  and thus  $x\leq 1.5\| d\|_{\infty}$ .

By (67) we have

$$
s _ {i} \geq \frac {\mu n}{2 m x _ {i}} \geq \frac {\mu n}{1 . 5 \| d \| _ {\infty}}
$$

which in turn implies

$$
\| \frac {s ^ {\prime} - s}{s} \| _ {\infty} \leq (\| c ^ {\prime} \| _ {\infty} + \| c \| _ {\infty}) \frac {1 . 5 \| d \| _ {\infty}}{\mu n} \leq (\| c ^ {\prime} \| _ {\infty} + 3 n) \frac {1 . 5 \| d \| _ {\infty}}{\mu n} \leq \frac {4 . 5 \| c ^ {\prime} \| _ {\infty} \| d \| _ {\infty}}{\mu}
$$

Thus for  $\mu \geq 45\|c'\|_{\infty}\|d\|_{\infty} \epsilon$  we can bound

$$
\left\| \frac {s ^ {\prime} - s}{s} \right\| _ {\infty} \leq \frac {\epsilon}{1 0}.
$$

This then implies  $x s^{\prime}\approx_{\epsilon /2}\mu \tau (x,s^{\prime})$

![](images/ac16866c40068386bf73ba3fe40a1044a10a2883078db9efac8e2d8e3778f3e1.jpg)

# A.3 Rounding an Approximate to a Feasible Solution (Proof of Lemma 8.9)

Our IPM is not able to maintain a feasible primal solution  $x$ , instead we can only guarantee that  $\frac{1}{\mu} \| \mathbf{A}^\top x - b \|_{(\mathbf{A}^\top \mathbf{X}\mathbf{S}^{-1}\mathbf{A})^{-1}}^2$  is small. We now show that this is enough to construct a feasible solution that does not differ too much from the provided approximately feasible solution. We further show how small the progress parameter  $\mu$  of the IPM must be, in order to obtain a small additive  $\epsilon$  error compared to the optimal solution. Below we restate Lemma 8.9.

Lemma 8.9. Consider any  $\epsilon >0$  and an uncapacitated min-cost flow instance on a starred flow graph (Definition 8.6) with cost vector  $c\in \mathbb{R}^E$ , demand vector  $d\in \mathbb{R}^V$  and the property that any feasible flow  $f$  satisfies  $f\leq \| d\|_{\infty}$ .

Assume we are given a primal dual solution pair  $(x,s)$ , with  $xs\approx_{1 / 2}\mu \tau (x,s)$  and

$$
\frac {1}{\mu} \cdot \| \mathbf {A} x - d \| _ {(\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1}} ^ {2} \leq \frac {1}{1 0}
$$

for  $\mu \leq \frac{\epsilon}{24n}$ .

Let  $\delta = \frac{\epsilon^2}{12(\|c\|_{\infty}\|d\|_{\infty}m)^2}$ , then in  $\widetilde{O}(m\log \delta^{-1})$  time we can construct a feasible flow  $f$  with  $c^\top f \leq \mathrm{OPT} + \epsilon$ , where  $\mathrm{OPT}$  is the optimal value of the min-cost flow instance.

In order to prove Lemma 8.9, we will first prove a helpful lemma that bounds  $\| \mathbf{A}^\top x - b\| _2$  with respect to  $\frac{1}{\mu}\| \mathbf{A}^{\top}x - b\|_{(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{S}^{-1}\mathbf{A})^{-1}}^{2}$ .

Lemma A.2. Let  $G'$  be a uncapacitated flow instance for demand  $d$  and cost  $c$  where for every feasible flow  $f$  we have  $f \leq \|d\|_{\infty}$ . Let  $\mathbf{A}$  be the incidence matrix of  $G'$ . Given a primal dual pair  $(x, s)$  with infeasible  $x$  and  $xs \approx_1 \tau(x, s)$ , define

$$
\delta := \frac {1}{\mu} \cdot \| \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x - d \| _ {(\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1}} ^ {2}.
$$

Then

$$
\| \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x - d \| _ {2} \leq 6 m \| d \| _ {\infty} ^ {2} \delta
$$

Proof. By Lemma 4.38 we know that there is a feasible  $x'$  with  $\| x^{-1}(x' - x) \|_{\infty} \leq 3\delta$ .

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x - d \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \frac {1}{\lambda_ {\min } \left(\left(\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}\right) ^ {- 1}\right)} \left\| \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x - d \right\| _ {\left(\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}\right) ^ {- 1}} ^ {2} \\ = \lambda_ {\mathrm {m a x}} (\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X} \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) \mu \delta \\ \leq \max _ {i} \left\{x _ {i} / s _ {i} \right\} \lambda_ {\max } (\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {A}) \mu \delta \\ \leq 2 n \max _ {i} \{x _ {i} / s _ {i} \} \mu \delta \\ \end{array}
$$

Because of  $xs \approx_1 \mu \tau(x, s)$  and  $x \leq \|d\|_{\infty}$  we have

$$
s _ {i} \geq \frac {\mu \tau (x , s)}{3 x _ {i}} \geq \frac {n \mu}{3 m \| d \| _ {\infty}}
$$

which implies

$$
\frac {x _ {i}}{s _ {i}} \leq \frac {3 m \| d \| _ {\infty} ^ {2}}{n \mu}
$$

and thus

$$
\left\| \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x - d \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq 6 m \left\| d \right\| _ {\infty} ^ {2} \delta .
$$

![](images/54196fa8c29246f7a783a3dbc81bea9a61b1df7ac7f76d4190ebe551a39082a9.jpg)

We can now prove Lemma 8.9.

Proof of Lemma 8.9. The algorithm works as follows. First we compute

$$
x ^ {\prime} = x + \mathbf {X S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A H} ^ {- 1} (d - \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x)
$$

for some  $\mathbf{H}^{-1}\approx_{\delta}(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{S}^{-1}\mathbf{A})^{-1}$ . This can be done in  $\widetilde{O}(m\log \delta^{-1})$  time via a Laplacian solver.

Then for every  $u \in U$  with  $(\mathbf{A}^\top x')_u > d_u$  we reduce the flow on some edges incident to  $u$  such that the demand of  $u$  is satisfied. We also do the same for every  $v \in V$  with  $(\mathbf{A}^\top x')_v < d_v$ . Let  $x''$  be the resulting flow. After that we route  $(\mathbf{A}^\top x'')_u - d_u$  flow from  $u \in U$  to  $z$  and likewise  $d_v - (\mathbf{A}^\top x'')_v$  flow from  $z$  to  $v \in V$ . Let  $f$  be the resulting flow, then  $f$  is a feasible flow as all demands are satisfied. Constructing  $f$  from  $x'$  this way takes  $\widetilde{O}(m)$  time.

Optimality We are left with proving claim  $c^\top f \leq \mathrm{OPT} + \epsilon$ . By Lemma 4.38 the vector  $x'$  satisfies

$$
\left\| \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x ^ {\prime} - d \right\| _ {(\mathbf {A X S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1}} ^ {2} \leq 5 \delta \cdot \left\| \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x - d \right\| _ {(\mathbf {A X S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1}} ^ {2} \leq \frac {\delta \mu}{2}, \text {a n d} \tag {68}
$$

$$
\left\| \mathbf {X} ^ {- 1} \left(x - x ^ {\prime}\right) \right\| _ {\infty} ^ {2} \leq \frac {5}{\mu} \cdot \left\| \mathbf {A} x - b \right\| _ {\left(\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {X} \mathbf {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}\right) ^ {- 1}} ^ {2} \leq \frac {1}{2} \tag {69}
$$

So by Lemma A.2 we have

$$
\| \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x ^ {\prime} - d \| _ {2} ^ {2} \leq \frac {6 m \| d \| _ {\infty} ^ {2} \| \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} x ^ {\prime} - d \| _ {(\mathbf {A X S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1}} ^ {2}}{\mu} \leq 3 m \| d \| _ {\infty} ^ {2} \delta .
$$

As this bounds the maximum error we have for the demand of each node, this also bounds how much we might change the flow on some edge, i.e. we obtain the bound

$$
\| x ^ {\prime} - f \| _ {2} \leq \| d \| _ {\infty} \sqrt {3 m \delta}.
$$

Thus we have

$$
c ^ {\top} f \leq c ^ {\top} x ^ {\prime} + \| c \| _ {2} \| x - f \| _ {2} \leq c ^ {\top} x ^ {\prime} + m \| c \| _ {\infty} \| d \| _ {\infty} \sqrt {3 \delta}.
$$

We are left with bounding  $c^\top x'$ . For this note that the duality gap of  $(x', s)$  can be bounded by

$$
\sum_ {i \in [ m ]} x _ {i} ^ {\prime} s _ {i} \leq 2 \sum_ {i \in [ m ]} x _ {i} s _ {i} \leq 6 \mu \sum_ {i \in [ m ]} \tau (x, s) _ {i} \leq 1 2 \mu n,
$$

where we use (69),  $xs \approx_1 \mu \tau(x, s)$ , and  $\sum_{i \in [m]} \tau(x, s)_i = 2n$ . As the duality gap is an upper bound on the difference to the optimal value, we have

$$
c ^ {\top} x ^ {\prime} \leq \mathrm {O P T} + 1 2 \mu n
$$

so for  $\mu \leq \frac{\epsilon}{24n}$  and  $\delta \leq \frac{\epsilon^2}{12(\|c\|_{\infty}\|d\|_{\infty}m)^2}$  we have

$$
c ^ {\top} f \leq \mathrm {O P T} + 1 2 \mu n + m \| c \| _ {\infty} \| d \| _ {\infty} \sqrt {3 \delta} \leq \mathrm {O P T} + \epsilon .
$$

![](images/8d687626cad42af776f67b0c8b816f81fbd26ce84da6ccdb72c0f6c36fd6fc89.jpg)

# A.4 Rounding to an Integral Solution (Proof of Lemma 8.10)

In Appendix A.3 we showed how to obtain a feasible nearly optimal fractional solution. We now show that we can also obtain a feasible truly optimal integral solution by rounding each entry of the solution vector  $x$  to the nearest integer. This only works, if the optimal solution is unique. In order to make sure that the optimal solution is unique, we use a variant of the Isolation-lemma. In high-level, this lemma shows that by adding a small random cost to each edge, the minimum-cost flow becomes unique. Below we restate Lemma 8.10.

Lemma 8.10. Let  $\Pi = (G, b, c)$  be an instance for minimum-cost flow problem where  $G$  is a directed graph with  $m$  edges, the demand vector  $b \in \{-W, \dots, W\}^V$  and the cost vector  $c \in \{-W, \dots, W\}^E$ . Further assume that all optimal flows have congestion at most  $W$ .

Let the perturbed instance  $\tilde{\Pi} = (G, b, \tilde{c})$  be such that  $\tilde{c}_e = c_e + z_e$  where  $z_e$  is a random number from the set  $\left\{\frac{1}{4m^2W^2}, \ldots, \frac{2mW}{4m^2W^2}\right\}$ . Let  $\tilde{f}$  be a feasible flow for  $\tilde{\Pi}$  whose cost is at most  $\mathrm{OPT}(\tilde{\Pi}) + \frac{1}{12m^2W^3}$  where  $\mathrm{OPT}(\tilde{\Pi})$  is the optimal cost for problem  $\tilde{\Pi}$ . Let  $f$  be obtained by rounding the flow  $\tilde{f}$  on each edge to the nearest integer. Then, with probability at least  $1/2$ ,  $f$  is an optimal feasible flow for  $\Pi$ .

A variant of Lemma 8.10 was proven in [DS08] via the following result from [KS01].

Lemma A.3 ([KS01, Lemma 4]). Let  $C$  be any collection of distinct linear forms in variables  $z_{1},\ldots ,z_{\ell}$  with coefficients in the range  $\{0,\dots ,K\}$ . If  $z_{1},\ldots ,z_{\ell}$  are independently chosen uniformly at random in  $\{0,\dots ,K\ell /\epsilon \}$ , then, with probability greater than  $1 - \epsilon$ , there is a unique form of minimal value at  $z_{1},\ldots ,z_{\ell}$ .

Here we prove Lemma 8.10 via a similar proof to the one in [DS08].

Proof of Lemma 8.10. We start by proving that  $\tilde{\Pi}$  has a unique optimal solution. We then show that rounding a nearly optimal fractional matching results in the optimal integral matching.

Unique Optimal Solution Let  $C \subset \mathbb{R}^E$  be the set of optimal integral flows. Then we can interpret each  $f \in C$  as a linear form  $z \mapsto \langle f, z \rangle$  in the variables  $z_1, \ldots, z_m$ . Further we have  $0 \leq f_e \leq W$  for all  $f \in C$  and  $e \in E$ .

Now consider  $z \in \mathbb{R}^m$  where each  $z_i$  is an independent uniformly sampled element from  $\{0, \dots, K\ell/\epsilon\}$ . By Lemma 8.10 the minimizer  $\operatorname{argmin}_{f \in C} \langle f, z \rangle$  is unique with probability at least  $1/2$ . As  $\langle f, c \rangle = \langle f', c \rangle$  for all  $f, f' \in C$ , we have that for  $c' := c + z/(4m^2 W^2)$  there also exists a unique  $f \in C$  that minimizes  $\langle f, c' \rangle$ . Further note that  $\langle f, c' \rangle < \langle f, c \rangle + 1$  so this  $f$  is also the unique optimal solution to  $\widetilde{\Pi}$ .

Rounding to the optimal integral matching Since we added random multiples of  $\frac{1}{4m^2W^2}$  to each edge cost, the second best integral matching  $f'$  has cost at least  $\mathrm{OPT}(\tilde{\Pi}) + \frac{1}{4m^2W^2}$ .

Now assume we have some feasible fractional matching  $\tilde{f}$ . As every feasible fractional matching is a convex combination of feasible integral matchings, we can write  $tf = \lambda f + (1 - \lambda)g$ , where  $\lambda \in [0,1]$ ,  $f$  is the optimal (integral) matching, and  $g$  is a feasible fractional matching with cost at least  $\mathrm{OPT}(\widetilde{\Pi}) + \frac{1}{4m^2W^2}$  (i.e.  $g$  is a convex combination of non-optimal feasible integral matchings).

If  $\widetilde{f}$  has cost at most  $\mathrm{OPT}(\widetilde{\Pi}) + \frac{1}{12m^2W^3}$ , then  $\lambda \geq 1 - \frac{1}{3W}$  and thus  $\| \widetilde{f} - f \|_{\infty} \leq 1/3$ . So by rounding the entries of  $\widetilde{f}$  to the nearest integer we obtain the optimal flow  $f$ .

# B Leverage Score Maintenance

In this section we explain how to obtain the following data structure for maintaining approximate leverage scores via results from [BLSS20].

Theorem B.1. There exists a Monte-Carlo data-structure, which works against an adaptive adversary, that supports the following procedures:

- INITIALIZER  $(\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}, g \in \mathbb{R}^m, \epsilon \in (0,1))$ : Given incidence matrix  $\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ , scaling  $g \in \mathbb{R}^m$  and accuracy  $\epsilon > 0$ , the data-structure initializes in  $\widetilde{O}(m\epsilon^{-2})$  time.  
-  $\operatorname{SCALE}(I \subset [m], u \in \mathbb{R}^I)$ : Sets  $g_I = u$  in  $\widetilde{O}(|I|e^{-2})$  time.  
-  $QUERY(): Let g^{(t)} \in \mathbb{R}^m$  be the vector  $g \in \mathbb{R}^m$  during t-th call to QUERY and assume  $g^{(t)} \approx_{1/32} g^{(t-1)}$ . W.h.p. in n the data-structure outputs a vector  $\tilde{\tau} \in \mathbb{R}^m$  such that  $\tilde{\tau} \approx_{\epsilon} \sigma(\sqrt{\mathbf{G}^{(t)}}\mathbf{A}) + \frac{n}{m}\vec{1}$ . Further, with probability at least  $\frac{1}{2}$  the total cost over  $K$  steps is

$$
\widetilde {O} \Big (K ^ {2} \cdot \epsilon^ {- 4} \cdot m / n + K (n \epsilon^ {- 2} + n \epsilon^ {- 1} \log W) \Big),
$$

where  $W$  is a bound on the ratio of largest to smallest entry of any  $g^{(t)}$ .

Let  $g^{(k)}$  be the state of  $g$  during the  $k$ -th call to QUERY and define  $\tau(g) \coloneqq \sigma(\sqrt{\mathbf{G}}\mathbf{A}) + \frac{n}{m}\vec{1}$ . Then the complexity bound on QUERY holds, if there exists a sequence  $\widetilde{g}^{(1)}, \ldots, \widetilde{g}^{(K)} \in \mathbb{R}^m$  where for all  $k \in [K]$

$$
g _ {i} ^ {(k)} \in \left(1 \pm \frac {1}{1 6 \cdot 1 4 4 \epsilon \log^ {2} n}\right) \widetilde {g} _ {i} ^ {(k)} f o r a l l i \in [ m ], a n d \tag {70}
$$

$$
\frac {1}{\epsilon^ {2}} \left\| \mathbf {G} ^ {(k) - 1} \left(g ^ {(k + 1)} - g ^ {(k)}\right)\right\| _ {\tau \left(g ^ {(k)}\right)} ^ {2} + \left\| \right.\left( \right.\mathbf {T} \left(g ^ {(k)}\right) ^ {- 1} \left(\tau \left(g ^ {(k + 1)}\right) - \tau \left(g ^ {(k)}\right)\right)\left. \right\| _ {\tau \left(g ^ {(k)}\right)} ^ {2} = O (1). \tag {71}
$$

We use the following Lemma B.2 from [BLSS20] which describes a data structure for maintaining approximate leverage scores. Note that the complexity scales with the Frobenius norm of some matrix. We obtain Theorem B.1 by combining Lemma B.2 with another data structure that guarantees that this norm bound is small.

Lemma B.2 ([BLSS20, Theorem 8, Algorithm 7, when specialized to incidence matrices]). There exists a Monte-Carlo data-structure, which works against an adaptive adversary, that supports the following procedures:

- INITIALIZER  $(\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}, g \in \mathbb{R}^m, \epsilon \in (0,1))$ : Given incidence matrix  $\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ , scaling  $g \in \mathbb{R}^m$  and accuracy  $\epsilon > 0$ , the data-structure initializes in  $\widetilde{O}(m\epsilon^{-2})$  time.  
-  $\text{SCALE}(i \in [m], u \in \mathbb{R})$ : Given  $i \in [m]$  and  $u \in \mathbb{R}$  sets  $g_i = u$  in  $\widetilde{O}(\epsilon^{-2})$  time.

-  $QUERY(\Psi^{(t)} \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}, \Psi_{\mathrm{(safe)}}^{(t)} \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n})$ : Let  $g^{(t)} \in \mathbb{R}^m$  be the vector  $g \in \mathbb{R}^m$  during  $t$ -th call to  $QUERY$ , assume  $g^{(t)} \approx_{1/32} g^{(t-1)}$  and define  $\mathbf{H}^{(t)} = \mathbf{A}^\top \mathbf{G}^{(t)}\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ . Given random input-matrices  $\Psi^{(t)} \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$  and  $\Psi_{\mathrm{(safe)}}^{(t)} \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$  such that

$$
\Psi^ {(t)} \approx_ {\epsilon / (2 4 \log n)} (\mathbf {H} ^ {(t)}) ^ {- 1}, \Psi_ {\mathrm {(s a f e)}} ^ {(t)} \approx_ {\epsilon / (2 4 \log n)} (\mathbf {H} ^ {(t)}) ^ {- 1}
$$

and any randomness used to generate  $\Psi_{\mathrm{(safe)}}^{(t)}$  is independent of the randomness used to generate  $\Psi^{(t)}$ , w.h.p. in  $n$  the data-structure outputs a vector  $\widetilde{\tau} \in \mathbb{R}^m$  independent of  $\Psi^{(1)}, \ldots, \Psi^{(t)}$  such that  $\widetilde{\tau} \approx_{\epsilon} \sigma(\sqrt{\mathbf{G}^{(t)}}\mathbf{A}) + \frac{n}{m}\vec{1}$ . Further, the total cost over  $K$  steps is

$$
\begin{array}{l} \widetilde {O} \Big (\Big (\sum_ {t \in [ K ]} \| \sqrt {\mathbf {G} ^ {(t)}} \mathbf {A} \boldsymbol {\Psi} ^ {(t)} \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \sqrt {\mathbf {G} ^ {(t)}} - \sqrt {\mathbf {G} ^ {(t - 1)}} \mathbf {A} \boldsymbol {\Psi} ^ {(t - 1)} \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \sqrt {\mathbf {G} ^ {(t - 1)}} \| _ {F} \Big) ^ {2} \cdot \epsilon^ {- 4} m / n \\ + K \left(T _ {\Psi_ {\mathrm {(s a f e)}}} + T _ {\Psi} + n \epsilon^ {- 2} + n \epsilon^ {- 1} \log W\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $T_{\Psi}, T_{\Psi_{(\mathrm{safe})}}$  is the time required to multiply a vector with  $\Psi^{(t)} \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$  and  $\Psi_{(\mathrm{safe})}^{(t)} \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$  respectively (i.e. in case the matrices are given implicitly via data structures).

The initialization, scale and query complexity of [BLSS20, Theorem 8, Algorithm 7] are larger by a factor of  $n$  compared to the complexities stated in Lemma B.2. This difference in complexity is because the data structure of [BLSS20] was proven for general matrices  $\mathbf{A}$  which may have up to  $n$  entries per row, while in Lemma B.2 we consider only incidence matrices with only 2 entries per row. The proof of [BLSS20, Theorem 8, Algorithm 7] works by reducing the problem of maintaining leverage scores to the problem of detecting large entries of the product  $\mathbf{Diag}(g)\mathbf{A}h$ , for some vectors  $g \in \mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{m}$ ,  $h \in \mathbb{R}^{n}$ . This is exactly what our data structure of Lemma 5.1 does. By plugging our data structure of Lemma 5.1 into the algorithm of [BLSS20] and observing the sparsity of  $\mathbf{A}$ , we obtain the faster complexities as stated in Lemma B.2 for incidence matrices  $\mathbf{A}$ .

We combine Lemma B.2 with the following Algorithm 12. The data structure of Algorithm 12 guarantees that the Frobenius norm (as used in the complexity statement of Lemma B.2) stays small. The properties of Algorithm 12 are stated in Lemma B.3 and Lemma B.4, where the latter bounds the change of the Frobenius norm.

Lemma B.3 ([BLSS20, Theorem 10, Algorithm 8]). There exists a randomized data-structure that supports the following operations (Algorithm 12)

- INITIALIZER  $(\overline{g} \in \mathbb{R}^m, \overline{\tau} \in \mathbb{R}^m, \epsilon)$ : Preprocess vector  $\overline{g} \in \mathbb{R}^m, \overline{\tau} \in \mathbb{R}^m$  and accuracy parameter  $\epsilon > 0$  in  $O(m)$  time. Returns two vector  $\widetilde{g}, v \in \mathbb{R}^m$ .  
-  $UPDATE(I \subset [m], s \in \mathbb{R}^I, t \in \mathbb{R}^I)$  Sets  $\overline{g}_I \gets s$ ,  $\overline{\tau}_I \gets t$  in  $\widetilde{O}(|I|)$  time. Returns set  $J \subset [m]$  and two vectors  $\widetilde{g}, v \in \mathbb{R}^m$ . Here  $\widetilde{g}$  is returned via pointer and  $J$  lists the indices  $i$  where  $\widetilde{g}_i$  or  $v_i$  changed compared to the previous output.

If the update does not depend on any of the previous outputs, and if there is some matrix  $\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$  with  $\overline{\tau} \approx_{0.5} \tau(\overline{\mathbf{G}}^{1/2}\mathbf{A})$ , then  $\widetilde{g}, v$  satisfy following two properties: (i) with high probability  $\|v\|_0 = O(n\epsilon^2 \log n)$ . (ii)  $\widetilde{g} \approx_{\epsilon} \overline{g}$ , and with high probability  $\mathbf{A}^\top \mathbf{V}\mathbf{A} \approx_{\epsilon} \mathbf{A}^\top \overline{\mathbf{G}}\mathbf{A}$ .

Lemma B.4 ([BLSS20, Lemma 11]). Let  $\overline{g}^{(k)},\overline{\tau}^{(k)}\in \mathbb{R}^m$ $\widetilde{g}^{(k)},v^{(k)}\in \mathbb{R}^m$  be the vectors  $\overline{g}$ $\overline{\tau}$ ,  $\widetilde{g}$ ,  $v$  right before the  $k$ -th call to UPDATE of Lemma B.3. Assume there exists a sequence  $g^{(1)},\dots,g^{(K)}\in \mathbb{R}^m$  and matrix  $\mathbf{A}\in \mathbb{R}^{m\times n}$  with

$$
\overline {{g}} _ {i} ^ {(k)} \in (1 \pm 1 / (1 6 \epsilon \log n)) g _ {i} ^ {(k)}, \overline {{\tau}} _ {i} ^ {(k)} \in (1 \pm 1 / (1 6 \log n)) \tau (\mathbf {G} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {A}) _ {i}, \forall i \in [ m ]
$$

Algorithm 12: Pseudocode for Lemma B.3, based on [BLSS20, Algorithm 8].

2  $\begin{array}{rl} & {\widetilde{g},\overline{g},\widetilde{\tau},\overline{\tau},\epsilon ,v,y,\gamma \coloneqq c\log n\mathrm{~for~some~large~constant~}c.}\\ & {\widetilde{g}\gets \overline{g},\widetilde{g}\gets \overline{g},\overline{\tau}\gets \overline{\tau},\widetilde{\tau}\gets \overline{\tau},\epsilon \gets \epsilon ,y\gets \overrightarrow{0}_m}\\ & {v_i\gets \widetilde{g}_i / \min \{1,\gamma \epsilon^{-2}\widetilde{\tau}_i\} \mathrm{independently~for~each~}i\in [m]\mathrm{~with~probability}}\\ & {\min \{1,\gamma \epsilon^{-2}\widetilde{\tau}_i\} \mathrm{and~}v_i\gets 0\mathrm{~otherwise}.} \end{array}$

5  $v_{i}\leftarrow \widetilde{g}_{i} / \min \{1,\gamma \epsilon^{-2}\widetilde{\tau}_{i}\}$  independently for each  $i\in [m]$  with probability min{1,  $\gamma \epsilon^{-2}\widetilde{\tau}_i$  } and  $v_{i}\gets 0$  otherwise.

8  $y_{i}\gets \frac{8}{\epsilon} (\overline{g}_{i} / \widetilde{g}_{i} - 1)$  and  $y_{i + n}\gets 2(\overline{\tau}_i / \widetilde{\tau}_i - 1)$  for  $i\in I$    
9 Let  $\pi :[2n]\to [2n]$  be a sorting permutation such that  $|y_{\pi (i)}|\geq |y_{\pi (i + 1)}|$    
10 For each integer  $\ell$  , we define  $i_{\ell}$  be the smallest integer such that  $\sum_{j\in [i_{\ell}]}\overline{\tau}_{\pi (j)}\geq 2^{\ell}$    
11 Let  $k$  be the smallest integer such that  $|y_{\pi (i_k)}|\leq 1 - \frac{k}{2[\log n]} J\gets \emptyset$    
12 for each coordinate  $j\in [i_k]$  do   
13 Set  $i = \pi (j)$  if  $\pi (j)\leq n$  and set  $i = \pi (j) - n$  otherwise   
14  $\widetilde{g}_i\gets \overline{g}_i,\widetilde{\tau}_i\gets \overline{\tau}_i,y_j\gets 0,J\gets J\cup \{i\}$    
15  $v_{i}\leftarrow \left\{ \begin{array}{l l}{\widetilde{g}_{i} / \min \{1,\gamma \epsilon^{-2}\cdot \widetilde{\tau}_{i}\}} & {\mathrm{with~probability~min}\{1,\gamma \epsilon^{-2}\cdot \widetilde{\tau}_{i}\}}\\ {0} & {\mathrm{otherwise}} \end{array} \right.$    
16 return J, g, v

and

$$
\epsilon^ {- 2} \| \left(\mathbf {G} ^ {(k)}\right) ^ {- 1} \left(g ^ {(k + 1)} - g ^ {(k)}\right) \| _ {\tau (g ^ {(k)})} ^ {2} + \| \left(\mathbf {T} ^ {(k)}\right) ^ {- 1} \left(\tau^ {(k + 1)} - \tau^ {(k)}\right) \| _ {\tau (g ^ {(k)})} = O (1)
$$

for all  $k \in [K - 1]$ , then

$$
\mathbb {E} \left[ \sum_ {k \in [ K - 1 ]} \left\| \sqrt {\widetilde {\mathbf {G}} ^ {(k + 1)}} \mathbf {A} (\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {V} ^ {(k + 1)} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \sqrt {\widetilde {\mathbf {G}} ^ {(k + 1)}} - \sqrt {\widetilde {\mathbf {G}} ^ {(k)}} \mathbf {A} (\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {V} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \sqrt {\widetilde {\mathbf {G}} ^ {(k)}} \right\| _ {F} \right]
$$

is at most  $O(K \log^{5/2} n)$ .

The complexity of UPDATE in [BLSS20, Lemma 11] is a bit higher since they consider dense matrices. Here we show that for sparse matrices the amortized update time is nearly linear in the input size.

Lemma B.5. The function  $UPDATE$  can be implemented to run in amortized  $\widetilde{O}(|I|)$  time.

Proof. We maintain a balanced binary search tree to sort the  $y$ . This takes  $\widetilde{O}(|I|)$  amortized time. Then use that binary search to tree as a prefix data-structure, i.e. each node is marked by the sum of its children to the left. Then the prefix of first  $i$  elements is obtained by going from the root down to the  $i$ th element and adding the value of a node whenever we take the right edge. When removing/inserting a node, updating these partial sums takes only  $O(depth) = \widetilde{O}(1)$  time. Hence the amortized time for maintaining this structure is  $\widetilde{O}(|I|)$ .

The rest of UPDATE simply uses that data-structure, and can find the right value for  $i_{\ell}$  and  $k$  via binary-search. Note that every index  $i$  for which the loop of UPDATE is executed, has been part of some input set  $I$  before and will not be included in that loop again, unless it is part of another future input set  $I$ . Hence we can charge the cost of the loop to previous calls to UPDATE. So we obtain  $O(|I|)$  amortized cost for the loop per call to UPDATE.

By combining Lemma B.2 with Lemma B.3 to get a good bound on the Frobenius norm via Lemma B.4 we then obtain Theorem B.1.

Algorithm 13: Pseudocode for the data structure of Theorem B.1  
1 members  
2  $D^{(\mathrm{stable})}$ $D^{(\tau)}$ $\tilde{g}\in \mathbb{R}^m$ $v\in \mathbb{R}^m$   
3 procedure INITIALIZE(A  $\in \mathbb{R}^{m\times n}$ $g\in \mathbb{R}^m$ $\epsilon \in (0,1)$ )  
4  $\overline{\tau}\gets$  compute  $\sigma (\sqrt{\mathbf{G}}\mathbf{A}) + n / m$   
5  $\widetilde{g},v\gets D^{(\mathrm{stable})}.\mathrm{INITIALize}(g,\overline{\tau},\epsilon /(144\log n))$  (Lemma B.3)  
6  $D^{(\tau)}.\mathrm{INITIALize}(\mathbf{A},\widetilde{g},\epsilon /10)$  (Lemma B.2)  
7 procedure SCALE(I  $\subset [m]$ $u\in \mathbb{R}^{|I|})$   
8  $J,\widetilde{g},v\gets D^{(\mathrm{stable})}.\mathrm{UPDATE}(i,u)$   
9  $D^{(\tau)}.\mathrm{SCALE}(i,\widetilde{g}_i)$  for all  $i\in J$   
10 procedure QUERY()  
11  $\Psi \gets$  Laplacian Solver for  $(\mathbf{A}^\top \mathbf{V}\mathbf{A})^{-1}$   
12  $\Psi_{(\mathrm{safe})}\gets$  Laplacian Solver for  $(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{A})^{-1}$  where  $w\in \mathbb{R}^m$  is a leverage score sample from  $\overline{\tau}$ .  
13  $I,\overline{\tau}\gets D^{(\tau)}.\mathrm{QUERY}(\Psi ,\Psi_{(\mathrm{saf})})$   
14 return  $I,\overline{\tau}$

Proof of Theorem B.1. The algorithm is given by Algorithm 13. We will first prove correctness, and then bound the complexity.

Correctness Note that by Line 6 and Line 9, the data structure  $D^{(\tau)}$  always maintains the leverage score of  $\sqrt{\widetilde{g}}\mathbf{A}$ , where  $\widetilde{g}$  is the vector maintained by  $D^{(sample)}$  (Lemma B.3). We will argue that  $D^{(\tau)}$  maintains  $\overline{\tau} \approx_{\epsilon /2} \tau (\sqrt{\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}} \mathbf{A})$ . By  $\widetilde{g} \approx_{\epsilon /4} g$  this then implies  $\overline{\tau} \approx_{\epsilon} \tau (\mathbf{G}\mathbf{A})$ .

For Lemma B.3 to return a good approximation, we require that (i)  $\Psi \approx_{1/(48\log n)} \widetilde{\mathbf{A}} \widetilde{\mathbf{G}} \mathbf{A}$ , (ii)  $\Psi_{(\mathrm{safe})} \approx_{1/(48\log n)} \widetilde{\mathbf{A}} \widetilde{\mathbf{G}} \mathbf{A}$ , and (iii) the randomness of  $\Psi_{(\mathrm{safe})}$  does not depend on the randomness in  $\Psi$ .

By Lemma B.3 the vector  $v$  satisfies  $\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{V}\mathbf{A} \approx_{\epsilon/(144\log n)} \mathbf{A}\mathbf{G}\mathbf{A}$ , and  $\widetilde{g} \approx_{\epsilon/(144\log n)} g$ . So for a  $\epsilon/(144\log n)$  accurate Laplacian Solver (Lemma 2.1) we have  $\Psi \approx_{\epsilon/(48\log n)} \mathbf{A}\widetilde{\mathbf{G}}\mathbf{A}$ . Thus condition (i) is satisfied.

We assume that the result of the last call to  $D^{(\tau)}$ . QUERY satisfied  $\overline{\tau} \approx \tau (\sqrt{g}\mathbf{A})$ . Then by the assumption  $g^{(t)} \approx_{1/32} g^{(t-1)}$  (i.e. the vector  $g$  does not change too much between two calls to QUERY) we have that any entry of  $\tau (\sqrt{\mathbf{G}}\mathbf{A})$  can change by at most a constant factor between any two calls to QUERY. As  $\widetilde{g} \approx g$ , this means that this old  $\overline{\tau}$  from the previous iteration is still a constant factor approximation of the new  $\tau (\sqrt{g}\mathbf{A})$ . So we can use the old  $\overline{\tau}$  of the previous iteration for the leverage score sampling in Line 12 to obtain a good approximation of  $\mathbf{AGA}$ . Note that the approximation can be arbitrarily (i.e.  $\delta > 0$ ) close by sampling  $O(n\delta^{-2}\log n)$  entries. Hence we can have a Laplacian solver with  $\Psi_{(\mathrm{safe})} \approx_{\epsilon/(48\log n)} \mathbf{AGA}$ . Also note that the randomness of  $\Psi_{(\mathrm{safe})}$  depends only on  $\overline{\tau}$ , which by Lemma B.2 does not depend on  $\Psi$ . Thus (iii) is also satisfied.

Complexity The complexities for INITIALIZATION and SCALE come directly from Lemma B.2 and Lemma B.3. So let us consider QUERY instead.

By assumption (71) and (70) the requirements for Lemma B.4 are satisfied and we have

$$
\mathbb {E} \left[ \sum_ {k \in [ K - 1 ]} \left\| \sqrt {\mathbf {G} ^ {(k + 1)}} \mathbf {A} (\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {V} ^ {(k + 1)} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \sqrt {\mathbf {G} ^ {(k + 1)}} - \sqrt {\mathbf {G} ^ {(k)}} \mathbf {A} (\mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \mathbf {V} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {A}) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \sqrt {\mathbf {G} ^ {(k)}} \right\| _ {F} \right]
$$

is at most  $O(K \log^{5/2} n)$ .

Note that the Laplacian Solver for  $(\mathbf{A}^{\top}\mathbf{V}^{(k)}\mathbf{A})^{-1}$  can be made  $\delta >0$  accurate at cost  $O(\log \delta^{-1})$  (Lemma 2.1), so by choosing some  $\delta = O(\epsilon /\mathrm{poly}(n))$ , we can bound

$$
\mathbb {E} \left[ \sum_ {k \in [ K - 1 ]} \left\| \sqrt {\mathbf {G} ^ {(k + 1)}} \mathbf {A} \boldsymbol {\Psi} ^ {(k + 1)} \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \sqrt {\mathbf {G} ^ {(k + 1)}} - \sqrt {\mathbf {G} ^ {(k)}} \mathbf {A} \boldsymbol {\Psi} ^ {(k)} \mathbf {A} ^ {\top} \sqrt {\mathbf {G} ^ {(k)}} \right\| _ {F} \right] \leq O (K \log^ {5 / 2} n).
$$

By Markov inequality we thus have with probability at least  $1/2$  that the queries to Lemma B.2 have total complexity  $\widetilde{O}(K^2 \cdot \epsilon^{-4} \cdot m/n + K(n\epsilon^{-2} + n\epsilon^{-1}\log W))$ , where we used that both  $\Psi$  and  $\Psi_{\mathrm{(safe)}}$  can be applied in  $\widetilde{O}(n\epsilon^{-2})$  time, because of the sparsity of  $v$  and  $w$ .

# C Degeneracy of A

For all our data structures and the final algorithm from Section 8, for simplicity we always assumed that the constraint matrix  $\mathbf{A} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$  of the linear program is the incidence matrix of some directed graph. However, this comes with some other problem: Note that the rank of such a matrix is at most  $n - 1$ , so  $\mathbf{A}$  is a so-called degenerate matrix, and the Laplacian  $\mathbf{L} = (\mathbf{A}^{\top} \mathbf{A})$  is singular, i.e.  $\mathbf{L}^{-1}$  does not exist. However, the IPM presented in Section 4 assumes that the constraint matrix is non-degenerate and that  $(\mathbf{A}^{\top} \mathbf{A})^{-1}$  exists. So technically it is not clear if the IPM can indeed be used to solve the perfect  $b$ -matching problem as we did in Section 8. We outline here how we can assume that  $\mathbf{A}$  is indeed non-degenerate and what small modifications must be performed to our data structures to handle the new matrix  $\mathbf{A}$ .

Assume  $\mathbf{A}$  is the incidence matrix of some minimum weight perfect bipartite  $b$ -matching instance, and the corresponding formulation as a linear program is  $\min_{\mathbf{A}^{\top}x = b,x\geq 0}c^{\top}x$ . Instead of using the incidence matrix  $\mathbf{A}$  and cost vector  $c$ , we use

$$
\mathbf {A} ^ {\prime} = \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {A} \\ \mathbf {I} _ {n \times n} \end{array} \right], \mathrm {a n d} c ^ {\prime} = \left[ \begin{array}{c} c \\ \| b \| _ {1} \| c \| _ {\infty} \cdot \vec {\mathbf {I}} _ {n}. \end{array} \right]
$$

That is, we add an  $n \times n$  identity block to the bottom of  $\mathbf{A}$  and add a large cost at the bottom of  $c$  to make sure the optimal solution of  $\min_{\mathbf{A}'^{\top}x' = b,x'\geq 0}c'^{\top}x'$  has  $x_k' = 0$  for all  $m < k \leq m + n$ , so that the first  $m$  coordinates of  $x'$  are an optimal solution for the original linear program.

After this modification, the matrix  $\mathbf{A}'$  is full rank and the IPM from Section 4 can be applied. However, the matrix  $\mathbf{A}'$  is no longer an incidence matrix, so we have to modify our data structures that assume  $\mathbf{A}$  is an incidence matrix.

Lemma 5.1: We can detect the large entries of  $\mathbf{A}^{\prime}h$  by using Lemma 5.1 for the first  $m$  entries and simply computing the bottom  $n$  entries of  $\mathbf{A}^{\prime}h$  explicitly. This additional cost of  $O(n)$  is subsumed by Lemma 5.1. We can handle the sampling in SAMPLE the same way by computing the impact of the bottom  $n$  entries of  $\mathbf{A}^{\prime}h$  to the norm by computing the bottom  $n$  entries explicitly. For the upper bound on the leverage scores and the leverage score sample, note that adding more rows to  $\mathbf{A}$  only decreases the leverage score of the original rows. Hence we can use the previous upper bound for the top  $m$  rows and simply use 1 as an upper bound on the leverage scores of the bottom  $n$  rows.

Laplacian Solvers: We use Laplacian solvers in several algorithms. While  $\mathbf{A}^{\prime \top}\mathbf{G}\mathbf{A}^{\prime}$  is no longer a Laplacian matrix, it is still an SDDM (symmetric diagonally dominant matrix) and solving systems in such matrices also has fast nearly linear time solvers.

Other data structures All other data structures do not require any special structure to the matrix  $\mathbf{A}'$  besides of very sparse rows. While results such as Theorem 6.1 state that  $\mathbf{A}$  must be an incidence matrix, this is only because it uses Lemma 5.1 internally, which has already been covered. Likewise, Lemma B.2 is obtained via a reduction from [BLSS20] to the data structure Lemma 5.1.

Initial Points Finding initial points is even easier now, as we no longer have to add a star to the bipartite graph. To construct a feasible  $x'$ , we again route  $1/n$  flow on each edge of the bipartite graph, and then set the bottom  $n$  coordinates of  $x'$  such that we satisfy  $\mathbf{A}'^{\top}x' = b$ . The slack of the dual is constructed in the same way as before in Lemma 8.15 and Lemma 8.7.
